**Acknowledgments**

This work is offered with deep gratitude to the countless generations who preserved the spiritual heritage of ancient India.

The *Bhagavad-Gītā* has endured not because it belongs to one religion, community, or historical period, but because it speaks to questions that have accompanied humanity for millennia: What is duty? What is freedom? What is the nature of the self/soul-spirit *ātma*? How should one live in a world marked by conflict, suffering, and impermanence? Through oral tradition, careful memorization, devoted recitation, and faithful manuscript transmission, this remarkable dialogue has reached the present age. For that enduring legacy, every student of Indian philosophy owes a profound debt of gratitude.

Equal gratitude is due to the lineage of Theravāda from Gautama the Supreme Arahant, through which the teachings of Gautama Buddha have likewise been preserved with extraordinary care. The Pāli Canon remains one of the oldest surviving collections of spiritual literature in the world, offering a direct window into the Buddha\'s teaching and providing the interpretive lens through which this study has been undertaken.

Although the *Bhagavad-Gītā* and the Pāli Canon ultimately present different paths of liberation, both arise from the same remarkable civilization that made the systematic exploration of consciousness, ethics, meditation, and liberation the highest human pursuit. They stand among humanity\'s greatest intellectual and spiritual achievements. This book is written in the hope that reading them together, respectfully and critically, will deepen appreciation for both traditions.

The author also expresses gratitude to the many scholars, translators, archaeologists, historians, Sanskritists, Pāli specialists, and monastic teachers whose lifelong efforts have made these ancient texts accessible to readers throughout the world. Every modern study rests upon foundations patiently laid by generations of dedicated researchers and practitioners.

Finally, this work is dedicated to all sincere seekers of truth. It is written not to diminish any religious tradition, but to encourage careful reading, thoughtful dialogue, and deeper contemplation. Genuine wisdom is never threatened by honest inquiry. On the contrary, it is through patient investigation, respectful comparison, and direct practice that truth reveals itself.

May this study contribute, however modestly, to greater understanding between the spiritual traditions of India, and may it encourage all who read it to cultivate wisdom, compassion, and the sincere aspiration for liberation.

**Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera**\
Center for Liberation Studies\

### Prologue: From the Renunciate Path to Karma-Yoga 

The first volume of *Bhagavad-Gītā: A Theravāda Buddhist Reading* began with a simple but fundamental question: **What does the Bhagavad-Gītā reveal when it is read through the liberation teaching of Gautama the Supreme Arahant, rather than through later sectarian assumptions about either Hinduism or Buddhism?**

Volume I established that the Bhagavad-Gītā cannot be understood adequately by isolating it from the ancient Indian civilization in which it arose. The Gītā belongs to the vast spiritual, philosophical, social, and cultural world of ancient Bharata---a world in which questions concerning karma, rebirth, dharma, meditation, renunciation, higher realms, the nature of the human being, and liberation were already deeply established. The Mahābhārata itself was not merely a literary composition but part of the cultural atmosphere through which generations of Indians understood themselves and the world. Gautama the Supreme Arahant emerged from this same civilization and inherited this ancient spiritual vocabulary.

**Chapter 1: The Historical and Social World of the Bhagavad-Gītā**

Chapter 1 therefore examined the historical background and social context of the Bhagavad-Gītā. The battlefield of Kurukṣetra is not an isolated philosophical stage. It represents an ancient society structured around family, kingship, social duty, lineage, warfare, sacrifice, Brahmanical authority, and the obligations of the various social classes.

The crisis of Arjuna is consequently much deeper than the personal fear of a warrior standing before battle. His crisis is the collision between two powerful demands: the demand of worldly duty and the desire to renounce worldly life. Arjuna sees his relatives, teachers, elders, and friends standing on the opposite side of the battlefield. He begins to question whether victory, kingdom, pleasure, and even life itself have any value when they must be purchased through the destruction of those to whom he is bound by family and social obligation. This historical and social setting becomes essential for understanding everything that follows.

The Gītā is speaking to a human being who is deeply embedded in society. Arjuna cannot simply abandon his position, his family, his kingdom, and his social obligations without consequences. The problem that emerges throughout the Gītā is therefore not simply, **How can a human being become liberated?** It is also, **How can a human being pursue spiritual freedom while continuing to participate in the world?** This tension becomes central moving from Chapter 2 into Chapter 3.

**Chapter 2: The Renunciate Path and the Forgotten Significance of Ātmā**

Chapter 2 is, in many respects, the most remarkable chapter of the Gītā for a student of Gautama\'s liberation teaching of renunciants.

When read carefully, Chapter 2 presents an extraordinary concentration of terminology and concepts associated with the ancient Indian renunciate path: Ātmā, Brahma-nirvāṇa, Brahmacariya, bhikkhu/bhikṣu, paññā/prajñā, samādhi, rāga, dosa/dveṣa, moha, the worldly shore, the other shore, renunciation, restraint of the senses, freedom from craving, and liberation from the cycle of birth and death.

More importantly, Chapter 2 describes a path that is remarkably close to, if not the exactly summary from a 200 BC's view, the renunciate path preserved in the Bhikkhu and Bhikkhuni lineages of Gautama the Supreme Arahant. This is not merely a path of intellectual knowledge. It is not merely a philosophical yoga for contemplative thinkers. It is a path of **renunciation**.

The practitioner turns away from the worldly objects of attraction, abandons rāga, dosa, and moha, disciplines the senses, cultivates samādhi and paññā, develops disenchantment with the world, allows worldly passion to fade away, and ultimately seeks complete liberation from worldly existence. As Volume I argued, this is fundamentally a path of total renunciation of worldly life and activities for complete liberation from the world.

The sequence is therefore unmistakable: **Nibbidā → Virāga → Vimutti.** Disenchantment with the world leads to fading away of worldly passion, which leads to complete liberation from worldly existence. The ultimate problem is not simply suffering inside the world. The problem is continued involuntary existence within the world---continued birth, aging, death, and rebirth. The goal is therefore not to become more comfortable within saṃsāra, nor merely to ascend to a higher cosmic state, but to overcome the entire cycle of worldly existence and reach the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

And yet one striking feature of the Gītā must be emphasized. **The doctrine of anattā is absent as a teaching from this presentation.**

The Gītā does not present *anattā* as a meditation subject, a practice, a method of liberation, or the central philosophical discovery by which the practitioner is instructed to deny the existence of Ātmā. Instead, the language repeatedly points toward Ātmā as the internal spiritual principle of the human being. Chapter 2 even speaks directly in the language of Ātmā, while the renunciate path is presented as a movement away from the external world toward the transcendent shore.

This absence is significant.

If the doctrine in today's mainstream academia commonly presented as the defining teaching of Gautama Buddha---**"there is no self," "there is no soul," and liberation comes through realizing anattā**---were truly the central engine of Gautama\'s original renunciate path, one would expect this teaching to appear naturally within a text that preserves such a detailed summary of the ancient Indian path of renunciation.

But it does not.

What we instead find is a vocabulary of liberation built around **renunciation, Brahmacariya, samādhi, paññā, purification, abandonment of rāga, dosa, and moha, nibbidā, virāga, and vimutti**.

This raises a profound question that Volume I deliberately places before the reader: **Has the later Buddhist emphasis on anattā obscured the actual structure of Gautama\'s original liberation teaching?**

The argument of this work is that it has.

Modern Buddhist interpretation has elevated anattā into a dominant doctrinal principle and, in doing so, has frequently displaced the actual practical structure of Gautama\'s path. Volume I therefore returns attention to what the earliest renunciants actually practiced: the purification of the human being, the overcoming of worldly bondage, the destruction of rāga, dosa, and moha, and the movement beyond the world toward Nibbāna.

Gautama\'s teaching was not designed to make the practitioner intellectually comfortable with worldly existence. It was designed to **escape it**. The ancient path is a path toward the Deathless.

**The Historical Significance of Chapter 2**

The remarkable similarity between Chapter 2 and the renunciate path of Gautama also raises a historical question.

The Bhagavad-Gītā was incorporated into the larger Mahābhārata literature during a period in which Gautama\'s renunciate movement had already become a major spiritual force in India. The Gītā\'s Chapter 2 can therefore be read as preserving, a summary, in another Sanskrit tradition, a substantial body of the ancient renunciate vocabulary and methodology that had become associated with the liberation movement of the śramaṇas.

This is why Chapter 2 is so important.

It allows us to see that the renunciate path was not an invention of a later Buddhist scholastic tradition. The spiritual vocabulary was already part of the ancient Indian world. Gautama did not appear in an intellectual vacuum. He inherited an ancient path, rediscovered its deepest dimensions, purified it, systematized it, and led his bhikkhu and bhikkhuni disciples toward complete liberation from the world to attain the deathless, permanent and joyful existence in another realm names Nibbana. Volume I therefore emphasized Gautama as the restorer of the **purāṇa-magga**, the ancient path, rather than as a philosopher who invented an entirely new spiritual system.

Chapter 2 consequently stands at the threshold of the entire argument of this book. It shows us the possibility of complete renunciation.

But Chapter 3 asks a different question: **What happens when human beings are not prepared to renounce the world?**

**Chapter 3: Karma-Yoga and the Necessity of Remaining in the World**

Chapter 3 introduces the great counter-movement. Arjuna has just heard a teaching that points toward renunciation, spiritual discernment, restraint, samādhi, and liberation. Naturally, he asks why he is then being commanded to perform terrible worldly action. The Gītā\'s answer is Karma-Yoga.

Human beings have social responsibilities. They have families, kingdoms, occupations, duties, relationships, and obligations. They cannot all simply abandon society, take up the alms bowl, enter the forest, and devote themselves entirely to meditation and liberation. This is the social problem that Karma-Yoga attempts to solve.

The Gītā therefore teaches Arjuna to continue acting while abandoning attachment to the fruits of action. One should perform one\'s obligatory duty without becoming attached to the results. Action itself need not become a source of bondage if the practitioner acts without personal attachment.

This is the fundamental innovation of Chapter 3. Instead of saying, **leave the world**, it says, in effect: **Remain in the world, perform your duty, but do not become attached to what you do.**

This allows the individual to participate in society while attempting to maintain an inward freedom from the results of action. The tension between the two paths is therefore unmistakable. Chapter 2 presents the possibility of complete renunciation and departure from worldly existence. Chapter 3 presents a way for the householder, warrior, ruler, and socially obligated person to continue living and acting within the world while attempting to free himself from karmic attachment.

The Gītā thus creates a spiritual accommodation between liberation and worldly responsibility. The practitioner does not necessarily have to abandon action. He can transform action into yoga. He can perform his duty. He can sacrifice the fruits. He can remain inwardly unattached. And through this process, action itself becomes a means of purification. This is the great subject that Volume II will examine more deeply.

**Chapter 4: The Fruits of Karma-Yoga and the Question of the Final Destination**

Chapter 4 develops the consequences of this teaching. Karma-Yoga is joined with jñāna, renunciation, sacrifice, devotion, and the divine identity of Kṛṣṇa. Action performed without attachment becomes karmically neutral, while knowledge destroys ignorance and enables the practitioner to transcend ordinary bondage.

But here the deepest problem emerges. **Where does this path ultimately lead?** The Gītā\'s answer moves toward higher cosmic existence, divine participation, celestial realms, and ultimately union with or re-absorption into the divine source.

This is where the distinction between the Gītā and Gautama\'s liberation teaching becomes fundamental. There is a profound difference between **ascending within the universe** and **exiting the universe**. There is a profound difference between **union with the cosmic source** and **entry into Nibbāna-Dhātu**. There is a profound difference between **non-duality** and Gautama\'s path of liberation through the destruction of rāga, dosa, and moha.

The so-called transcendence of duality described in the Gītā does not produce the same destination as Gautama\'s liberation. In the Gītā, the practitioner can remain active within the material field while realizing an unconditioned divine core beyond the changing guṇas. The world becomes something to transcend inwardly while continuing to participate in it.

But this is not the liberation taught by Gautama the Supreme Arahant. Gautama\'s liberation is not the realization that the individual is ultimately one with the universe. It is not dissolution into the universal source. It is not absorption into the cosmic engine. It is not merely elevation into a higher heaven. It is **exit**.

The purified Citta leaves behind the entire worldly structure of loka and passes beyond the universe into the deathless realm of Nibbāna-Dhātu. Volume I therefore distinguished sharply between divine re-absorption and true renunciation: the former remains connected to the cosmic structure, while the latter culminates in complete transcendence of it. This distinction is the key to reading the Bhagavad-Gītā through Gautama\'s Dhamma.

**From Volume I to Volume II**

Volume I therefore established four fundamental movements.

**Chapter 1** placed the Bhagavad-Gītā within its historical, civilizational, and social environment.

**Chapter 2** revealed the extraordinary presence of the ancient renunciate path: Ātmā, Brahmacariya, samādhi, paññā, purification, abandonment of rāga, dosa, and moha, nibbidā, virāga, vimutti, and the aspiration toward the deathless.

**Chapter 3** introduced Karma-Yoga as the solution to the social problem created by radical renunciation: human beings have worldly duties, and they must continue to act, but they can attempt to free themselves from attachment to the fruits of their actions.

**Chapter 4** developed the fruits of this path into a larger theological and cosmological system involving divine knowledge, higher realms, non-duality, divine participation, and ultimately union with the cosmic source.

The crucial question is therefore no longer simply whether the Bhagavad-Gītā contains elements of the ancient Indian liberation path. It clearly does. The more important question is: **What happened to that renunciate path when it was placed alongside the demands of worldly duty, social order, divine sovereignty, and cosmic union?**

And beyond that lies an even more fundamental question: **Does liberation mean leaving the world, or does it mean remaining within the world without attachment?**

These are not two descriptions of the same liberation. They are two fundamentally different answers to the human problem. Gautama\'s path is a path of **world-renunciation**. Karma-Yoga is a path of **worldly engagement without attachment**. One seeks to conquer saṃsāra and exit the universe. The other seeks freedom while continuing to participate within the cosmic order. One culminates in Nibbāna-Dhātu, the Deathless realm. The other ultimately directs consciousness toward divine and cosmic realities.

Volume II begins from this threshold.

Having established the historical setting, recovered the renunciate vocabulary, examined the conflict between renunciation and social duty, and exposed the fundamental difference between Nibbāna and cosmic re-absorption, we can now proceed further into the Bhagavad-Gītā and examine what its teachings reveal about **Ātmā, karma, renunciation, knowledge, devotion, divine reality, cosmic existence, and liberation**.

The purpose is not to force the Gītā to become Buddhism. Nor is it to force Gautama\'s Dhamma to conform to the Gītā. The purpose is much more direct: **to read the Sanskrit carefully, to recover the ancient spiritual vocabulary shared by these traditions, and then to distinguish precisely where their paths converge---and where they finally separate.**

Only by making this distinction can we understand what the Bhagavad-Gītā is actually teaching, what Gautama the Supreme Arahant actually taught, and why the difference between **remaining within the universe** and **exiting the universe into the Deathless** is the decisive question of liberation.

### Chapter 5 Karma Sannyāsa Yoga: The Yoga on Renunciation in Egoless Actions

***Arjuna uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   saṁnyāsaṁ karmaṇāṁ kṛṣṇa, punar yogaṁ ca śaṁsasi;**

**     yac chreya etayor ekaṁ, tan me brūhi su-niścitam.**

Renunciation/complete giving up (saṁnyāsaṁ) of actions (karmaṇāṁ), O Kṛṣṇa (kṛṣṇa), and again (punar) you praise (śaṁsasi) \[karma\] yoga/ path of selfless/egoless action (yogaṁ ca); which (yat) one (ekaṁ) of the two (etayoḥ) is the better (śreyas), that (tat) tell (brūhi) me (me) definitively / conclusively (su-niścitam).

**Commentary: Two Primary Spiritual Paths**

This verse captures Arjuna\'s existential dilemma at the boundary between two primary spiritual paths: the path of complete renunciation of action (*saṁnyāsa*) \[that Gautama the Supreme Arahant proclaims for the renunciates to exit the universe to enter into the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*\] versus the path of dedicated, detached engagement in action (*karma-yoga*) \[that many other non-renunciate religious or spiritual lineages promote including the Bodhisattva path\]. Arjuna senses a contradiction in Kṛṣṇa\'s teachings---asking whether true spiritual progress requires complete physical withdrawal or selfless/egoless execution of one\'s duties.

While the Bhagavad Gītā presents these as complementary modes of practice within the Vedic framework, we can examine how the themes of action, renunciation, and liberation correlate with the early Theravāda Pali framework:

- **Renunciation (*Pabbajjā* / *Nekkhamma*/ *Saṁnyāsa*):** In the Theravāda tradition, leaving behind worldly duties---entering the life of a *bhikkhu*---is a key step to practice the higher *Brahmacariya*. True renunciation, however, is not merely physical non-action, but the complete eradication of *rāga* (worldly craving/passion and attachment), *dosa* (fear, anger, hatred/ill-will), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion).

- **Action and Intent (*Karma* / *Kamma*):** In the early Pali context, *kamma* is defined as intention (*cetanā*). Physical stillness without mental purity does not lead to freedom. It is through the complete cessation of worldly craving and clinging, the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, that we can escape our involuntary reincarnation in the world and gain true freedom.

- **The Ancient Path to Liberation:** The ancient path to the deathless realm of Nibbāna requires the systematic cultivation of *Sīla* (renunciation conduct training), *Samādhi* (Concentration), and *Paññā* (Wisdom). The practice of the *Brahmacariya* serves as an essential foundation. Joining the divine Brahma realms acts as a stepping stone toward final liberation.

- **The Pure Abode Brahma heavens as the Gateway:** The exit point from saṃsāra to Nibbāna-Dhātu is specifically through the Pure Abode Brahma heavens in the Rupa realm. Upon the dissolution of the final physical body of an Arahant (a fully purified being), the purified mind (*Citta*) of the Arahant enters the Pure Abode Brahma heavens, and liberation is realized through the exit of this world to the outside of the universe to go to the Nibbāna-dhātu, the deathless realm of Nibbāna.

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[2](javascript:void(0))   saṁnyāsaḥ karma-yogaś ca, niḥśreyasa-karāv ubhau;**

**     tayos tu karma-saṁnyāsāt, karma-yogo viśiṣyate.**

Renunciation (saṁnyāsaḥ) and (ca) yoga of action (karma-yogaḥ), both (ubhau) lead to the supreme good (niḥśreyasa-karau); but (tu) between these two (tayoḥ), yoga of action (karma-yogo) is superior (viśiṣyate) than renunciation of action (karma-saṁnyāsāt).

**Commentary**: In this response to Arjuna\'s query, Kṛṣṇa resolves the apparent contradiction between active engagement in the world and complete renunciation from all worldly activities, with a clear, definitive and conclusive answer as requested.

He declares that while both paths lead toward the ultimate good (*niḥśreyasa*), *karma-yoga* (selfless action) is practically superior to renunciation of action (*karma-saṁnyāsa*), because true renunciation is an internal transformation of mind rather than a cessation of physical actions.

**niḥśreyasa-karau** (*adjective, nominative dual, masculine*): Leading to the highest welfare (*niḥśreyasa* = ultimate welfare /liberation from suffering; *karau* = causing/bringing).

**niḥśreyasa**:

- **niḥ-** (निः / निर् *nir-*): A directional prefix meaning \"out of,\" \"beyond,\" \"away from,\" \"completely,\" or \"without.\" It denotes transcendence or complete removal from a given state or domain.

- **śreyasa** (श्रेयस / श्रेयस् *śreyas*): A comparative adjective/noun derived from the root *śrī* (श्री - beauty, prosperity, auspiciousness, excellence). *Śreyas* literally means \"better,\" \"superior,\" \"higher good,\" or \"ultimate welfare\" (often contrasted in Vedic and Upaniṣadic literature with *preyas*, which refers to immediate, pleasant, or worldly gratification).

Literally, **niḥ-śreyas** translates to **\"that than which there is no better\"** or **\"having nothing superior above it.\"** It signifies the absolute ultimate, top-most, unexcelled good beyond which no further progression or higher state exists. While translations frequently interchange \"ultimate bliss,\" \"liberation from suffering,\" or a general "liberation," they may refer to distinct cosmological and ontological realities:

**(1). \"Ultimate Bliss\" (Experiential / Phenomenal State)**

- **Domain:** Associated with feeling, experience, or elevated existence within worldly or heavenly realms (*loka*).

- **Characteristics:** \"Bliss\" (*sukha* or *ānanda*) is an experiential state. Including the highest refined mental bliss experienced in lofty heavenly states or deep meditative attainments.

- **Limitation:** It may belong to the world or belong to out-of-world deathless state, it does not distinguish, when it is worldly, then it is subject to change, cessation, and *dukkha*.

**(2). Liberation from what, from the world or from sufferings (Transcendent Release / Transcendence vs. Worldly Blissful state)**

- **Worldly Phenomena and Transcendent Release:** All physical phenomena and mental phenomena in this world---including heavenly realms and refined states of meditative bliss---are worldly; they belong to the world (*loka*), and the related laws of death, therefore they rise and fall, *dukkha*.

**Commentary: The Rhetorical Substitution in Kṛṣṇa's Response**

A critical, rigorous examination of Bhagavad Gītā 5.2 reveals a profound structural shift in how the problem of spiritual practice is framed. When Arjuna asks for a definitive declaration regarding which path is superior---the path of renunciation (saṁnyāsa) or the path of action (karma-yoga)---he is asking a fundamental question about the ultimate destination and efficacy of these two distinct orientations.

However, Kṛṣṇa's reply subtly redefines the terms of the debate, bypassing the radical, absolute distinction between world-transcending liberation and worldly activity, and substituting instead a comparison between two methods of internal mental management.

*Arjuna\'s True Dilemma: World-Transcending Renunciation (Exit) vs. Action/Karma Yoga (Ascension/Duty)*

*Kṛṣṇa\'s / Gita Author's Rhetorical Shift: Physical Non-Action vs. Action with Mental Detachment*

*Kṛṣṇa\'s / Gita Author's Verdict: Karma-Yoga is \"Superior\"*

**1). Bypassing the True Cosmological and Ontological Distinction**

The true, unyielding comparison between these two paths lies in their ultimate cosmological trajectories:

- **The Path of True Renunciation (*Saṁnyāsa*):** Directed at total transcendence---completely exiting the cosmic structure of rebirth, overcoming aging, decay, and death, and transcending all physical and mental phenomena belonging to the world (*loka*).

- **The Path of Action (*Karma-Yoga*):** Directed at maintaining cosmic/social order (*lokasaṅgraha*), purifying internal motives, and attaining elevated, blissful existence or heavenly ascension within the bounds of the cosmos.

Instead of addressing this fundamental contrast---between total exit from *saṃsāra* versus engagement within it---Kṛṣṇa bypasses the absolute outcome of world-transcending liberation. He merges both trajectories under the ambiguous, blanket term ***niḥśreyasa*** (\"the highest good\" / \"ultimate welfare\"), treating them as if they are merely two parallel tracks leading to the exact same destination.

**2). Redefining *Saṁnyāsa* to Secure the Argument**

By conflating the ultimate endpoints under *niḥśreyasa*, Kṛṣṇa shifts the focus away from the *destination* and onto the *method*. To make *karma-yoga* appear practically superior, he redefines *saṁnyāsa* in its weakest, most superficial form: **mere physical cessation of activity**.

- He frames *saṁnyāsa* not as the radical, inward eradication of worldly binding ties and total transcendence of worldly phenomena, but as the mere external dropping of physical work.

- Having reduced renunciation to simple physical inactivity, he easily points out its flaw: physical non-action without mental detachment is ineffective and unstable.

- He then presents *karma-yoga* as superior by arguing that true renunciation is an internal transformation of the mind rather than a cessation of physical actions.

By setting up a false choice between *faulty physical non-action* and *disciplined internal detachment during action*, the choice becomes rigged. The radical, world-transcending trajectory of total renunciation---which aims at complete detachment from all worldly phenomena---is effectively obscured.

**\**

**3). The Functional Consequence of the Rhetorical Shift**

This rhetorical maneuver serves a specific pragmatic purpose within the narrative context of the Mahābhārata: **it keeps Arjuna engaged on the battlefield.**

If the absolute distinction between total world-transcendence and worldly duty were kept in sharp focus, the path of complete renunciation would remain the logical imperative for one seeking ultimate release from aging and death. By obscuring this contrast and redefining renunciation as a purely internal state of mind that can be fully realized *while fighting a war*, Kṛṣṇa redirects Arjuna away from physical renunciation and back into the execution of worldly *karma*.

While this argument successfully motivates social and military duty, it avoids the core ontological reality: all physical and mental actions (*karma*) in this world will inevitably defile the mind. Only through rigorous and radical repentance is there a chance to purify such defilement. Assuming that such worldly actions---including battlefield killing---are acceptable, and attempting to self-justify them as \"righteous anger\" or \"righteous violence,\" will nullify any acts of purification, such as repentance, and leave the mind-heart (*citta*) impure. Only the purified *citta* can become disenchanted with the world and achieve complete transcendence.

**[3](javascript:void(0))   jñeyaḥ sa nitya-saṁnyāsī, yo na dveṣṭi na kāṅkṣati;**

**     nirdvando hi mahā-bāho, sukhaṁ bandhāt pramucyate.**

He (sa) should be known (jñeyaḥ) as a perpetual renunciate (nitya-saṁnyāsī), who (yo) neither (na) hates (dveṣṭi) nor (na) desires (kāṅkṣati); for (hi) being free from dualities (nirdvandaḥ), O mighty-armed one (mahā-bāho), easily (sukhaṁ) he is completely released (pramucyate) from bondage (bandhāt).

**Commentary: Non-Dual Cosmic Merging vs. Noble Duality for World-Transcending Exit**

A critical comparison between Kṛṣṇa's teaching in this verse and Gautama's *Arahanism* reveals a fundamental cosmological divide. While Kṛṣṇa proposes that freedom is achieved by transcending dualities (*nirdvanda*) and cultivating a non-dual state of mind while performing worldly duties, Gautama's path of true liberation (*Vimutti*) is built upon a sharp, uncompromising **noble duality** designed for complete exit from the world.

**1). Kṛṣṇa's Non-Dualism: World-Sustaining Absorption and Dissolution**

Kṛṣṇa instructs Arjuna that one who neither hates (*dveṣṭi*) nor desires (*kāṅkṣati*) is free from dualities and thus \"easily\" released from bondage while remaining active in worldly warfare. In this framework:

- **Non-Duality as a Structural Tool:** Dualities such as pleasure/pain, gain/loss, and wholesome/unwholesome are collapsed into a non-dual cosmic essence. By viewing all conflicts as mere manifestations of the natural cosmic essence (*prakṛti*), the actor neutralizes internal moral friction.

- **The Destination:** Because the non-dual approach aims to sustain the cosmic order (*lokasaṅgraha*) rather than escape it, it keeps the actor anchored within the machinery of the cosmos. Since the universe operates on non-dual essence with its manifest of cycles of creation, decay, and dissolution, adhering to non-dual transcendence leads eventually to the soul-spirit (*ātmā*) dissolving back into the cosmic source---subsumed under the universal law of aging and death (*jarāmaraṇa*).

**2). Gautama's Noble Duality: The Engine of Pure Exit**

In stark contrast, Gautama's teaching in *Arahanism* asserts that non-duality within worldly action is a cosmic trap that binds beings to the natural cosmic order. True liberation is not a psychological adjustment to tolerate aging and death, but an **ontological emancipation from the universe itself**.

**Two Radically Different Liberations**

The distinction between today's mainstream academia's liberation teaching and Gautama's *Vimutti* liberation (**Arahanism**) highlights a fundamental divide: **psychological liberation within the universe versus ontological exit from the universe.**

**(1). Ancient Hinduism/Vedanta and Today's Mainstream Academia's Liberation: Freedom from Internal Bondage**

This version of liberation refers to a state of **internal, psychological freedom**:

- **The Problem:** Bondage is caused by internal defilements---compulsory desires for the worldly, cravings and attachment (*kāma*), fear (*bhaya*), anger (*krodha*), and egoic attāchment (*saṅga*).

- **The Scope:** It does not seek to escape the physical laws of the universe. The universe operates under the law of decay and dissolution, as long as anyone, any soul-spirit stays in the universe, it will eventually result in dissolution into the source of the universe. The non-dual awakening (wrong awakening according to Gautama's true Dhamma) leads to transcendence & merging with the source of universe, which differs from Gautama's true dhamma of Arahanism with noble duality for liberation from the world, and the universe. The distinction between \"transcendence of duality\" (dvandvātīta) and Gautama's uncompromising ethical dualism (Arahanism) exposes a fundamental cosmological divide between world-sustaining absorption and world-transcending exit.

- **The Posture:** The liberated individual remains fully active within the cosmic structure. By anchoring their mind in non-dual transcendence knowledge (*jñāna*), they transcend internal duality and friction. The universal law of aging and death still apply for their goal remains inside the universe, but the inner actor is unaffected by worldly dualities of heat and cold, pleasure and pain, praise and blame, wholesome and unwholesome, yin and yang, while sustaining the worldly order (*lokasaṅgraha*).

**\**

**(2). Gautama's *Liberation*: Freedom from the Laws of worldly existence (*Jārāmaraṇa*)**

Gautama's motive to leave his life as a prince and completely renounce the world (*pabbajjā*) was not merely to find mental peace or overcome internal anxiety---it was driven by the direct, disturbing realization of **Old Age (*jarā*) and Death (*maraṇa*)**:

- **The Problem:** The entire fabric of the universe (the *loka*) is governed by the law of impermanence, conditionality, and dissolution (*dukkha*). As long as one remains within the cosmic realms of existence, one is subject to compulsory cycles of aging and death.

- **The Realization:** True safety cannot be achieved by merely changing one\'s mental attitude while remaining inside a realm where everything is governed by the universal law of decay and death. Aging and death are non-negotiable structural laws of the universe.

- **The Solution:** To overcome aging and death, one must **transcend and exit the universe itself**. Gautama's liberation is an absolute severance from the entire universal process of *saṃsāra*---the ending of involuntary reincarnation within the universe through the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*.

Kṛṣṇa and mainstream academia's liberation is psychological mastery over the mind within the world: it allows the individual to stand untouched by fear and desire while fulfilling their role inside the cosmic machinery.

Gautama's liberation is ontological emancipation from the world: recognizing that the universe is an inescapable trap of decay and death, his path requires the practitioner---as a renunciate (bhikkhu or bhikkhunī)---to completely purify the citta, means to detach from the world. Upon physical dissolution, the purified citta passes through the Pure Abodes Brahma heavens as the gateway threshold, completing its final, irreversible exit from this universe to Nibbāna-Dhātu---the realm entirely beyond aging and death with permanent existence and bliss.

**[4](javascript:void(0))   sāṅkhya-yogau pṛthag bālāḥ, pravadanti na paṇḍitāḥ;**

**     ekam apy āsthitaḥ samyag, ubhayor vindate phalam.**

The childish/immature (bālāḥ) speak of analytical knowledge (sāṅkhya) and karma-yoga \[yoga of action\] (yogau) as separate thing (pṛthag), declare (pravadanti) not (na) the wise/learned (paṇḍitāḥ); firmly and properly (samyag) established (āsthitaḥ) in indeed (api) one (ekam) of both (ubhayoḥ), one obtains (vindate) the fruit (phalam).

**Commentary**: In this verse, Kṛṣṇa dismisses the distinction between *Sāṅkhya* (intellectual/analytical discernment and knowledge) and *Karma-Yoga* (action with internal detachment), calling those who view them as fundamentally separate \"childish\" or spiritually immature (*bālāḥ*). He asserts that a wise person (*paṇḍita*) recognizes that thoroughly applying oneself to either path yields the exact same fruit (*phalam*).

**[5](javascript:void(0))   yat sāṅkhyaiḥ prāpyate sthānaṁ, tad yogair api gamyate;**

**     ekaṁ sāṅkhyaṁ ca yogaṁ ca, yaḥ paśyati sa paśyati.**

Which (yat) state/abode (sthānaṁ) is reached (prāpyate) by the analytical renunciates (sāṅkhyaiḥ), that (tat) by the practitioners of karma-yogis (yogair) also (api) is attained (gamyate); as one (ekaṁ) analytical knowledge (sāṅkhyaṁ ca) and action/yoga (yogaṁ ca), whoever (yaḥ) sees (paśyati), he (sa) truly sees (paśyati).

**Commentary**: In this verse, Kṛṣṇa advances his argument by declaring that the destination (*sthāna*) attained by practitioners of analytical knowledge and renunciation (*sāṅkhya*) is identical to the destination reached by those who engage in action through *karma-yoga*. He explicitly praises as truly wise (*yaḥ paśyati sa paśyati*) anyone who sees these two fundamentally different paths as one (*ekaṁ*).

This verse represents the culmination of the rhetorical strategy begun in 5.2. Having redefined renunciation as internal mental detachment (5.3) and declared that distinguishing between knowledge and action is childish (5.4), Kṛṣṇa now asserts a total equivalence of their ultimate endpoints (*sthāna*), and a total equivalence of destination (*sthāna*) for two fundamentally opposed spiritual paradigms.

**Opposing Methodologies and Divergent Destinations**

A critical examination of the underlying mechanics of these two paths reveals that they operate on contradictory principles, making an identical outcome logically impossible:

**The Analytical Renunciates (*Sāṅkhyaiḥ*): Dualistic Discernment for Total Exit**

The true analytical renunciate relies on sharp, dualistic intellectual/ analytical discernment. By strictly distinguishing between the wholesome (*kusala*) and unwholesome (*akusala*), and separating the eternal from worldly phenomena, the renunciate systematically purifies the mind-heart (*citta*) or soul-spirit (*ātmā*). This dualistic discernment allows them to detach from all physical and mental phenomena belonging to the world, labelling them as unwholesome, breaking the chain of cause and effect to achieve a complete, ontological exit from the universe.

**The Karma-Yogis: Non-Dualistic Action Leading to Worldly Entrapment**

In contrast, *karma-yogis* adopt a non-dualistic posture toward worldly dualities. By treating pleasure and pain, gain and loss, wholesome and unwholesome as equal---neither hating (*na dveṣṭi*) nor desiring (*na kāṅkṣati*)---they collapse moral and operational friction while continuing to act within the world. Because this non-dual framework functions inside the cosmic field, it leaves the actor completely trapped within the universe. Instead of escaping, the soul-spirit (*ātmā*) remains bound to the laws of decay and death (*jarāmaraṇa*), ultimately dissolving back into the cosmic source of the universe.

**Kṛṣṇa's Fallacious Equivalence**

How can two paths operating on opposite methodologies---one using dualistic discernment to exit the universe, the other using non-dual absorption that traps one within it---yield the same result? Claiming that both reach the exact same *sthāna* conflates two entirely different destinations: **world-transcending liberation** versus **cosmic entrapment and dissolution**.

**[6](javascript:void(0))   saṁnyāsas tu mahā-bāho, duḥkham āptum ayogataḥ;**

**     yoga-yukto munir brahma, na cireṇādhigacchati.**

Renunciation (saṁnyāsaḥ), however (tu), O mighty-armed one (mahā-bāho), is difficult (duḥkham) to attain (āptum) without yoga (ayogataḥ); *munis* disciplined/engaged in yoga (yoga-yuktaḥ) to *Brahma*, without delay / swiftly (na cireṇa) attains (adhigacchati).

Commentary: The structural misdirection in the verse is its equating *karma-yoga* to *yoga* while reductively labeling the renunciate path as mere intellectualism. True renunciates (*sannyāsins*) and *munis* are not passive theorists; they are the authentic *yogis* engaged in the complete discipline of mind purification and world-transcendence.

1.  **The Fallacy of the Mislabeling**

Conflating *karma-yoga* with *yoga* while reducing renunciation (*sannyāsa*) to mere analytical knowledge dishonors rigorous debate. By switching concepts and mislabeling terms, this framing misdirects seekers. Renunciation is not idle speculation; it is an active, rigorous path of spiritual labor/action designed to attain complete liberation from the world (*saṃsāra*).

2.  **The True Nature of the Renunciate (*Muni*)**

The renunciates are the true *yogis*. While they renounce all worldly activities, they actively engage in every practice required to purify the *citta* (mind). Their path includes:

**Practice of Jhāna and Samādhi:** Entering deep *Samādhi* to induce *tapas* (primordial spiritual force) to purify the *ātma*.

**The Threefold Training:** Establishing *Sīla* as restraint and discipline, practicing *Brahmacariya*, cultivating *Samādhi*, and studying analytical knowledge for *Paññā* (wisdom).

3.  **Cultivation of the Brahmavihāras and Union with Brahma**

Far from passive detachment, the renunciate rigorously practices the four *Brahmavihāras*: **Mettā:** Kindness, **Karuṇā:** Compassion, **Muditā:** Brahmic joy, **Upekkhā:** Transcendental release.

By steadfastly fulfilling *Brahmacariya* and the *Brahmavihāras*, the renunciate establishes true union (*yoga*) with Brahma---the source of the cosmic mankind race and the original source of liberation wisdom.

**[7](javascript:void(0))   yoga-yukto viśuddhātmā, vijitātmā jitendriyaḥ;**

**     sarva-bhūtātma bhūtātmā, kurvann api na lipyate.**

One who is yoked/united to yoga (yoga-yukto), whose ātma is purified (viśuddha-ātmā), whose ātmā is thoroughly mastered/conquered (vijita-ātmā), whose sense faculties are restrained/conquered (jita-indriyaḥ), whose ātmā manifest \[individual\] \[connects with\] the manifest of all living beings' ātmā (sarva-bhūta-ātma bhūta-ātmā), even while performing activities (kurvann api), is not tainted/ defiled (na lipyate).

**Commentary**: The interpretation of the compound **sarva-bhūta-ātma-bhūta-ātmā** highlights the fundamental divergence between a **non-dualistic (monistic) cosmological view** and the **dualistic, world-transcending framework**.

**1). The Non-Dualist (Advaitic) View: Essential Identity of All Beings**

From a non-dualistic perspective, the phrase *sarva-bhūta-ātma-bhūta-ātmā* is interpreted as the realization of essential sameness across all life:

- **Universal Source Identity:** Every individual *ātma*---whether embodied as a human, an ant, a snake, or any insectoid/animaloid form---originates from the same singular source of the universe.

- **Difference Only in Manifestation:** In this view, the individual living beings differ solely in their external, physical, and mental manifestations. The underlying essence (*ātma* / soul-spirit) remains identical across every creature.

- **Non-Dual Connection:** Realizing that one's own *ātma* is nondifferent from the *ātma* of all living beings establishes an absolute, non-dual unity. The boundaries between self and other collapse because all beings are seen as fractal expressions or sparks of the exact same ultimate cosmic reality.

**2). The Dualistic World-Transcending Framework: The Special Nature of Mankind**

The dualistic framework rejects the non-dual assumption that all manifestations are equal or that liberation is merely realizing essential non-difference within the universe. Instead, it establishes that the human *ātma* (descended and hybridized from the cosmic mankind race) occupies a unique and special position capable of true liberation and exit from the world/universe.

This distinction rests upon **The Dual Composition of the Attā / Ātmā**:

**A. The Structural Blueprint: The Brahmā (Father / Morphogenetic Field)**

- **Brahmā as the Source of Mankind:** Brahmā is the high celestial progenitor and Father of the cosmic mankind race.

- **The Morphogenetic Field:** Brahmā imparts the specific morphogenetic information field---the structural blueprint, form, higher intellectual capacity, and lineage---that defines the cosmic mankind race within the universe and the human *ātma* inherited it and is part of the family.

- **The Unique Potential:** Unlike lower animal, insectoid, or elementary manifestations whose morphogenetic blueprints trap them in automatic worldly loops, the human blueprint carries the ancestral lineage, structural capacity, and liberation wisdom (*paññā*) necessary to break free from the trap of the universe.

**B. The Underlying Essence: The Source of the Universe (Mother / Content)**

- **The Fractal Consciousness:** While the structural blueprint comes from Brahmā, the content filling that field---the consciousness---is a fractal derived directly from the source of the universe, acting as a cosmic Mother.

- **The Trap vs. The Exit:** In the non-dual view, this shared essence is mistaken for the ultimate goal. In the dualistic, world-transcending framework, the universe itself is governed by the universal law of decay and death, and thus *dukkha*. Merely recognizing essential sameness keeps one entangled inside the cosmic *saṃsāric* system.

While the **non-dual view** interprets *sarva-bhūta-ātma-bhūta-ātmā* as a declaration that every ant, insect, and human shares an identical, undifferentiated essence from the universe, the **dualistic framework** demonstrates why this non-dual view fails to achieve liberation:

I.  Realizing oneness with the universe merely binds the seeker to the universe\'s internal mechanics (*saṃsāra*).

II. The **human *ātma*** is not merely another equal manifestation; it possesses the unique morphogenetic blueprint from **Brahmā**, granting it the capacity for *Brahmacariya*, deep *Samādhi*, and the induction of *tapas* (primordial force).

III. The true renunciate (*muni, samana, bhikkhu/bhikkhuni*) uses this unique human capacity to cultivate the *Brahmavihāras*, purify the *citta*, and achieve final, dualistic separation---exiting the universe into their true home and destiny of **Nibbāna-Dhātu**.

**[8](javascript:void(0))   naiva kiñcit karomīti, yukto manyeta tattva-vit;**

**paśyañ śṛṇvan spṛśañ jighrann, aśnan gacchan svapañ śvasan.**

\"I do not do (*karomi*) anything (*kiñcit*) at all (*naiva*)\"---thus (*iti*), one who is yoked to \[karma-yoga\] (*yukto*), should know and reflect (*manyeta*), as a knower of thatness (absolute reality) (*tattva-vit*); even while seeing (*paśyan*), hearing (*śṛṇvan*), touching (*spṛśan*), and smelling (*jighran*), eating (*aśnan*), moving/walking (*gacchan*), sleeping (*svapan*), and breathing (*śvasan*).

**Commentary**: The author's core point in verse 5.8 (which continues directly into verse 5.9) boils down to a fundamental shift in **identity and observation**:

**1). The Core Point: You Are Not the Machine**

The author is telling the practitioner to establish a permanent internal realization (*manyeta tattva-vit*): **the core self/ ātmā is not the driver or author of automatic, physical processes.**

When you eat, walk, smell, or breathe, those actions belong entirely to the physical apparatus---the biological vehicle operating inside the physical world (*loka*). The knower of thatness/truth/ultimate reality (*tattva-vit*) observes these involuntary biological operations without mistaking them for the real self.

**The Outer Operations:** Seeing, hearing, touching, moving, and breathing belong to the laws of cause and effect in this world (*loka*). They rise and fall continuously according to biological necessity.

**The Realization (*Naiva kiñcit karomīti*):** The practitioner realizes, *\"These physical functions are occurring, but they are **not our self, nor part of our self, nor belonging to our self**.\"*

**2) Atmic Awakening Vs. Complete Sovereignty**

To understand the full scope of verse 5.8, a sharp distinction must be drawn between two fundamentally different spiritual paradigms: **Awakening to the Ātma** versus **Total Purification for Complete Sovereignty**.

**Paradigm 1: Awakening to the Ātma (Abiding Within)**

The mechanism described in the verse can function effectively if the practitioner operates through inward detachment. In this approach:

- **The Mechanism:** The practitioner awakens to the realization that the *ātma* is a fractal portion of the universal source and that all living beings share this identical underlying *ātmic* essence.

- **The Orientation:** Grounded fully in the inner *ātma*, the practitioner abides within, allowing the external, worldly apparatus---seeing, hearing, touching, eating, moving, and breathing---to run on automatic pilot without personal identification.

- **The Limitation:** The primary key here is simply **awakening to the *ātma* and abiding in it**. Because this approach views the *ātma* as intrinsically unified with the source of the universe, it remains unconcerned with its ultimate dependency on that cosmic source, preparing itself merely to re-merge into it.

**Paradigm 2: The World-Transcending Path (Purification for Full Independence)**

In direct contrast stands the path of the true world-transcending renunciate (*muni, samana, bhikkhu/bhikkhuni*), where the overarching goal is not merely abiding in an essence, but achieving **absolute independence and full sovereignty**:

- **The Uprooting of Attachment:** The renunciate does not stop at observing physical actions as external. The true target is the systematic purification and total eradication of the root causes of worldly binding---*rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. These defilements are the magnetic glue that keeps the mind (*citta*) anchored to the world (*loka*) and tied to the cycles of birth and death.

- **Separation from the Universal Source:** True liberation demands severing dependence not only from the manifest world, but also from the source of the universe. Merging back into a cosmic source is not ultimate freedom; it is a retention of subtle dependency.

- **The Ultimate Goal---Exit and Sovereignty:** Through absolute purification (*sīla*, *samādhi*, *paññā*), the *citta* achieves full autonomy. Fully disenchanted with all worldly phenomena, the sovereign, purified *citta* transcends the entire cosmos---exiting the universe through the **Pure Abodes Brahma realms** into the **Deathless**.

**Ātmic Awakening:** Focuses on shifting awareness to the inner *ātma* so bodily activities no longer disturb the mind. This achieves its ultimate goal of eliminating sufferings when living and performing duties in the world, accepting ultimate subsumption back into the universal source.

**Renunciate Purification:** Focuses on the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* to achieve absolute separation from both the world and its cosmic source, the goal is not to eliminate suffering as a feeling, but securing total sovereignty and irreversible exit from the universe.

**[9](javascript:void(0))   pralapan visṛjan gṛhṇann, unmiṣan nimiṣann api;**

**     indriyāṇ īndriyārtheṣu, vartanta iti dhārayan.**

Even while speaking (*pralapan*), letting go (*visṛjan*), and grasping/ holding things (*gṛhṇan*), opening the eyes (*unmiṣan*) and closing the eyes (*nimiṣan*) as well (*api*); \[realizing that\] the bodily sensory faculties (*indriyāṇi*) are interacting/ operating (*vartante*) among other sensory faculties (*indriyārtheṣu*), holding firm (*dhārayan*) to this \[awareness\] (*iti*).

**Commentary**: **Emotional Management vs. Existential Transcending**

Building directly upon the previous distinction between Ātmic Awakening and World-Transcending Purification, we must address the **fundamental divergence in their ultimate targets**. The core difference lies in whether a path aims merely at managing psychological feeling tones (*vedanā*) within the world, or at solving the structural, existential dilemma of cosmic decay and death.

**1). Ātmic Awakening: The Psychological / Emotional Level**

The Ātmic Awakening (Buddhi/Buddha) path operates primarily at the level of subjective feeling tones (*vedanā*) and mental disturbances:

- **The Scope:** Its primary target is the reduction or total elimination of *suffering as a feeling or emotion* (grief, distress, anxiety, or mental agitation) while interacting with the world (*loka*).

- **The Method:** By shifting awareness away from the physical apparatus to the inner *ātma*, the practitioner establishes an inner witness. Because bodily actions, sensory inputs, and external events are seen as belonging to the outer machine, they no longer disturb the peace of the mind.

- **The Result:** This achieves its ultimate goal within this life: peaceful living and the undisturbed execution of worldly duties. The practitioner manages emotional suffering while remaining inside the universe, content with eventual subsumption back into the universal cosmic source.

**2). World-Transcending Purification: The Existential Level**

In contrast, the path of World-Transcending Purification operates strictly at the **existential level**, targeting the structural mechanics of reality rather than mere emotional states.

- **Correction of the Term *Dukkha*:** Modern academia and popular translations make a grave error by translating *dukkha* as \"suffering.\" This reduces a profound cosmological law to a mere psychological feeling (and also translate ātmā as self, a psychological identity instead of soul-spirit, an existential identity). What Gautama the Supreme Arahant designated as *dukkha* is not emotional pain, but the **structural unsatisfactoriness and inherent badness of worldly existence**---specifically the inescapable universal laws of **aging, decay, dissolution, and death**.

- **The Core Objective:** The goal is not to cultivate emotional calm while continuing cyclically to decay and die within *saṃsāra*. The goal is to achieve the **Deathless** (*Amata*)---to completely transcend the world\'s universal laws of decay and mortality.

- **The Mechanism of Eradication:** To conquer this existential trap, the renunciate does not merely detach feelings from actions. The practitioner engages in the complete eradication of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. These defilements of the ātmā are the structural ties that bind the mind-heart (*citta*) to the universe with its laws of decay and death.

- **Absolute Sovereignty and Exit:** The goal of the true renunciate is not peace while performing duties in the world, but **absolute renunciation of worldly duties and separation from both the world (*loka*) and its cosmic source**. By purifying the *citta* of all worldly binding forces, the practitioner secures total sovereignty---irreversibly exiting the universe through the **Pure Abodes Brahma realms** into the deathless realm of **Nibbāna**.

**[10](javascript:void(0))   brahmaṇy ādhāya karmāṇi, saṇgaṁ tyaktvā karoti yaḥ;**

**       lipyate na sa pāpena, padma-patram ivāmbhasā.**

Having placed (ādhāya) actions (karmāṇi) in Brahma (brahmaṇi), having abandoned (tyaktvā) attachment (saṅgam), he who (yaḥ) acts (karoti)---he (sa) is not (na) stained (lipyate) by badness (pāpena), just as (iva) a lotus leaf (padma-patram) \[is not wetted\] by water (ambhasā).

**Commentary: The Parallel Mechanics of Disenchantment & The Critical Distinction of Brahmā**

**1). The Shared Operational Dynamic: Disenchantment and Non-Defilement**

The basic mental technique described in this verse---detaching from action and remaining unstained---mirrors the preliminary stages of the **Renunciate Purification Path**: **Disenchantment (*Nibbida*):** When the renunciate detaches from the worldly altogether, in absolute disenchantment from the world state, then no new defilements (*kilesa* / *pāpa*) enter or stain the mind-heart (*citta*).

However, while the karma-yoga practitioner uses this non-defilement to remain peacefully inside the world, the true renunciate uses this disenchantment to **sever ties completely** and prepare for total exit.

**2). The Misconception: Equating Brahmā with the Universal Source**

A major point of confusion---often perpetuated or deliberately conflated by later non-dualistic and Vedāntic authors---is equating **Brahmā** (or the Brahminic realm) with the ultimate, originative Source of the universe.

**Brahmā as the Progenitor of Mankind:** In ancient cosmology, **Brahmā** is not the creator or source of the universe (*loka* or *saṃsāra*). Rather: Brahmā is the **father/progenitor (*pitā*) of cosmic mankind** as a race. Brahmā upholds the values of virtue, purity, non-harming, non-violence, kindness, compassion etc., often called civilized way. Brahmā is a lineage and ancestral origin for noble beings. This is the complete opposite of the universal law of competition, law of power, to consume or be consumed, etc. the natural way. Aligning with Brahmā\'s standard/ values (*Brahmacariya*) allows a practitioner to join the Brahmā realms.

**[11](javascript:void(0))   kāyena manasā buddhyā, kevalair indriyair api;**

**       yoginaḥ karma kurvanti, saṅgaṁ tyaktvātma-śuddhaye.**

By the body (kāyena), by the mind (manasā), by the intellect (buddhyā), and even (api) by the unattached (kevalaiḥ) sense faculties (indriyaiḥ); the disciplined practitioners (yoginaḥ) perform (kurvanti) action (karma), having abandoned (tyaktvā) attachment (saṅgam), for the purification of the self (ātma-śuddhaye).

**Commentary**: **Disenchantment (*Nibbidā*) vs. Complete Fading off Passion (*Virāga*)**

The claim in this verse: "for the purification of the self (ātma-śuddhaye)" is a wrong claim. There is a fundamental structural boundary in spiritual mechanics: **the difference between stopping new defilements into the *ātmā* through detachment (*nibbidā*) and completely eradicating underlying defilements through fading of passion (*virāga*)**.

**1). Stopping New Defilements vs. True Purification**

The practice described in karma-yoga---acting without attachment---serves primarily as a **defensive barrier**:

- **Prevention of New Pollution:** By dropping personal ownership (*saṅga*) over actions and their outcomes, the practitioner prevents new karmic residue and emotional defilements (*kilesa* / *pāpa*) from sticking to or polluting the inner self (*citta* / *ātman*).

- **The Limit of Karma-Yoga:** This level of detachment corresponds to **disenchantment (*nibbidā*)**---completely withdraw from the worldly.

- **Why It Falls Short of True Purification (*Ātma-Śuddhi*):** While Karma-Yoga prevents *new* defilements from entering, it leaves the deep-seated, underlying tendencies (*anusaya*) of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* intact within the mind. The tank is no longer being filled with dirty water, but the sediment at the bottom has not been removed.

**2). The Crucial Renunciate Step: *Virāga* (Fading off Worldly Passions)**

True purification (*ātma-śuddhaye*) requires moving beyond mere disenchantment from the world (***nibbidā***) into **virāga** (dispassion / fading away of worldly passions):

a)  **Eradication of Latent Roots:** *Virāga* is the active, deep-level fading away of all subtle *raga*, *dosa*, and *moha* that binds the soul-spirit *ātmā* in the *saṃsāric world*.

b)  **Beyond Surface Duty:** Karma-Yoga relies on continuing worldly action while maintaining a mental posture of detachment. Renunciate practice, however, applies profound *Samādhi* and *Paññā* to systematically dry up the roots of defilements.

c)  **The Sequential Mechanics:** Disenchantment from the world (Nibbidā) -\> Fading of worldly Passion (Virāga) -\> Liberation from the world (Vimutti)

Karma-Yoga stops the continuous accumulation of new pollution, but attributing complete purification (*ātma-śuddhaye*) to this stage alone conflates the preliminary boundary wall (*nibbidā*) with the actual eradication of defilements (*virāga*). True purification requires the deeper renunciate work of *virāga* to fully cleanse the *citta* for total liberation.

**[12](javascript:void(0))   yuktaḥ karma-phalaṁ tyaktvā, śāntim āpnoti naiṣṭhikīm;**

**       ayuktaḥ kāma-kāreṇa, phale sakto nibadhyate.**

The yoked \[karma-yoga, with ātmā\] (yuktaḥ), having abandoned (tyaktvā) the fruit of action (karma-phalam), attains (āpnoti) absolute / ultimate peace (śāntim naiṣṭhikīm); the un-yoked (ayuktaḥ), acting under the impulse of desire (kāma-kāreṇa), attached (saktaḥ) to the fruit (phale), becomes bound (nibadhyate).

**[13](javascript:void(0))   sarva-karmāṇi manasā, saṁnyasyāste sukhaṁ vaśī**

**       nava-dvāre pure dehī, naiva kurvan na kārayan**

Renouncing (saṁnyasya) all actions (sarva-karmāṇi) mentally (manasā), the self-controlled embodied one \[ātmā\] (dehī vaśī) dwells (āste) happily (sukham) in the city with nine gates \[Refers to the physical body housing nine sensory portals (two eyes, two ears, two nostrils, mouth, urinary organ, and anus)\] (nava-dvāre pure), neither acting (na eva kurvan) nor causing action (na kārayan).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   na kartṛtvaṁ na karmāṇi, lokasya sṛjati prabhuḥ;**

**       na karma-phala saṁyogaṁ, svabhāvas tu pravartate.**

The Inner Master \[*citta* / *ātman*\] (prabhuḥ) creates (sṛjati) neither the state of doership (na kartṛtvam) nor actions (na karmāṇi) for the world (lokasya), nor the union with the fruits of action (na karma-phala-saṁyogam); but inherent nature / natural law (svabhāvaḥ tu) operates / proceeds (pravartate).

**[15](javascript:void(0))   nādatte kasyacit pāpaṁ, na caiva sukṛtaṁ vibhuḥ;**

**       ajñānenāvṛtaṁ jñānaṁ, tena muhyanti jantavaḥ.**

The All-Pervading \[*citta* / *ātman*\] (vibhuḥ) takes on (ādatte) neither the bad deed / demerit (pāpam) nor indeed the good deed / merit (sukṛtam) of anyone (kasyacit); knowledge (jñānam) is enveloped (āvṛtam) by ignorance (ajñānena)---by that, living beings (jantavaḥ) are deluded (muhyanti).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   jñānena tu tad ajñānaṁ, yeṣām nāśitam ātmanaḥ;**

**       teṣām āditya-vaj jñānaṁ, prakāśayati tat param.**

But (tu) for those (yeṣām) by whom that (tat) ignorance (ajñānam) of the ātmā (ātmanaḥ) is destroyed (nāśitam) through knowledge (jñānena)---for them (teṣām), knowledge (jñānam), like the sun (āditya-vat), illuminates (prakāśayati) the Supreme / the Beyond / the Transcendent (tat param).

**[17](javascript:void(0))   tad-buddhayas tad-ātmānas, tan-niṣṭhās tat-parāyaṇāḥ;**

**       gacchanty apunar-āvṛttiṁ, jñāna-nirdhūta-kalmaṣāḥ.**

That Buddha / Awakening (tad-buddhayaḥ), That ātmā (tad-ātmānaḥ), That firm foundation (tan-niṣṭhāḥ), That path to the Beyond/ Supreme (tat-parāyaṇāḥ)---their defilements fully cleansed by knowledge (jñāna-nirdhūta-kalmaṣāḥ), they go to (gacchanti) non-returning / complete escape from reincarnation (apunar-āvṛttim).

**Commentary**: This non-dual view of liberation as the awakening / Buddha of a singular, underlying reality where the individual ātmā which is no difference than that underlies all living beings, dissolves into the universal ātmā beyond the manifest world. Thus, *apunar-āvṛttim* (non-return) represents merging permanently into that absolute whole ātmā, ending the illusion of separation and stopping the cycle of rebirth in the manifest world.

This solves the core problem---ending involuntary reincarnation in the world---as ultimate oneness with the Source of the Universe, not the renunciate's path to ultimate departure, a completely different ultimate destination.

**[18](javascript:void(0))   vidyā-vinaya-sampanne, brāhmaṇe gavi hastini;**

**       śuni caiva śva-pāke ca, paṇḍitāḥ sama-darśinaḥ.**

Fully endowed with (sampanne) knowledge (vidyā) and *vinaya*, in a *brāhmaṇa*, in a cow (gavi / go), in an elephant (hastin), and even in a dog (śvan) and an outcast / one outside the social order (śvapāka)---the wise (paṇḍita) see/ perceive with equal \[status\] (samadassī).

**[19](javascript:void(0))   ihaiva tair jitaḥ sargo, yeṣāṁ sāmye sthitaṁ manaḥ;**

Indeed here (iha eva), by that (tair) letting go (sarga), it is overcome / conquered (jita) by those whose mind (manas) is established (sthita) in sameness / unbiased equilibrium (sāmya); for the *brahma* (source, ultimate reality) is faultless (nirdoṣa) and equal (sama)---therefore (tasmāt), they are established (sthita) in the *brahma*.

**[20](javascript:void(0))   na prahṛṣyet priyaṁ prāpya, nodvijet prāpya cāpriyam;**

One should not rejoice (na prahṛṣyet) upon encountering (prāpya) the pleasant / agreeable (priyam), nor (na) should one be agitated / disturbed (udvijet) upon encountering (prāpya) indeed (ca) the unpleasant / disagreeable (apriyam)---steadfast in (sthira) the awakening (buddhi), free from delusion (asammūḍha), knowing *Brahma* (brahma-vid), firmly established (sthita) in *Brahma*.

One whose *ātmā* is unattached (asakta) to external contacts (bāhya-sparśa) finds (vindati) that happiness/ bliss (sukha) in the *ātmā*; he (sa), who is yoked with his *ātmā* (yukta-*ātmā*) and in union with (yoga) *Brahma*, experiences (aśnute) imperishable / undecaying (akṣaya) happiness / bliss (sukha).

For (hi) those (ye) sensory pleasures (bhogāḥ) born of contact \[with sense objects\] (saṁsparśa-jāḥ) are (santi) indeed (eva) sources of suffering / pain (duḥkha-yonayaḥ); they have a beginning and an end (ādi-antavantaḥ), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya); the enlightened / awakened *budha* does not delight (na ramate) in them (teṣu).

One who is able (yaḥ śaknoti) right here \[in this life\] (iha eva), before (prāk) the release from the body (śarīra-vimokṣanāt), to withstand / endure (soḍhum) the force / impulse (vegam) born of sensual desire (kāma) and anger / aversion (krodha)---that person (saḥ naraḥ) is yoked \[with his *ātmā*\] (yuktaḥ), happy / blissful (sukhī).

One who is happy within (antaḥ-sukhaḥ), whose delight is within (antar-ārāmaḥ), and likewise (tathā) whose light / vision is within (antar-jyotiḥ) indeed (eva)---that (saḥ) *yogī*, having become (bhūtaḥ) *Brahma*, ascend to (adhigacchati) *brahma-nirvāṇa*.

The *ṛṣis* (seers) whose impurities are destroyed (*kṣīṇa-kalmaṣāḥ*), whose dualities are severed (*chinna-dvaidhāḥ*), whose *ātmā* are restrained (*yatātmānaḥ*), and who are devoted to the welfare of all beings (*sarva-bhūta-hite ratāḥ*), attain (*labhante*) *brahma-nirvāṇa*.

For these practitioner / strivers (yatīnām) who are completely freed from sensual desire and anger (*kāma-krodha-viyuktānām*), whose minds are restrained (*yata-cetasām*), and who have known *ātmā* (*vidita-ātmanām*), the *brahma-nirvāṇa* exists (*vartate*) all around / everywhere (*abhitaḥ*).
