Having made (kṛtvā) external sense contacts (bāhyān sparśān) strictly external (bahir), and \[fixing\] the vision (cakṣuḥ) indeed (ca eva) between (antare) the eyebrows (bhruvoḥ), having made equal (samau kṛtvā) the incoming and outgoing breaths (prāṇāpānau) moving within the nostrils (nāsābhyantara-cāriṇau)\...

**[28](javascript:void(0))   yatendriya-mano-buddhir, munir mokṣa-parāyaṇaḥ;**

**       vigatecchā-bhaya-krodho, yaḥ sadā mukta eva saḥ.**

The *muni* whose sense faculties, mind, and intellect/*buddhi* are thoroughly restrained (*yata-indriya-manaḥ-buddhiḥ*), who is on the path to the Supreme / Transcendent liberation (*mokṣa-parāyaṇaḥ*), and from whom worldly craving/desire, fear, and anger have departed (*vigata-icchā-bhaya-krodhaḥ*)---that individual (*saḥ*) who remains thus is indeed (*eva*) liberated forever (*sadā muktaḥ*).

**[29](javascript:void(0))   bhoktāraṁ yajña-tapasāṁ, sarva-loka-maheśvaram;**

**       suhṛdaṁ sarva-bhūtānāṁ, jñātvā māṁ śāntim ṛcchati.**

Having known (*jñātvā*) Me \[Kṛṣṇa\] (*mām*) as the receiver (*bhoktāram*) of sacrifices (*yajña*) and spiritual forces (*tapasām*), the Great *īśvaram* \[Sovereign ruler / Lord\] of all worlds (*sarva-loka-maheśvaram*), and one with good heart (*suhṛdam*) for all beings (*sarva-bhūtānām*), one attains (*ṛcchati*) peace (*śāntim*).

**Commentary**: In this verse, Kṛṣṇa explicitly declares His absolute authority over existence: He is **Sarva-loka-maheśvara**---the Great Sovereign Ruler of all worlds---and the ultimate receiver of all sacrifices (*yajña*) and spiritual forces (*tapasām*).

This self-declaration establishes a precise cosmic structure of **Absolute Lordship**. Within this framework, Kṛṣṇa is not merely a benevolent protector; He is the sole, unquestioned Sovereign of the entire cosmic worlds. Understanding this structural reality unveils the origin of teachings on self-denial and illuminates the fundamental distinction between subjugated integration within a cosmic kingdom and absolute individual liberation.

**1). Lordship (*Īśvaratva*) and the Necessity of Surrender**

A kingdom cannot have two sovereign rulers. Within any domain governed by a *Īśvara*, all inhabitants exist under the ruler's authority, laws, and supreme willpower.

- **The Price of Entry and Inclusion:** To remain within, draw shelter from, or join Kṛṣṇa's cosmic kingdom, an individual must completely yield their own autonomy. Cosmic Lordship inherently demands total surrender (*prapatti* or *śaraṇāgati*).

- **The Origin of Self-Denial:** This is the root of doctrines that demand the denial, surrender, or sacrifice of the individual *ātmā*, soul, or spirit. To fit into a cosmic order ruled by a single Supreme Sovereign, individual sovereignty is viewed as egoistic rebellion (*ahaṁkāra*). The individual must surrender their self-determination, merging their will into the will of the Lord. Within the universe, individual sovereignty is fundamentally incompatible with the Lordship of the *Īśvara*.

**2). Gautama's Liberation Path with Individual Sovereignty**

For the individual who seeks unconditioned freedom, the path cannot be one of yielding sovereignty to a Lord within a cosmic kingdom. True liberation (*mokṣa*) requires a radical alternative:

- **Preserving Individual Sovereignty:** To maintain complete autonomy and independence, the individual spirit cannot settle within any cosmic hierarchy or kingdom.

- **Leaving the Universe:** True liberation is the path of exit. By recognizing that all world realms (*sarva-loka*), are under one *Īśvara*, the practitioner disenchants the mind (*citta*) from all cosmic domains.

- **Transcendence Beyond All Realms:** The practitioner recognizes that rather than surrendering the soul-spirit to a cosmic sovereign within the world system, the individual completely severs ties to the universe itself---transcending all world realms (*loka*) to realize unconditioned, sovereign liberation outside the cosmic order.

**\**

### Chapter 6 Dhyana Yoga: The Yoga of Jhāna - Meditation and Mind Control

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   anāśritaḥ karma-phalaṁ, kāryaṁ karma karoti yaḥ;**

**     sa saṁnyāsī ca yogī ca, na niragnir na cākriyaḥ.**

Bhagava (Kṛṣṇa) said: One who performs (*karoti*) obligatory duty (*kāryaṁ karma*) without relying (*anāśritaḥ*) on the fruit of action (*karma-phalam*)---he (*saḥ*) is a renunciant (*saṁnyāsī*) and (*ca*) a *yogī*; not (*na*) one without maintaining the household ritual fire / externally abandoned ritual duties (*niragniḥ*), nor (*na*) one inactive / without action (*akriyaḥ*).

**Commentary**: **Śrī** (श्री)

**1). Etymology & Verbal Root Origin**

The word **Śrī** (श्री) originates from the primary Proto-Indo-Aryan verbal root śri, which signifies **\"to lean against, attach to, resort to, cover, cook, or diffuse light\"**.

a)  **Verbal Root (śri):** Originally carries the sense of *support, shelter, radiance, or diffusive warmth*.

b)  **Noun Formation (Śrī):** Derived as a feminine abstract noun, it meant **\"radiance, resplendence, beauty, majestic dignity, and auspicious abundance.\"**

c)  **Prakrit / Pāḷi Phonetic Shift:** In Middle Indo-Aryan, the consonant cluster śr- softened to s-, transforming Śrī into **Siri** (सिरी) or **Sī** (सी).

The pictograph of श्री does carry the meaning of "to lean against, attach to, resort to, cover, or seek shelter in." To uncover **what attaches to what** and **who leans upon whom** in the word **Śrī** (श्री) requires new reading of the ancient verses. Depending on whether the grammar operates in the **active voice** (*Śrayate* --- \"She/It attaches to another\") or the **passive voice** (*Śrīyate* --- \"She/It is attached to / leaned upon by others\"), the verses reveal three distinct layers of attachment.

**Layer 1: What Attaches to Whom?**

**Radiance & Sovereign Power Attach to the Divine/Leader**

In the earliest Vedic layers, **Śrī** is the radiant energy, beauty, and authority that **attaches itself to, coats, or rests upon** a divine pilot, king, or noble entity.

**Ṛgveda 1.85.1-5 (The Maruts / Storm-Beings):**

**Verse 1: The Ascent and Deployment into the Atmosphere**

\"They who adorn themselves like brides with metallic/shining armors (*śramabhiḥ*), the fierce Maruts advance in their chariots (*ratheṣu*). They shake the high ridges of the sky (*nākasya sānavaḥ*) and set the boundaries of the earth with atmospheric dust/mists (*rajobhiḥ*).\"

**New Decoding:** \"The *Maruts*, gleaming in fitted metallic alloy suits, deploy in their propulsion craft (*ratheṣu*). Re-entering through the upper-atmospheric ridge (*nākasya sānavaḥ*), they generate sonic shockwaves and atmospheric particle dispersals (*rajobhiḥ*) across the planetary grid.\"

**Verse 2: The Core Action (What & Why)**

\"They approach near as an ally/friend (*mitro na*); they attach/hold radiance (*śriyam*) among/upon mortals (*martyeṣām*).\"

**Decoding:** \"The *Maruts* make a close vector approach in non-hostile formation like friendly forces (*mitro na*); they deploy/attach radiant energy-fields (*śriyam*) over the earth humans (*martyeṣām*).\"

**What they are doing:** They are making a controlled, close-proximity landing (*anti sam eti*) near human populations without hostile intent (*mitro na*---acting as a friendly liaison).

**Why:** To extend protection, power, and atmospheric stabilization (*śrī*) to the mortal dwellers below.

**Verse 3: The Source of their Power & Armor**

\"Fierce is the impetuous force of these Maruts; their troop is invincible. He who possesses diverse light (*citrabhānuḥ*) goes far beyond\...\"

**Decoding:** \"Unyielding is the kinetic propulsion output of this Marut team; their unit cannot be breached. Their leader, emitting multi-spectrum frequency emissions (*citrabhānuḥ*), breaches orbital thresholds\...\"

**Verse 4: The Weaponry & Energy Sheath**

\"From the sky, their light spreads far with spears/projectiles (*ṛṣṭibhiḥ*); bound together with their shining armors/radiant harnesses\...\"

**Decoding:** \"Descending from orbit, their directional energy weapons (*ṛṣṭibhiḥ*) illuminate the zone; synchronized in automated link-formation, encased in energy-harnessing suits\...\"

**Verse 5: The Terraforming Objective (The \"Why\")**

\"The wonderfully-adorned Maruts move forward for the sacrifice; shedding rain, the keepers of divine Order (*ṛta*) listen to our calls, releasing the roaring rivers\...\"

**Decoding:** \"The *Maruts* advance to execute operational protocols (*yajñāya*); releasing atmospheric condensation to clear the water tables, these custodians of divine order (*ṛtāvaro*) react to ground transmissions, unlocking the river systems\...\"

Tracing verses 1 through 5 together, the mechanic of **Śrī** (śri = *to coat, attach, lean against, or sheath*) becomes completely logical:

a.  **Self-Encapsulation (The Maruts themselves):**

Before descending, the Maruts \"coat\" or \"attach\" light to their own vessels and bodies (Verse 1 & 4). They are shielded in *śrī*---luminous armor, protective heat-shields, and energy fields.

b.  **Extending the Field to Mortals (*martyeṣām*):**

In Verse 2, when they land close (*anti*) like friends (*mitro na*), they do not keep this energy isolated. They **bestow/attach (*dadhire*) this same *śrī* onto humans**.

c.  **The Purpose (\"Why\"):**

**In the Literal Vedic View:** They bring vitality, royal dignity, atmospheric prosperity (rain/crops), and divine protection to human communities. *Śrī* is the physical sheen of health and divine grace clinging to the tribe.

**In the Decoding View:** They project a localized environmental grid or force-field (*śrī*) over human settlements to shield them from atmospheric radiation, terraforming turbulence, or hostile forces while opening water systems (Verse 5).

**Layer 2: Who Leans Upon Whom?**

**All Lean Upon Śrī (The Refuge / Shelter)**

In the passive grammatical derivation (*Śrīyate sarvair iti Śrīḥ* --- \"She who is leaned upon by all\"), **Śrī** functions as the foundational structure, hull, or shelter. Every living entity **attaches to / leans against Her** for survival and abundance.

**Ṛgveda Khila (*Śrī Sūktam*, Verse 1-6)**

**Verse 1: Invoking the Energy Channel to Summon the Radiant Entity:** *hiraṇyavarṇāṁ hariṇīṁ suvarṇarajatasrajām \|candrāṁ hiraṇmayīṁ lakṣmīm ātavedo ma āvaha \|\|*

\"O Agni (Jātavedas), bring to me that Śrī/Lakṣmī, who is golden-hued, radiant like a deer, adorned with gold and silver garlands, bright as the moon, and made of golden abundance.\"

**Decoding:** \"O Power / Energy Beacon (*Jātavedas*), establish a signal vector to direct to my coordinates that high-capacity vessel/entity (*Śrī*), who possesses a golden metallic hull reflectivity (*hiraṇyavarṇām*), bright as the moon (*candrām*), and carries abundant refined resources (*lakṣmīm*).\"

**Verse 2: The Attachment of Permanent Stability (Why She is Summoned):** *tām āvaha jātavedo lakṣmīm anapagāminīm \|yasyāṁ hiraṇyaṁ vindeyaṁ gām aśvaṁ puruṣān aham \|\|*

\"O Agni, bring that Lakṣmī who never departs (*anapagāminīm*), through whom I may obtain gold, cattle, horses, and personnel/servants.\"

**Decoding:** \"O Beacon, lock the orbital positioning of that resplendent module so it remains non-departing (*anapagāminīm*); through tethering to Her system, I secure metallic raw materials (*hiraṇyam*), propulsion stock (*aśvam*), and human personnel support (*puruṣān*).\"

**Verse 3: The Core Command Module & Hull Arrival:** *aśvapūrvāṁ rathamadhyāṁ hastinādaprabodhīnām \|śriyaṁ devīm upahvaye śrīr mā devī juṣatām \|\|*

\"Preceded by horses, situated in the center of the chariot/craft, awakened by the acoustic roar of elephants---I call upon that Divine Śrī; may that Divine Śrī attach to / accept me.\"

**Decoding:** \"Preceded by high-velocity scout escort units (*aśvapūrvām*), anchored at the central core of the primary mobile platform (*rathamadhyām*), heralded by low-frequency acoustic blasts (*hastināda-prabodhīnām*)---I broadcast docking telemetry to that supreme mother-craft (*Śrī*); may She authorize my attachment / entry (*mā devī juṣatām*).\"

**What She is Doing:** She is advancing at the center of a heavily shielded transport craft (*rathamadhyām*), flanked by high-speed escorts (*aśvapūrvām*), emitting massive low-frequency shockwaves (*hastināda*---decoded as acoustic thruster booms or elephant-like horn blasts).

**Why:** To establish a central, immobile sanctuary (*śrī*) over the grounds of the planetary occupants.

**Verse 4: The Physical Composition of the Sanctuary (Golden & Moist/Nourishing):** *kāṁ sosmitāṁ hiraṇyaprākārām ārdrāṁ jvalantīṁ tṛptāṁ tarpayantīm \|padme sthitāṁ padmavarṇāṁ tām ihopahvaye śriyam \|\|*

\"She who is radiant with a gentle smile, enclosed in a golden wall/rampart, moist/nourishing, glowing, contented and granting contentment, seated on a lotus, lotus-hued---that Śrī I invoke here.\"

**Decoding:** \"Encased within a **metallic-gold perimeter shield / hull (*hiraṇya-prākārām*)**, emitting thermal luminosity (*jvalantīm*), containing liquid biosphere reserves (*ārdrām*), fully self-sustained and sustaining all connected units (*tarpayantīm*), stabilized upon a central dampening array (*padme sthitām*)---I call that protective sanctuary module (*Śrī*) down to this coordinate (*iha*).\"

**Verse 5: Seeking Shelter in the Hull (The Active \"Leaning Upon\"):** *candrāṁ prabhāsāṁ yaśasā jvalantīṁ śriyaṁ loke devajuṣṭām udārām \|tāṁ padmanemīṁ śaraṇam ahaṁ prapadye \'lakṣmīr me naśyatāṁ tvā vṛṇe \|\|*

\"That Śrī who is bright like the moon, radiant with fame, exalted in the world, cherished by the gods, surrounded by lotuses---**in Her as my shelter/refuge (*śaraṇam*) I take refuge (*prapadye*)**. May non-prosperity (*alakṣmīḥ*) be destroyed; I choose You.\"

**Decoding:** \"Emitting bright light like the moon and high-frequency luminescent signatures, honored by the crew commanders / gods (*devajuṣṭām*)---**I initiate physical attachment / docking into Her outer structural perimeter (*śaraṇam ahaṁ prapadye*)**. Let all environmental degradation, scarcity, and system decay (*alakṣmīḥ*) be purged from my sector.\"

**Verse 6: The Solar Lineage and the Tree of Life:** *ādityavarṇe tapaso \'dhijāto vanaspatis tava vṛkṣo \'tha bilvaḥ \|tasya phalāni tapasā nudantu māyāntarāyāś ca bāhyā alakṣmīḥ \|\|*

\"O You who are solar-hued (*ādityavarṇe*), born from intense heat/primordial force (*tapasā*); produced from You is the tree, the Bilva. May its fruits, through energy (*tapasā*), drive away inner illusions and external decay.\"

**Decoding:** \"O Commander of solar fleet (*ādityavarṇe*), generated from primary energy synthesis (*tapasā*); from Your laboratory field originates the botanical biosystem (*vanaspati*). May its bio-active yields, driven by primordial force (*tapasā*), neutralize external structural decay (*bāhyā alakṣmīḥ*) and internal neural disruptions (*māyāntarāyāḥ*).\"

When reading Verses 1 through 6 as a single cohesive operational protocol, the mechanics of **Śrī** = *to lean against, resort to, take shelter, or attach*) clarify:

**The Nature of the Refuge (*Śrīyate*):** Śrī is not merely a passive personification of luck; She is described in Verse 4 as having a **golden outer rampart / protective hull (*hiraṇya-prākārām*)** and housing life-sustaining liquid bio-reserves (*ārdrām*). She is situated at the absolute center of the deployment module (*rathamadhyām* in Verse 3).

**The Act of Attachment (*Śaraṇam Prapadye*):** In Verse 3, the seeker broadcasts a request: *\"May She attach to / accept me\"* (*mā devī juṣatām*). In Verse 5, the seeker executes the action explicitly: *\"In Her as my protective shelter/hull (śaraṇam), I attach / enter into refuge (prapadye).\"*

**Why the Seeker Leans Upon Her:** **In the Literal Vedic View:** To escape poverty, famine, disease, and instability (*alakṣmī*). Mortals lean upon Śrī because She holds the solar energy, fertility, royal protection, and food reserves that keep human civilization alive. **In the Decoding View:** To dock ground personnel into a secure, solar-powered mobile habitat (*hiraṇya-prākārām*) that protects against planetary radiation, atmospheric decay, and environmental scarcity (*alakṣmīḥ*), powered by primary energy synthesis (*tapas*).

**Layer 3: Who Attaches to the Ultimate Awakened Being?**

**Śrī (Supreme Glory) Attaches to the Purified Citta / Bhagavān**

In classical Sanskrit and Prakrit Jain texts, **Śrī** (Prakrit: *Siri*) is the ultimate spiritual majesty and perfection. It does not exist as an unanchored force; it **attaches permanently** to the *Bhagavān*, *Arhat*, or *Tīrthaṅkara* who has completely eradicated inner defilements.

**Jain Prakrit Text: *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (Chapter 20, Verse 1-7)**

In Chapter 20 (*Meyārjijjaṁ* or *The Great Renunciation*), the text presents the encounter between King Śreṇika (Bimbisāra) and a young, fully liberated *bhikkhu*. The chapter opens by defining the precise structural relationship between **Siri** (transcendent spiritual resplendence) and the purified **Citta** of an Arhat.

**Verse 1: The Anchoring of Siri to the Purified Vessel:** *sirimanto mahāṇāṇī, vīro \'gga-tava-saṁjuo \|dhammaṁ sāhūhiṁ sāhaṁsi, bhāsai siddhi-muttimaṁ* **/ Sanskrit:***śrīmān mahājñānī, vīro \'gra-tapaḥ-saṁyutaḥ \|dharmaṁ sādhubhiḥ sādhayan, bhāṣate siddhi-muktamam \|\|*

\"The one permanently attached to supreme spiritual radiance (*sirimanto* / *śrīmān*), possessing great knowledge (*mahāṇāṇī*), heroic, yoked to foremost primordial force (*agga-tava* / *agra-tapaḥ*)---expounding the Dhamma practiced by the noble ones, speaks of perfection and liberation (*siddhi-mukti*).\"

**What is happening:** **Siri** (the radiant aura of spiritual completion) is not hovering externally; it is \"held\" (*-manta* / *-vān*) by the being who has generated immense internal power through **primordial force (*tapas*)**.

**Verse 2: The Physical Manifestation & The Approach of the King:** *aneagā-rāyā-sarisaṁ, ujjāṇaṁ nandanaṁ jahā \|tattha so paṇao rāyā, seṇio magahāhivo \|\|*

\"In a park resplendent like the divine Nandana grove of the gods, King Śreṇika, ruler of Magadha, approached that sage with deep reverence\...\"

**Verse 3: The Perception of the Radiant Field (*Siri*):** *pāsāīyaṁ darisaṇijjaṁ, suruvaṁ cāru-dansaṇaṁ \|pasanna-lessaṁ succhāyaṁ, dippantaṁ iva teyasā \|\|*

\"Seeing the ascetic, who was serene, inspiring awe, perfectly formed, lovely to behold, with a tranquil aura/thought-coloration (*pasanna-lessam*), splendid shadow/halo (*succhāyam*), glowing as if burning with raw energy/light (*teyasā*)\...\"

**Why Siri attaches:** The ascetic's total purification of mind (*citta*) alters his subtle aura (*leśyā*). The field of **Siri** manifests physically as an unshakeable, luminous bio-energy halo (*succhāyam dippantaṁ teyasā*) that coats his presence.

**Verse 4: The King's Astonishment at the Young Ascetic:** *tam abbhūyaṁ mahābhāgaṁ, adiṭṭha-puvvaṁ maṇussa-rāeṇa \|vandai maṇasā vagasā, kāeṇa ya maṇorameṇa \|\|*

\"Beholding this extraordinary, highly exalted being---the likes of whom the king of men had never seen before---he paid homage in mind, word, and physical body.\"

**Verse 5: The King Questions the Lack of Worldly Attachments:** *taruṇo tumaṁ asi bhikkhū, ṇāṇa-viṇṇāṇa-saṁjue \|bhuñjāhī māṇusse bhoge, kāyāṇaṁ aṇukampae \|\|*

\"\[The King said:\] You are young, O *bhikkhu*, yet yoked to knowledge and supreme insight (*jñāna-vijñāna*). Enjoy the delights of mortal human existence; have compassion for your own body!\"

**Verse 6: The Ascetic Explains His True Sanctuary (*Anātha-bhāva*):** *aṇāho \'hammi mahārāya, natthi me koi rakkhio \|aṇukampao va natthi, na yassiṁ mama koi vā \|\|*

\"\[The *bhikkhu* replied:\] I am without a worldly master (*anātha*), O Great King; there is no worldly protector for me. I have no guardian, nor do I belong to anyone in this world.\"

**Verse 7: Transcending Worldly Reincarnation into Transcendent Radiance:** *eyāiṁsu mahārāya, samayā ia mayā kaḍā \|tao haṁ niggao rāyā, pāvettā kevalaṁ ṇāṇaṁ \|\|*

\"Thus, O Great King, I entered into monastic discipline and fulfilled my vows. Leaving behind all worldly ties, I departed, attaining absolute omniscience knowledge (*kevala-jñāna*).\"

When analyzing Verses 1 through 7, the mechanics of **Siri** reveal why this spiritual resplendence behaves the way it does:

**Siri is Not Worldly Wealth (*Riddhi*):** King Śreṇika offers the young ascetic human pleasures and material kingdoms (Verse 5). But worldly wealth is transient and bound to the law of cause and effect (*karma*).

**Siri**, by contrast, is the **unshakeable, non-worldly majesty** that naturally coats a mind that has severed all clinging to the world (*loka*).

***Tapas* / Primordial Force:** Verse 1 states that the person is yoked to **foremost primordial force (*agga-tava* / *agra-tapaḥ*)**. It is through the intensity of *tapas* that all karmic defilements (*kamma-raya*) are burned away. As the *citta* becomes pure, **Siri attaches to it permanently**. It cannot be detached because the defilements that cause decay have been eradicated.

**The Physical and Mental Manifestation:** Mentally: great and omniscience knowledge (*mahāṇāṇī* / *kevala-jñāna*) and supreme liberation (*siddhi-mukti*). Physically: In Verse 3, **Siri** manifests as an undisturbed thought-spectrum (*pasanna-lessam*) and a luminous, energy-emitting aura (*succhāyam dippantaṁ teyasā*) that commands immediate reverence even from the highest earthly rulers.

**Later Usage in Titles like *Śrī-Bhagavān***

When prefixed to terms such as *Bhagavān* (*Śrī-Bhagavān*), *Guru* (*Śrī-Guru*), or *Arhat* (*Śrī-Arhat*), it functions as an honorific prefix indicating that the subject is **clothed in spiritual radiance, complete inner perfection, supreme majesty, and sacred authority**.

**2). Śrī in the Most Ancient Veda (Ṛgveda)**

In the Ṛgveda Saṁhitā, **śrī** is generally an abstract noun denoting **luminescent power, majestic grace, or vital force**---often embodied by solar, atmospheric, and divine entities---rather than a single personified deity.

**Example 1: Associated with Maruts (Storm Deities) and Light**

In Ṛgveda 1.85.2, the Maruts are described as putting on *śrī* (splendor/radiance) like a garment: *te aksann anti mitro na sam eti, te śriyaṁ dadhire martyeṣām.*

\"They \[the Maruts\] approach close like a friend; they wear/hold radiance (*śriyam*) among mortals.\"

**Example 2: Dawn (*Uṣas*) as the Personification of Radiance**

In Ṛgveda 3.61.7, the goddess of Dawn (*Uṣas*) is portrayed as mounting her chariot of radiant beauty (*śrī*): *vimuñcantīḥ sudhuro devayugbhiḥ, pra śriyam añjate akra praketaiḥ.*

\"Unyoking the well-yoked steeds with divine harnesses, they display \[or smear themselves with\] resplendence (*śriyam*) through their visible rays.\"

**Example 3: The *Śrī Sūktam* (Late Rigvedic Khila)**

By the time of the *Śrī Sūktam* (an appendix to the Ṛgveda), *Śrī* evolves into a personified principle of divine prosperity and cosmic radiance: *hiraṇyavarṇāṁ hariṇīṁ suvarṇarajatasrajām \| candrāṁ hiraṇmayīṁ lakṣmīm ājātavedo ma āvaha \|\|*

\"O Jātavedas (Agni), bring to me that Śrī, who is golden-hued, radiant like a deer, adorned with gold and silver garlands, bright as the moon, and full of golden abundance.\"

**3). Śrī (Siri) in Ancient Jain Literature**

In the Jain tradition (Prakrit Āgamas and early Prakrit/Sanskrit literature), the root appears as **Siri** (सिरि) or **Sī** (सी). Rather than representing a worldly bestowal of material wealth, **Siri** in Jainism stands for **spiritual perfection, auspiciousness (*maṅgala*), and the transcendent radiance of an Arhat/Tīrthaṅkara**.

**Example 1: The *Kalpa Sūtra* (1st Century BCE / CE)**

In the *Kalpa Sūtra*, during the narrative of Queen Triśalā\'s 14 auspicious dreams before the birth of Mahāvīra, the tenth dream is the vision of Goddess **Siri** (Śrī) seated on a lotus, symbolizing the spiritual majesty of the arriving Tīrthaṅkara: *Tayamāṇaṁ padama-saravar-avagaāṁ siriṁ devīṁ pāsai\...*

\"Then she sees the goddess Siri (Śrī), residing on a lotus-lake, resplendent with divine light and supreme fortune.\"

**Example 2: Prakrit Honorific for the Tīrthaṅkara (*Śrī-Arhat*)**

Early Jain Prakrit texts utilize **Siri** to prefix the supreme qualities of purified beings who have destroyed the defilements (*kamma-raya*): *Siri-Vaddhamāṇa-Jina-paṇiyaṁ\...*

\"Taught by the resplendent (*Siri*) Victorious One (*Jina*), Vardhamāna\...\"

**Example 3: *Siri-manta* (Possessor of Spiritual Majesty)**

In the *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra*, the term *Sirimanta* (Sanskrit: *Śrīmat*) is applied to an ascetic who has attained inner liberation: *Sirimanto mahānāṇī, vīro kassaṁ pahu-ppaho.*

\"Endowed with spiritual resplendence (*Sirimanto*), possessing great direct knowledge (*mahānāṇī*), the heroic ascetic stands master of himself.\"

**Bhikkhu Before Sectarian Schism In Ancient Bharata (India)**

**Chapter 20 of the *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* is about King Śreṇika talking with a Jain practitioner**, a *bhikkhu*. The young monk in Chapter 20 is a Jain ascetic belonging to the lineage of **Vardhamāna Mahāvīra** (the 24th Tīrthaṅkara).

He is also called a **bhikkhu** in this Prakrit text. **Bhikkhu** is an ancient generic Magadhan word for a homeless renunciant---literally \"one who lives on alms\" (derived from the root bhikṣ, to beg for alms).

**(1). Proof of His Jain Lineage in the Text**

Later in Chapter 20 and throughout the *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* (which is one of the core primary scriptures of Jainism), the monk\'s doctrine and lineage are explicitly Jain:

**The Four Vows and Jain Restraints:** The young bhikkhu describes his renunciation in terms of the strict Jain ascetic code (*Samiti* and *Gupti*).

**Destruction of Karma via *Tapas*:** He explains that he burned away his past *karma-raya* (karmic dust) through severe bodily restraint and **primordial force (*tapasā*)**, leading directly to *Kevala-jñāna* (omniscient knowledge).

**Context of the Scripture:** The *Uttarādhyayana Sūtra* records the final sermons delivered by **Mahāvīra** before his liberation (*Nirvāṇa*).

**(2). Why the Word Bhikkhu Appears in a Jain Text**

Before sectarian definitions became strictly fixed in later centuries, the Middle Indo-Aryan word **bhikkhu** (Prakrit) / **bhikṣu** (Sanskrit) was shared across the ancient Magadhan landscape:

**In Early Buddhist Texts (Pāḷi):** *Bhikkhu* specifically became the formal technical title for an ordained disciple of Gautama the Arahant.

**In Jain Prakrit Texts:** The text uses *bhikkhu*, *muni*, *anāgārikā* (homeless one), and *sādhu* (noble practitioner) interchangeably to refer to Jain ascetics.

So while the text calls him a bhikkhu in the broad linguistic sense of an alms-seeking ascetic, **he was ordained under Jain teachers and practiced the Jain path**, not the path of Gautama\'s *Saṅgha*.

**Ordination Ceremony For Jains**

Jains have a grand formal ceremony for ascetic ordination called **Dīkṣā** (दीक्षा) or **Pravrajyā** (प्रव्रज्या / *Pabbajjā* in Prakrit). It serves the same definitive function as ordination in Gautama\'s *Saṅgha*, marking the official transition from householder life (*Gṛhastha*) to a homeless ascetic (*Anāgārikā* / *Bhikkhu*).

While the underlying goal---renouncing worldly life to pursue liberation---is parallel to the Buddhist *Upasampadā*, the Jain **Dīkṣā** ceremony is distinctively rigorous and public, reflecting the intense ascetic demands of the Jain tradition. The ordination process is a multi-day ritualized rite of passage overseen by a senior Ācārya (monastic teacher):

**A. Pre-Ordination Phase: *Varṣīdāna* (The Renunciation)**

Before taking the vows, the candidate undergoes the **Varṣīdāna** (literally, \"gifting for a year\").

**Public Procession:** The candidate is dressed in royal, opulent attire and jewelry, paraded through the town in a grand procession on a chariot or elephant.

**Giving Away Wealth:** Symbolizing the total abandonment of material attachments, the candidate publicly distributes their wealth, money, clothes, and personal possessions to the crowd.

**Familial Consent:** The candidate must receive formal, explicit consent from their family and parents to join the order, demonstrating that all worldly debts and responsibilities have been resolved.

**B. The Ordination Ritual (*Dīkṣā Vidhi*)**

On the designated auspicious day, the core ritual takes place before the monastic assembly and the lay community:

**Stripping of Ornamentation:** The candidate sheds their luxurious clothes and jewelry, washing away all worldly perfumes and cosmetics. They put on simple, unstitched white robes (in the *Śvetāmbara* tradition) or discard clothing entirely (in the *Digambara* tradition).

**Kesh Lochan (Hair Plucking):** A central, defining act of Jain asceticism. The candidate\'s hair is pulled out by the roots in handfuls (often using dried herb powders to facilitate grip). This ritual demonstrates total detachment from physical pain, personal vanity, and bodily identification.

**Taking the Great Vows (*Mahāvratas*):** Standing before the Ācārya, the candidate formally recites and pledges the **Five Great Vows** (*Mahāvratas*)---promising lifelong adherence to total non-violence (*Ahiṃsā*), truthfulness (*Satya*), non-stealing (*Asteya*), complete celibacy (*Brahmacarya*), and non-possession (*Aparigraha*), governed by the strict controls (*Guptis*) and careful observances (*Samitis*).

**Bestowal of Monastic Requisites:** The Ācārya assigns the newly ordained ascetic a new monastic name, severing their past identity, and presents them with their essential ascetic tools (such as the *Rajoharaṇa*---a soft wool whisk used to gently brush micro-organisms out of the way before sitting or walking---and an alms bowl).

**Comparison: Jain Dīkṣā vs. Buddhist Ordination**

  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Feature**                 **Jain Ascetic Ordination (Dīkṣā)**                                                     **Buddhist Monastic Ordination (Pabbajjā / Upasampadā)**
  --------------------------- --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Primary Rite**            **Dīkṣā**                                                                               Two-stage: **Pabbajjā** (novice) and **Upasampadā** (full higher ordination).

  **Physical Testing**        Features severe physical endurance acts like **Kesh Lochan** (hair plucking by hand).   Hair and beard are shaved using a razor.

  **Public Ceremony**         High-profile, dramatic public event featuring the *Varṣīdāna* parade.                   Typically a restrained, monastic-centric ceremony within a designated boundary (*Sīmā*).

  **Vows Taken**              Lifetime, unbending commitment to the **5 Mahāvratas**.                                 Observance of the **Patimokkha** (bhikkhu vinaya).

  **Rejection of Identity**   Immediate and total surrender of past lineage, name, wealth, and caste.                 Abandonment of lay identity and entry into the unified *Saṅgha* brotherhood.
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

In Jainism, the ethical and ascetic framework governing a practitioner is divided into the **Five Great Vows** (*Mahāvratas*)---often referenced historically in its earlier fourfold form (*Cāturyāma Dharma*)---and the **Eighteen Restraints / Controls** comprised of the **Three Controls** (*Guptis*) and **Five Regulations** (*Samitis*).

Together, these form the core structural discipline through which a Jain ascetic (*muni*) halts the influx of new karma (*saṁvara*) and burns away existing karmic defilements (*nirjarā*) through **primordial force (*tapasā*)**.

**I. The Four Vows (*Cāturyāma Dharma*) & The Five Great Vows (*Mahāvratas*)**

Historically, the 23rd Tīrthaṅkara, **Pārśvanātha**, taught the **Fourfold Restraint** (*Cāturyāma Dharma*). The 24th Tīrthaṅkara, **Mahāvīra**, later expanded this into the **Five Great Vows** (*Mahāvratas*) by explicitly separating chastity from general non-possession to address the spiritual discipline of his era.

**The Original Four Vows (*Cāturyāma*)**

1)  **Ahiṃsā-viramaṇa:** Complete abstention from causing harm or violence to any living being (*jīva*), whether fine or gross, mobile or immobile.

2)  **Mṛṣāvāda-viramaṇa:** Complete abstention from false, deceptive, or harsh speech.

3)  **Adattādāna-viramaṇa:** Complete abstention from taking anything that has not been explicitly given (*non-stealing*).

4)  **Bahiddhādāna-viramaṇa / Parigraha-viramaṇa:** Abstention from external attachments, possessions, and worldly entanglements.

**The Five Great Vows (*Mahāvratas* under Mahāvīra)**

When Mahāvīra re-systematized the monastic code, he divided the fourth restraint into two distinct, unbending vows:

**4) Chastity (*Brahmacarya Mahāvrata*):** Complete celibacy in mind, speech, and physical body; total detachment from sensory indulgence.

**5). Non-Possession (*Aparigraha Mahāvrata*):** Total renouncement of all physical property, psychological attachments, and worldly clinging.

**II. The Jain Restraints: *Guptis* and *Samitis***

While the Vows establish what the practitioner *renounces*, the **Restraints** define how the ascetic actively guards the physical vessel and the mind (*citta*) during daily existence. These are categorized into the **Three Guptis** (Total Controls) and the **Five Samitis** (Careful Observances).

**The Three Controls (*Tri-Gupti*)**

The *Guptis* are internal shields or locks that completely curb the three channels of activity (*yoga*---mind, speech, and body) to prevent any karmic influx (*āsrava*):

1)  **Mano-gupti (Control of Mind):** Guarding the mind against negative, worldly, or passional thoughts (*vitakkā* directed toward worldly objects), anchoring it instead in equanimity and self-restraint.

2)  **Vaci-gupti (Control of Speech):** Absolute restraint of speech, maintaining noble silence (*mauna*) or speaking only when essential for spiritual practice.

3)  **Kāya-gupti (Control of Body):** Restraining physical movements, postures, and gestures to ensure the physical frame remains completely disciplined and motionless during meditation.

**The Five Regulations (*Pañca-Samiti*)**

Because an ascetic must still walk, eat, and speak to maintain the physical vessel, the *Samitis* govern how actions are performed with extreme mindfulness so no living beings are harmed:

1)  **Īryā-samiti (Care in Walking):** Walking only on inspected, clear paths during daylight, carefully observing the ground to avoid stepping on insects, plant life, or tiny organisms.

2)  **Bhāṣā-samiti (Care in Speaking):** Speaking gently, deliberately, and without condemnation, anger, or pride.

3)  **Eṣaṇā-samiti (Care in Begging/Receiving Alms):** Accepting alms (*pīṇḍa*) only if the food is completely pure, free from living matter, prepared without the monk\'s involvement, and offered voluntarily.

4)  **Ādāna-nikṣepaṇā-samiti (Care in Handling Utensils):** Inspecting and sweeping any spot with a soft broom before placing down an alms bowl, cloth, or body to prevent crushing micro-organisms.

5)  **Utsarga-samiti / Pariṣṭhāpanā-samiti (Care in Disposal):** Disposing of bodily waste strictly in dry, secluded, organism-free locations where no life forms can be harmed or contaminated.

**Commentary: Redefine Renunciant (*Saṁnyāsī*) and *Yogi* and Rejection of the Tradition**

**1). Redefinition of the Renunciant (*Saṁnyāsī*)**

Traditionally, renunciation (*saṁnyāsa*) demands total world-abandonment---leaving behind the household, family life, ritual fires, and social obligations to enter a homeless state, completely surrendering worldly duties to pursue world-transcendent spiritual practices aimed at the liberation from the worldly trap through transformation of the individual soul-spirit (*ātman*).

In this verse, Kṛṣṇa fundamentally redefines *saṁnyāsa*. Rather than renouncing the world itself, he shifts the scope of renunciation entirely to an internal state: renouncing merely the *fruit of action* (*karma-phala*). Under this framework, a person remains fully immersed in obligatory household and worldly duties (*kāryaṁ karma*), yet is declared a true *saṁnyāsī* simply by remaining unattached (*anāśritaḥ*) to the results.

**2). Subversion of Traditional Yoga Practice**

Traditionally, *yoga*---particularly as framed in rigorous ascetic and meditative traditions---is the highest, most demanding form of personal practice. It requires extreme discipline, intense effort, and deep contemplation to attain *samādhi*, quiet the mind\'s modifications, and attain psychic powers, and finally achieve transcendent liberation.

Kṛṣṇa (or the authorial voice behind the text) subverts this traditional rigor by reinterpreting *yoga* as the performance of ordinary worldly and social duties without attachment. Maintaining the household ritual fire (*sāgnikā*) and carrying out prescribed social obligations are elevated to the status of high *yoga* practice. By conflating outward secular or ritual duty with spiritual realization, this doctrine lowers the threshold of traditional *yoga*, subverting the intense discipline historically required for genuine spiritual transformation.

**3). Rejection of True World-Abandonment and Rigorous *Samādhi***

The verse explicitly invalidates those who undertake genuine world renunciation and dedicated meditative practice: *\"not one without maintaining the household ritual fire (niragniḥ), nor one inactive / without action (akriyaḥ).\"*

Through this declaration, the text directly rejects true renunciants---those who physically abandon household duties including ritual fires, relinquish worldly ties, and engage in dedicated, individual transformation based *samādhi* practice---claiming they are neither true renunciants nor true *yogīs*. Instead, it insists that remaining a householder, keeping the ritual fires burning, and fulfilling active social responsibilities constitute the authentic path. From the standpoint of strict, world-transcending spiritual traditions, this claim inverted the traditional spiritual hierarchy by replacing genuine world-abandonment with householder duty.

Hence, the ancient spiritual landscape degraded from true renunciants to laypeople---shifting from renunciation-based Arahanism to a layperson-centered pursuit of Buddhahood/awakening, from Theravada to Mahayana.

**       samprekṣya nāsikāgraṁ svaṁ, diśaś cānavalokayan.**

Holding (*dhārayan*) the body, head, and neck straight / aligned / equal (*samam kāya-śiro-grīvam*) and steady (*sthiraḥ*), motionless (*acalam*); fixing one\'s sight / looking intently (*samprekṣya*) the tip of one\'s nose (*nāsikāgram svam*), and not looking around in any direction (*diśaś ca anavalokayan*).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   praśāntātmā vigata-bhīr, brahmacāri-vrate sthitaḥ;**

**       manaḥ saṁyamya mac-citto, yukta āsīta mat-paraḥ.**

With the *ātmā* thoroughly calmed (*praśānta*), fear departed (*vigata-bhīḥ*), firmly established in the disciplined practice of *Brahmacariya* (*brahmacāri-vrate sthitaḥ*); subduing the mind-will (*manaḥ saṁyamya*), with *citta* centered on Me \[Krishna\] (*mac-citto*), yoked (*yuktaḥ*), one should sit (*āsīta*) holding Me \[Krishna\] as the supreme goal / intent on Me \[Krishna\] above all else (*mat-paraḥ*).

**Commentary**: The preceding verses detail the precise, objective mechanics of preparing for formal *jhāna* / *dhyāna*: setting up a stable seat, aligning the physical posture (*kāya-śiro-grīvam*), and turning the senses inward away from worldly distractions. Up to that point, the textual instructions align closely with the traditional, universal methodology of concentration cultivation.

In this verse, however, the text shifts its core orientation. By inserting *mac-citto* (\"with *citta* centered on Me\") and *mat-paraḥ* (\"holding Me as the supreme goal\"), the author re-frames the target of meditative absorption. Rather than preserving *jhāna* as a precise, process-driven refinement of consciousness, the practice is redirected toward personal devotion and union with Kṛṣṇa. From the standpoint of early meditative frameworks, this represents a theological adaptation of a functional technology.

In the early teachings of Gautama the Supreme Arahant, *jhāna* is not an act of devotional submission or dynamic union with a deity. It is a precise psychological transformation for the *citta*:

- **Shift in Realm Existence:** Humans basically are Sex Realm (*kāma-dhātu*) beings, driven by sensory engagement and sexual proliferation.

- **Refinement to Ascend to *Rūpa* Realm:** The primary purpose of *jhāna* training is to make it concentrated enough to be able to stably live within the Fine-Material Realm (*rūpa-dhātu*).

- **Structural Elevation:** The practitioner essentially undergoes a complete transformation of the mind-heart *Citta*---ceasing to operate as a sex realm human being and be able to ascend and abide in as a *rūpa* realm being while still in the physical frame.

***Brahmacariya* as the Foundation for Realm Transcendence**

*Brahmacāri-vrate sthitaḥ* (\"firmly established in the disciplined practice of *Brahmacariya*\"). In the traditional meditative framework, *Brahmacariya* (celibacy and total detachment from sensual cravings) is not merely a moral virtue, but a mechanical prerequisite. Because *kāma-dhātu* is defined by sensual and sexual inclination, one cannot stabilize the mind in the *rūpa-dhātu* without completely severing sexual and sensual leaks.

By replacing the objective stabilization of the *citta* in the *rūpa-dhātu* with a devotional focus (*mac-citto* / *mat-paraḥ*), verse 14 redirects the ultimate scope of the practice:

- **Primary Target:** Where Gautama's framework focuses on the suppression of the sensual drive to elevate and establish the *citta* within the Fine-Material Realm (*rūpa-dhātu*), the re-interpreted framework of Gītā 6.14 shifts the target entirely toward directing the *citta* onto a deity (Kṛṣṇa).

- **Role of *Brahmacariya*:** In the original meditative system, *Brahmacariya* serves as an essential, non-negotiable mechanism to sever all energetic connections with the Sexual / Sensual Realm (*kāma-dhātu*). In the Gītā's adaptation, it becomes a moral purity vow required to qualify the practitioner for sacred devotion.

- **Mechanism of Practice:** Traditional *jhāna* practice operates through the systematic abandonment of coarse mental phenomena (such as *vitakkā* and sensual desires), whereas the Gītā's approach relies on absorption generated through personal concentration on the deity.

- **Ultimate Outcome:** Gautama's framework treats the stabilization of *jhāna* as the attainment of higher realms (*rūpa-dhātu*)---a necessary stepping stone toward world-transcending liberation. In contrast, Gītā 6.14 frames personal union with Kṛṣṇa as the supreme goal (*mat-paraḥ*) in and of itself.

By analyzing the verse through this lens, it becomes evident where the traditional, step-by-step methodology of *jhāna* was modified to serve a specific theological narrative, diverging from the original process of systematic realm-transition and pure mental concentration training.

**[15](javascript:void(0))   yuñjann evaṁ sadātmānaṁ, yogī niyata-mānasaḥ;**

**       śāntiṁ nirvāṇa-paramāṁ, mat-saṁsthām adhigacchati.**

Yoke (*yuñjan*) the *ātmā* \[with Krishna\] always (*sadā*) in this manner (*evaṁ*), the *yogī's* mind-will is restrained (*niyata-mānasaḥ*); Attains (*adhigacchati*) peace (*śāntim*) in supreme (*paramām*) *nirvāṇa*, which is abiding in Me \[Krishna\] (*mat-saṁsthām*).

**Commentary**: The author of the *Bhagavad Gītā* executes a deliberate textual and doctrinal redefinition of *nirvāṇa*. By borrowing a term that held an established, specific meaning among early ascetic communities and embedding it directly into a devotional framework centered on Krishna, the Gītā transforms both the mechanism and the ultimate destination of liberation.

**1). The Traditional Early Ascetic View: A World-Transcendent Realm**

In the early ascetic traditions---exemplified by Gautama the supreme Arahant, alongside numerous *munis* and *samaṇas*---*nirvāṇa*/ *nibbāna* is understood as the complete, unconditioned exit from the world and the universe.

It is not a state within the physical cosmos, nor a merge into a universal cosmic Source. It is a deathless realm (*Nibbāna-dhātu*) lying entirely outside the universe with laws such as decay and death. It serves as the ultimate home for the cosmic mankind race with permanent and blissful existence there.

**2). The Non-Dualist & Monistic Shift: Immanence and Merging**

Later **Non-Dualist** movements redefined *nirvāṇa* away from an absolute transcendent exit and toward an immanent oneness, for example:

**Non-Dualists & Zenists:** Reframe *nirvāṇa* not as an external realm to be reached, but as the awakening to one\'s inherent nature of *ātmā* that aligns with the Source of universe, and dissolving the illusion of a separate *ātmā* into the unconditioned source of reality.

**Daoist Integration:** Views ultimate liberation as aligning or merging back into the primordial *Dao* (the ultimate source of the universe), collapsing the distinction between the individual and the cosmic structure.

**3). The Gītā's Redefinition: *Nirvāṇa* as Abiding in Krishna (*Mat-saṁsthām*)**

In Verse 15, the Gītā performs its own unique synthesis by redefining *nirvāṇa* as direct abiding in Krishna. Here, the author of the Gītā retains the venerable word *nirvāṇa*---and emphasizes its ultimate quality with the adjective *paramām* (\"supreme\")---but fundamentally alters its definition through the compound **mat-saṁsthām** (\"abiding in Me \[Krishna\]\" / \"established in Me \[Krishna\]\"):

**Re-anchoring the Ultimate Destination:** Rather than *nirvāṇa* being an unconditioned realm completely outside the cosmic structure, the Gītā defines supreme *nirvāṇa* as a state whose locus and foundation is Krishna the deity (*mat-saṁsthām*).

**From Absolute Transcendence to Devotional Union:** For the follower of Krishna, *nirvāṇa* is no longer defined solely as the disenchantment from the world (*nibbida*), fading of worldly passion (*viraga*) and departure from the universe (*vimutti*); it becomes the peace achieved by yoking (*yuñjan*) the individual soul-spirit *ātmā* to Krishna.

**Semantic Co-optation:** By declaring that *nirvāṇa* is \"abiding in Me \[Krishna\],\" the Gītā absorbs the ultimate spiritual goal of the ancient *samaṇas* and subordinates it directly to the theology of Vāsudeva-Krishna.

Through this single verse, *nirvāṇa* is reinterpreted: it is no longer merely the cessation of *dukkha* and exit from *saṃsāra* into the transcendent realm, but the supreme peace of eternal resting in Krishna.

**[16](javascript:void(0))   nāty-aśnatas tu yogo 'sti, na caikāntam anaśnataḥ;**

**       na cāti-svapna-śīlasya, jāgrato naiva cārjuna.**
