### Chapter 12 Bhakti Yoga: The Yoga of Devotion

***Arjuna uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   evaṁ satata-yuktā ye, bhaktās tvāṁ paryupāsate;**

**     ye cāpy akṣaram avyaktaṁ, teṣāṁ ke yoga-vittamāḥ.**

Arjuna said: Thus (evam), those (ye) devotees (bhaktāḥ) who, being perpetually yoked (satata-yuktāḥ), reverently worship (paryupāsate) You (tvām); and (ca) those who (ye) also (api) \[worship\] the imperishable (akṣaram), the unmanifest (avyaktam)---of these (teṣām), who (ke) are the most accomplished in yoga/union (yoga-vittamāḥ)?

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[2](javascript:void(0))   mayy āveśya mano ye māṁ, nitya-yuktā upāsate;**

**     śraddhayā parayopetās, te me yuktatamā matāḥ.**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: Those who (ye), fixed (āveśya) their mind-will (manas) in Me (mayi), being perpetually yoked (nitya-yuktāḥ), worship and attend upon (upāsate) Me (mām); endowed with (upetāḥ) supreme (parayā) faith and trust (śraddhayā)---they (te) are considered (matāḥ) by Me (me) to be the most accomplished in yoga/union (yuktatamāḥ).

**[3](javascript:void(0))   ye tv akṣaram anirdeśyam, avyaktaṁ paryupāsate;**

**     sarvatra-gam acintyaṁ ca, kūṭa-stham acalaṁ dhruvam.**

But (tu) those who (ye) reverently contemplate and honor (paryupāsate) the imperishable (akṣaram), the indefinable / indefinable in words (anirdeśyam), the unmanifest (avyaktam), the all-pervading (sarvatra-gam), the incomprehensible / unthinkable by the ordinary mind (acintyam) and (ca), the immovable / standing at the summit (kūṭa-stham), the unmoving (acalam), the eternal / constant (dhruvam)---

**  **

**\**

**[4](javascript:void(0))   sanniyamyendriya-grāmaṁ, sarvatra sama-buddhayaḥ;**

**     te prāpnuvanti mām eva, sarva-bhūta-hite ratāḥ.**

Fully restraining (sanniyamya) the aggregate of the senses (indriya-grāmam), possessing equal, unbiased awakening / **buddha** (sama-buddhayaḥ) in all conditions and everywhere (sarvatra); they (te) reach (prāpnuvanti) Me (mām) also (eva), being actively engaged in (ratāḥ) the welfare (hite) of all beings (sarva-bhūta).

**[5](javascript:void(0))   kleśo 'dhikataras teṣām, avyaktāsakta-cetasām;**

**     avyaktā hi gatir duḥkhaṁ, dehavadbhir avāpyate.**

Greater / harder (adhikataraḥ) is the exertion and hardship (kleśaḥ) of those (teṣām) whose minds are attached to the unmanifest (avyaktāsakta-cetasām); for (hi) the unmanifest (avyaktā) path (gatiḥ) is attained (avāpyate) with difficulty and distress (duḥkham) by embodied beings (dehavadbhiḥ).

**[6](javascript:void(0))   ye tu sarvāṇi karmāṇi, mayi saṁnyasya mat-parāḥ;**

**     ananyenaiva yogena, māṁ dhyāyanta upāsate.**

But (tu) those who (ye), renouncing (saṁnyasya) all (sarvāṇi) actions and duties (karmāṇi) unto Me (mayi), holding Me as the supreme goal (mat-parāḥ); meditating upon (dhyāyantaḥ) and worshipping (upāsate) Me (mām) with an undivided/exclusive (ananyena) yoga/union (yogena) alone (eva)---

**[7](javascript:void(0))   teṣām ahaṁ samuddhartā, mṛtyu-saṁsāra-sāgarāt;**

**     bhavāmi na cirāt pārtha, mayy āveśita-cetasām.**

For them (teṣām), I (aham) become (bhavāmi) without delay / swiftly (na cirāt), O son of Pṛthā (pārtha), the savior (samuddhartā) from the ocean of death and rebirth / saṁsāra (mṛtyu-saṁsāra-sāgarāt)---for those whose minds are fixed (āveśita-cetasām) in Me (mayi).

**\**

**[8](javascript:void(0))   mayy eva mana ādhatsva, mayi buddhiṁ niveśaya;**

**     nivasiṣyasi mayy eva, ata ūrdhvaṁ na saṁśayaḥ.**

Fix and place (ādhatsva) your mind-will (manas) in Me (mayi) alone (eva), enter and settle (niveśaya) your awakening/ buddhi faculty of discernment (buddhim) in Me (mayi); thereafter (ataḥ ūrdhvam), you shall dwell (nivasiṣyasi) in Me (mayi) alone (eva)---there is no (na) doubt or hesitation (saṁśayaḥ).

**[9](javascript:void(0))   atha cittaṁ samādhātuṁ, na śaknoṣi mayi sthiram;**

**     abhyāsa-yogena tato, mām icchāptuṁ dhanañjaya.**

Now if (atha), you are not (na) able (śaknoṣi) to firmly set (samādhātum) your mind-heart (cittam) steadily (sthiram) in Me (mayi); then (tataḥ), wish (iccha) to reach (āptum) Me (mām) through yoga of repeated practice (abhyāsa-yogena), O conqueror of wealth (dhanañjaya).

**[10](javascript:void(0))   abhyāse 'py asamartho 'si, mat-karma-paramo bhava;**

**       mad-artham api karmāṇi, kurvan siddhim avāpsyasi.**

If you are (asi) unable (asamarthaḥ) even (api) for repeated practice (abhyāse), become (bhava) one whose supreme focus is action performed for Me (mat-karma-paramaḥ); performing (kurvan) actions and duties (karmāṇi) even (api) for My sake (mad-artham), you shall attain (avāpsyasi) perfection (siddhim).

**[11](javascript:void(0))   athaitad apy aśakto 'si, kartuṁ mad-yogam āśritaḥ;**

**       sarva-karma-phala-tyāgaṁ, tataḥ kuru yatātmavān.**

Now if (atha) you are (asi) unable (aśaktaḥ) to perform (kartum) even (api) this (etat), taking refuge in / relying upon (āśritaḥ) My yoga/union (mad-yogam); then (tataḥ), restrained your ātmā (yatātmavān), make and perform (kuru) the renunciation (tyāgam) of the fruits of all actions (sarva-karma-phala).

**[12](javascript:void(0))   śreyo hi jñānam abhyāsāj, jñānād dhyānaṁ viśiṣyate;**

**       dhyānāt karma-phala-tyāgas, tyāgāc chāntir anantaram.**

Knowledge (jñānam) is indeed (hi) better (śreyaḥ) than mechanical practice (abhyāsāt); absorption meditation (dhyānam) is superior (viśiṣyate) than knowledge (jñānāt); from absorption meditation (dhyānāt) \[arises\] the renunciation of the fruits of action (karma-phala-tyāgaḥ); from renunciation (tyāgāt) \[follows\] peace and stillness (śāntiḥ) immediately (anantaram).

**[13](javascript:void(0))   adveṣṭā sarva-bhūtānāṁ, maitraḥ karuṇa eva ca;**

**       nirmamo nirahaṅkāraḥ, sama-duḥkha-sukhaḥ kṣamī.**

Non-hating (adveṣṭā) toward all living beings (sarva-bhūtānām), friendly and kind (maitraḥ), compassionate (karuṇaḥ) indeed (eva) and (ca); free from possessiveness and ownership (nirmamaḥ), free from egoism and conceit (nirahaṅkāraḥ), maintaining equanimity in pain and pleasure (sama-duḥkha-sukhaḥ), be patient and forgiving (kṣamī).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   santuṣṭaḥ satataṁ yogī, yatātmā dṛḍha-niścayaḥ;**

**       mayy arpita-mano-budhir, yo mad-bhaktaḥ sa me priyaḥ.**

The *yogī* who is constantly (satatam) content (santuṣṭaḥ), whose ātmā is restrained (yatātmā), of firm resolve and determination (dṛḍha-niścayaḥ); whose mind and awakening are surrendered (arpita-mano-buddhiḥ) to Me (mayi)---that one (saḥ) who (yaḥ) is My devotee (mad-bhaktaḥ) is dear (priyaḥ) to Me (me).

**[15](javascript:void(0))   yasmān nodvijate loko, lokān nodvijate ca yaḥ;**

**       harṣāmarṣa-bhayodvegair, mukto yaḥ sa ca me priyaḥ.**

From whom (yasmāt) the world (lokaḥ) does not tremble (na udvijate), and (ca) who (yaḥ) does not tremble (na udvijate) from the world (lokāt); who (yaḥ) is liberated (muktaḥ) from joyful excitement (harṣa), envy and intolerance (amarṣa), fear (bhaya), and anxiety or distress (udvegaiḥ)---that one (saḥ) also (ca) is dear (priyaḥ) to Me (me).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   anapekṣaḥ śucir dakṣa, udāsīno gata-vyathaḥ;**

**       sarvārambha-parityāgī, yo mad-bhaktaḥ sa me priyaḥ.**

Free from worldly expectations and desires (anapekṣaḥ), pure (śuciḥ), capable and skillful (dakṣaḥ), unattached and impartial (udāsīnaḥ), free from anxiety and distress (gata-vyathaḥ); who has completely relinquished all ego-driven worldly undertakings (sarva-ārambha-parityāgī)---that one (saḥ) who (yaḥ) is My devotee (mad-bhaktaḥ) is dear (priyaḥ) to Me (me).

**[17](javascript:void(0))   yo na hṛṣyati na dveṣṭi, na śocati na kāṅkṣati;**

**       śubhāśubha-parityāgī, bhaktimān yaḥ sa me priyaḥ.**

He who (yaḥ) does not become elated (na hṛṣyati), does not hate (na dveṣṭi), does not grieve (na śocati), does not crave (na kāṅkṣati); who completely renounces and relinquishes both agreeable and disagreeable phenomena (śubha-aśubha-parityāgī)---that devoted practitioner (bhaktimān) who \[is such\] (yaḥ) is dear (priyaḥ) to Me (saḥ me).

**[18](javascript:void(0))   samaḥ śatrau ca mitre ca, tathā mānāpamānayoḥ;**

**       śitoṣṇa-sukha-duḥkheṣu, samaḥ saṅga-vivarjitaḥ.**

Equanimous (samaḥ) toward an enemy (śatrau) and (ca) a friend (mitre) and (ca), likewise (tathā) in honor and praise (māne) and dishonor or insult (apamānayoḥ); equanimous (samaḥ) in cold and heat (śīta-uṣṇa), and in pleasure and pain (sukha-duḥkheṣu), completely free from all clinging and attachment (saṅga-vivarjitaḥ).

**[19](javascript:void(0))   tulya-nindā-stutir maunī, santuṣṭo yena kenacit;**

To whom blame, criticism, and insult (nindā) as well as praise and flattery (stutiḥ) are equal and same (tulya-); who is restrained in speech, quiet, and reflective (maunī); content and satisfied (santuṣṭaḥ) with whatever little comes by any chance or means (yena kenacit); homeless and free from attachment to fixed dwelling or place (aniketaḥ); of steady and unshakeable (sthira-matiḥ)---that devoted person (bhaktimān naraḥ) is dear (priyaḥ) to Me (me).

**[20](javascript:void(0))   ye tu dharmyāmṛtam idaṁ, yathoktaṁ paryupāsate;**

**       śraddadhānā mat-paramā, bhaktās te 'tīva me priyāḥ.**

Those who indeed (ye tu) fully follow (paryupāsate) this (idam) teaching/ dharma on the deathless (dharmya-amṛtam) as declared and spoken (yathā-uktam); endowed with unshakeable faith and conviction (śraddadhānāḥ), holding Me as the supreme goal (mat-paramāḥ)---those devoted practitioners (bhaktāḥ te) are exceedingly (atīva) dear (priyāḥ) to Me (me).

### Chapter 13 Kṣetra Kṣetrajña Vibhāga Yoga: The Yoga of Distinction Between Worldly Body-Mind and the Transcendent Soul-Spirit

***Arjuna uvāca***

**       prakṛtiṁ puruṣaṁ caiva, kṣetraṁ kṣetra-jñam eva ca;**

**       etad veditum icchāmi, jñānaṁ jñeyaṁ ca keśava.**

Arjuna said: O Keśava (keśava)! I wish (icchāmi) to know (veditum) this (etat): the making-forth (prakṛtim) and Puruṣa indeed (ca eva), the field of experience through the body/mind complex (kṣetram) and the knower of the field experience (kṣetra-jñam) indeed (eva ca), as well as knowledge (jñānam) and that which is to be known (jñeyam).

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   idaṁ śarīraṁ kaunteya, kṣetram ity abhidhīyate;**

**     etad yo vetti taṁ prāhuḥ, kṣetra-jña iti tad-vidaḥ.**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: This (idam) physical body (śarīram), O Son of Kuntī (kaunteya), is called (abhidhīyate) the field of experience (kṣetram) thus (iti); one who (yaḥ) knows (vetti) this (etat), him (tam) those who know the truth of it (tat-vidaḥ) declare (prāhuḥ) as the knower of the field (kṣetra-jñaḥ).

**[2](javascript:void(0))   kṣetra-jñaṁ cāpi māṁ viddhi, sarva-kṣetreṣu bhārata;**

**     kṣetra-kṣetrajñayor jñānaṁ, yat taj jñānaṁ mataṁ mama.**

Know (viddhi) Me (mām) also (ca api) as the knower of the field (kṣetra-jñam) in all fields of experience (sarva-kṣetreṣu), O Scion of Bharata (bhārata); that which (yat) is the knowledge (jñānam) regarding both the field and the knower of the field (kṣetra-kṣetrajñayoḥ)---that (tat) is considered (matam) by Me (mama) as knowledge (jñānam).

** **

**\**

**[3](javascript:void(0))   tat kṣetraṁ yac ca yādṛk ca, yad-vikāri yataś ca yat;**

**     sa ca yo yat-prabhāvaś ca, tat samāsena me śṛṇu.**

What (yat) that (tat) field of experience (kṣetram) is, and (ca) of what nature/ properties (yādṛk) it is, what its changes and transformations are (yad-vikāri), from where (yataḥ) it originates, and (ca) what (yat) comes from it; and (ca) who (yaḥ) that knower of the field is (saḥ), and (ca) what its powers and spiritual influence are (yat-prabhāvaḥ)---hear (śṛṇu) that (tat) from Me (me) in summary (samāsena).

**[4](javascript:void(0))   ṛṣibhir bahudhā gītaṁ, chandobhir vividhaiḥ pṛthak;**

**     brahma-sūtra-padaiś caiva, hetumadbhir viniścitaiḥ.**

This wisdom regarding the field and the knower of the field has been chanted (gītam) in many ways (bahudhā) by seers and sages (ṛṣibhiḥ), in various (vividhaiḥ) distinct (pṛthak) sacred Vedic hymns (chandobhiḥ), and (ca) indeed (eva) through the verses of the Brahma-sūtra (brahma-sūtra-padaiḥ), which are well-reasoned (hetumadbhiḥ) and definitive and indisputable (viniścitaiḥ).

**[5](javascript:void(0))   mahā-bhūtāny ahaṅkāro, buddhir avyaktam eva ca;**

**     indriyāṇi daśaikaṁ ca, pañca cendriya-gocarāḥ.**

The great primary elements \[earth, water, fire, air, space\] (mahā-bhūtāni), I-maker/ ego-identity (ahaṅkāraḥ), intellect or awakening faculty / *buddhi*, the unmanifest (avyaktam) indeed (eva) and (ca); the ten senses \[five cognitive senses: hearing, touch, sight, taste, smell; and the five active capacity faculties: speaking, grasping, walking, excreting, reproducing\] (indriyāṇi daśa) and the one \[internal organ / mind-will, *mana*\] (ekam ca), and (ca) the five external sense objects \[sound, touch, form/color, taste, odor) (pañca indriya-gocarāḥ).

**[6](javascript:void(0))   icchā dveṣaḥ sukhaṁ duḥkhaṁ, saṇghātaś cetanā dhṛtiḥ;**

**     etat kṣetraṁ samāsena, sa-vikāram udāhṛtam.**

Desire or wish (icchā), aversion or repulsion (dveṣaḥ), pleasure (sukham), *dukkha*, the aggregate (saṅghātaḥ), mind consciousness (cetanā), and fortitude or persistence (dhṛtiḥ)---this (etat) field of experience (kṣetram) together with its modifications and transformations (sa-vikāram) has been described (udāhṛtam) in summary (samāsena).

**[7](javascript:void(0))   amānitvam adambhitvam, ahiṁsā kṣāntir ārjavam;**

**     ācāryopāsanaṁ śaucaṁ, sthairyam ātma-vinigrahaḥ.**

Humility/ freedom from pride or self-conceit/ absence of demand for honor from others (amānitvam), unpretentiousness/ freedom from hypocrisy (adambhitvam), harmlessness or non-violence (ahiṁsā), patience or forbearance (kṣāntiḥ), straightforwardness or integrity (ārjavam), service and reverence to the teacher (ācāryopāsanam), purity or cleanliness (śaucam), steadfastness or unwavering resolution (sthairyam), and restraint or control of ātmā (ātma-vinigrahaḥ).

**[8](javascript:void(0))   indriyārtheṣu vairāgyam, anahaṇkāra eva ca;**

**     janma-mṛtyu-jarā-vyādhi-, duḥkha-doṣānudarśanam.**

Disenchantment (vairāgyam) towards sense objects (indriya-artheṣu), absence of egoism or false self-identity (anahaṅkāraḥ) indeed (eva) and (ca); and the constant profound observation and reflection (anudarśanam) upon the inherent flaw and misery (doṣa) of *dukkha* (duḥkha) in birth (janma), death (mṛtyu), aging and decay (jarā), and disease or illness (vyādhi).

**[9](javascript:void(0))   asaktir anabhiṣvaṇgaḥ, putra-dāra-gṛhādiṣu;**

**     nityaṁ ca sama-cittatvam, iṣṭāniṣṭopapattiṣu.**

Non-attachment (asaktiḥ), non-clinging or freedom from external entanglement (anabhiṣvaṅgaḥ) towards son, wife, home, and the like (putra-dāra-gṛha-ādiṣu); and (ca) constant (nityam) unshakeable mental composure and equanimity (sama-cittatvam) amidst the occurrence of desirable and undesirable events (iṣṭa-aniṣṭa-upapattiṣu).

**[10](javascript:void(0))   mayi cānanya-yogena, bhaktir avyabhicāriṇī;**

**       vivikta-deśa-sevitvam, aratir jana-saṁsadi.**

And (ca) unswerving, single-minded devotion (bhaktiḥ avyabhicāriṇī) unto Me (mayi) through the undivided, exclusive yoga/ union (ananya-yogena); resort to solitary and secluded places (vivikta-deśa-sevitvam), and absence of pleasure or delight (aratiḥ) for the gatherings of people (jana-saṁsadi).

**[11](javascript:void(0))   adhyātma-jñāna-nityatvaṁ, tattva-jñānārtha-darśanam;**

**       etaj jñānam iti proktam, ajñānaṁ yad ato 'nyathā.**

Constant on knowledge of high/transcendent ātmā (adhyātma-jñāna-nityatvam), and seeing the goal for knowledge and truth (tattva-jñāna-artha-darśanam)---all this (etat) is declared (proktam) as knowledge (jñānam); that which (yat) is otherwise and contrary to this (ataḥ anyathā) is ignorance (ajñānam).

**[12](javascript:void(0))   jñeyaṁ yat tat pravakṣyāmi, yaj jñātvāmṛtam āśnute;**

**       anādi mat-paraṁ brahma, na sat tan nāsad ucyate.**

I shall now declare (pravakṣyāmi) that which is to be known (yat tat jñeyam), by knowing which (yat jñātvā) one attains immortality / deathless (amṛtam āśnute), the beginningless (anādi) *Brahma*, having Me as the supreme goal (mat-param), which is spoken of (ucyate) as neither manifest (sat) nor unmanifest (asat).

**[13](javascript:void(0))   sarvataḥ pāṇi-pādaṁ tat, sarvato 'kṣi-śiro-mukham;**

**       sarvataḥ śrutimal loke, sarvam āvṛtya tiṣṭhati.**

That transcendent ultimate reality (*tat*) has hands and feet everywhere (*sarvataḥ pāṇi-pādam*), eyes, heads, and faces facing everywhere (*sarvataḥ akṣi-śiraḥ-mukham*); possessing hearing everywhere in the worldly realm (*sarvataḥ śrutimat loke*), it stands (*tiṣṭhati*) fully enveloping, pervading, and encompassing all (*sarvam āvṛtya*).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   sarvendriya-guṇābhāsaṁ, sarvendriya-vivarjitam;**

**       asaktaṁ sarva-bhṛc caiva, nirguṇaṁ guṇa-bhoktṛ ca.**

Shining forth through the functions of all the senses organs (sarvendriya-guṇa-ābhāsam), yet completely free from all sensory organs (sarvendriya-vivarjitam); unattached and unbound by worldly objects (asaktam), yet supporting and sustaining all (sarva-bhṛt ca eva); untouched by the changing qualities of the world (nirguṇam), yet experiencing and perceiving all worldly modes (guṇa-bhoktṛ ca).

**[15](javascript:void(0))   bahir antaś ca bhūtānām, acaraṁ caram eva ca;**

**       sūkṣmatvāt tad avijñeyaṁ, dūra-sthaṁ cāntike ca tat.**

Existing both outside and inside all living beings (bahiḥ antaḥ ca bhūtānām), both the unmoving and moving alike (acaram caram eva ca); by reason of its extreme subtlety (sūkṣmatvāt), that transcendent reality is unknowable (tat avijñeyam); it stands far away in the distant realm (dūra-stham), yet it is in the immediate presence (ca antike ca tat).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   avibhaktaṁ ca bhūteṣu, vibhaktam iva ca sthitam;**

Undivided in all living beings (avibhaktam ca bhūteṣu), yet standing as if separated into distinct individual forms (vibhaktam iva ca sthitam); that which to be known (tat jñeyam) is the sustainer of all beings (bhūta-bhartṛ ca), the cause of cosmic dissolution (grasiṣṇu), and the cause of cosmic manifestation (prabhaviṣṇu ca).

That \[*jñeyam* / *brahma*\] (*tat*) is declared to be the Light of all lights (*jyotiṣām api jyotiḥ*), situated entirely beyond the darkness of delusion (*tamasaḥ param ucyate*); it is knowledge (*jñānam*), that which to be known (*jñeyam*), to be reached through knowledge (*jñāna-gamyam*), distinctly abiding in the heart of all living beings (*hṛdi sarvasya viṣṭhitam*).

Know (viddhi) that both (ubhau api) ***prakṛti*** (the making-forth) and the ***puruṣa*** (individual observer/person) are indeed beginningless (anādī ca eva); and know also (viddhi ca eva) that changes and evolutes (vikārān) as well as the worldly modes and attributes (guṇān) originate from the making-forth (prakṛti-sambhavān).

That which is to be produced of body and sensory objects (*kārya*), instrumental causes of sensory organs and inner mental faculties (*kāraṇa*), and agency / doership (*kartṛtve*), ***prakṛti*** (the making-forth) is declared to be the operational cause (*hetuḥ ucyate*); in the subjective experiencing of pleasure and pain (*sukha-duḥkhānāṁ bhoktṛtve*), ***puruṣa*** (the individual observer/person) is the cause (*hetuḥ ucyate*).

***Puruṣa*** (the individual observer/person), when abiding in(*sthaḥ hi*) *prakṛti* (the making-forth), experiences (*bhuṅkte*) the worldly modes and attributes born of the making-forth (*prakṛti-jān guṇān*); attachment to these modes (*guṇa-saṅgaḥ asya*) is the cause (*kāraṇam*) for its recurring births in good and bad realms / wombs (*sat-asat-yoni-janmasu*).

The supreme ***puruṣa*** (*puruṣaḥ paraḥ*) residing within this physical body (*asmin dehe*) is declared to be (*uktaḥ ca api*) the witness / overseer (*upadraṣṭā*), the permitter / sanctioner (*anumantā*), the supporter / bearer (*bhartā*), the experiencer (*bhoktā*), the sovereign controller (*maheśvaraḥ*), and the transcendent ***ātmā*** (*paramātmā iti*).

One who comprehends in this manner (*yaḥ evaṁ vetti*) of the ***puruṣa*** and ***prakṛti*** together with her worldly modes and attributes \[sattva, rajas, tamas) (*guṇaiḥ saha*), though conducting under all circumstances (*sarvathā vartamānaḥ api*), he is not born into rebirth (*na saḥ bhūyaḥ abhijāyate*).

Some (*kecit*) perceive (*paśyanti*) *ātmā* by *ātmā* through *Jhāna* (*dhyānena*) in *ātmā*; others (*anye*) achieve this through analytical discernment \[separating *puruṣa* from *prakṛti*\] (*sāṅkhyena yogena*), and still others (*ca apare*) through the yoga of action/karma (*karma-yogena*).

Whatever being comes to be (*yāvat sañjāyate kiñcit sattvaṁ*), whether motionless (*sthāvara*) or moving (*jaṅgamam*), know that (*tat viddhi*), O bull among the Bharatas (*bharatarṣabha*), from the union (*saṁyogāt*) of the field of sense experiences (*kṣetra*) and the knower of the field / observing consciousness (*kṣetrajña*).

One who sees (*yaḥ paśyati*) that actions (*karmāṇi*) are in every way (*sarvaśaḥ*) being executed solely by *prakṛti* (*prakṛtyā eva ca kriyamāṇāni*), and likewise (*tathā*) sees the *ātmā* as the non-doer (*akartāram*)---that person truly sees (*saḥ paśyati*).

**[30](javascript:void(0))   yadā bhūta-pṛthag-bhāvam, eka-stham anupaśyati;**

**       tata eva ca vistāraṁ, brahma sampadyate tadā.**

When one high-sees (*yadā anupaśyati*) the multidimensional beings (*bhūta-pṛthag-bhāvam*) as residing in the One \[like Russian doll\] (*eka-stham*), and manifestation (*vistāraṁ*) as originating from that indeed (*tataḥ eva ca*)---at that moment (*tadā*), one reaches (*sampadyate*) Brahma.

**[31](javascript:void(0))   anāditvān nirguṇatvāt, paramātmāyam avyayaḥ;**

**       śarīra-stho 'pi kaunteya, na karoti na lipyate.**

Because it is without beginning (*anāditvāt*) and free from modes of *prakṛti* (*nirguṇatvāt*), this supreme *ātmā* (*paramātmā ayam*) is undecaying (*avyayaḥ*); even while residing in the physical body (*śarīra-sthaḥ api*), O son of Kuntī (*kaunteya*), it neither acts (*na karoti*) nor becomes tainted, defiled or bound by karmic residue (*na lipyate*).

**Commentary**: **Kṛṣṇa's Misinterpretation of Ātmā**

Kṛṣṇa's formulation here represents a classic mistake: taking the dimensional immunity and continuous survival of the embodied entity (**Ātmā**) across physical realm changes and mistaking it for ultimate, unconditioned liberation. By confusing dimensional persistence inside *saṃsāra* with ultimate release, Kṛṣṇa misleads beings into a passive \"awakening\" (*buddhi*) to a supposedly pre-existing eternal state, completely dismantling the necessity of the true path of renunciation.

The Dual Composition of the Ātmā

The Attā or *Ātmā* in Sanskrit is a complex composite entity with a dual heritage---operating as a morphogenetic information field populated by a fractal of the source consciousness of the universe:

- **The Brahmā:** **Brahmā** is the source, progenitor, and high celestial Father of the cosmic mankind race. **Brahmā** imparts the morphogenetic information field---the structural blueprint, form, knowledge and lineage---that defines the *Ātmā* as the cosmic mankind race within the universe.

- **The Source of the Universe:** The content that fills according to the information field---the underlying essence of mankind consciousness---is a fractal derivative derived directly from the source of the universe, functioning like a cosmic Mother.

**Ātmā Is Not Eternal and Indestructible, it can be Defiled and Purified**

A major misunderstanding in Indian religious thought is the assumption that **Ātmā** in Sanskrit, or **attā** in Pāli, means an absolutely eternal, indestructible, and already liberated soul-spirit. Such an interpretation turns terms describing the **nature and continuity of the living entity** into a claim that the entity is itself an absolute, unconditioned reality.

Like here, **Ātmā** is described as **ajaḥ** (*aja*), **avyayātmā** (*avyayĀtman*), **anāśin**, **nitya**, and **aprameya** describe the Ātmā in relation to the physical world.

Traditional translations routinely inflate the terms ***nitya***, ***anāśin***, and ***aprameya*** into sweeping, abstract metaphysical dogma---rendering them as *\"eternal,\" \"indestructible,\"* and *\"immeasurable.\"* This vague language obscures the operational reality of the text. The true meaning on the contrary is a concrete dimensional boundary: **the real inner entity (Śarīrin, Dehin, Ātmā) simply operates beyond the physical plane and easily survives physical realm changes.**

**1). *Nityasya* (*Nitya*): \"Eternal\" vs. \"Continuously Lasting\"**

**Traditional Error:** Translating *nitya* as \"eternal\" smuggles in an unconditioned, timeless state, confusing the embodied entity inside *saṃsāra* with ultimate liberation (*Nibbāna*).

**Etymological Root:** Derived from the prefix **ni** (*\"downward,\" \"inward,\" \"dwelling within\"*) + the suffix **-tya** (*\"belonging to,\" \"abiding in\"*).

**Literal Sense:** *Ni-tya* literally means *\"inwardly abiding\"* or *\"that which stays continuously in its own state.\"*

**The Clarification:** Etymologically, *nitya* denotes **uninterrupted continuity across time**. It means the embodied entity stays inwardly present and continuously lasts through physical realm changes while the outer 3D physical body (*deha*) crumbles. It is \"eternally\" present only relative to the temporary physical vehicle, remaining bound to cosmic cycles until ultimate release.

**2). *Anāśinaḥ*: Generic \"Indestructible\" vs. \"Physically Non-Perishing\"**

**Traditional Error:** Translating *anāśin* as a blanket \"indestructible\" hides the specific physical threat Kṛṣṇa is addressing on the battlefield.

**Etymological Root:** Privative prefix **a-** (*\"non-\" / \"not\"*) + **nāśin** (from the verbal root **naś**, *\"to disappear,\" \"to perish,\" \"to undergo physical destruction/dissolution\"*).

**Literal Sense:** *A-nāśin* means *\"non-disappearing\"* or *\"immune to physical ruin.\"*

**The Clarification:** In Chapter 2 (and explicitly reinforced in 2.17, 2.20, and 2.23), naś addresses Arjuna's fear that physical weapons (*śastrāṇi*), fire, or physical trauma can annihilate a life. *Anāśin* means the embodied entity is **physically non-perishing**---weapons operate strictly on a lower dimensional plane and cannot cause its dissolution or physical death.

**3). *Aprameyasya*: Mystical \"Immeasurable\" vs. \"Physically Immeasurable\"**

**Traditional Error:** Translating *aprameya* as \"immeasurable\" makes it sound like an incomprehensible theological mystery rather than a statement about physical metrics.

**Etymological Root:** Prefix **a-** (*\"not\"*) + **pra-** (*\"forth/intensively\"*) + **meya** (future passive participle from **mā**, *\"to measure, gauge, weigh, or bound\"*).

**Literal Sense:** *A-prameya* means *\"incapable of being measured or bounded by empirical means (pramāṇa).\"*

**The Clarification:** A 3D physical body can be measured (*meya*) using length, mass, volume, or physical instruments. Because the embodied entity is a higher-dimensional structure, it cannot be detected, weighed, or bounded by physical tools or sensory organs. It is strictly **unmeasurable by physical means**.

These three terms do not mean the embodied entity has achieved ultimate salvation. They simply define its dimensional immunity: physical weapons cannot kill it (*anāśin*), physical scales cannot weigh it (*aprameya*), and physical death cannot stop it from persisting (*nitya*). It easily survives physical realm changes---which is precisely why dying physically changes nothing, and why forced reincarnation (*bhava*) remains a trap until the roots of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* are destroyed.

Similar to ***nitya***, ***anāśin***, and ***aprameya*,** they are used to define how the embodied entity (Ātmā) survives physical destruction, here **ajaḥ** and **avyayātmā** define how the entity operates outside the physical plane of biological origin and thermodynamic decay.

**1). Ajaḥ (Aja): \"Unborn\" vs. \"Non-Originated in the Physical Plane\"**

- **Traditional Error:** Translating *ajaḥ* as a vague mystical attribute (\"uncreated,\" \"eternal god\") frames it as an incomprehensible metaphysical assertion.

- **Etymological Root:** Privative prefix *a-* (\"non-\" / \"not\") + *jaḥ* (from the verbal root *jan*, \"to be born,\" \"to be generated,\" \"to come into biological manifestation\").

- **Literal Sense:** *A-jaḥ* literally means \"non-generated,\" \"unborn,\" or \"not produced through physical birth.\"

- **The Reality:** Biological bodies (*deha*) are generated (*jan*) through physical conception, cellular division, and spatial assembly. Kṛṣṇa stresses that the real entity (*ātmā*) does not originate at biological birth. It enters the physical container from outside the physical system. Because physical birth (*janma*) is merely the pairing of an *ātmā* with a temporary physical vehicle, physical death (*mṛtyu*) is merely its decoupling. The *ātmā* itself is *ajaḥ*---it was never generated by physical parents or formed by physical elements.

**2). Avyayātmā (AvyayĀtman): \"Imperishable Soul\" vs. \"**P**hysically Non-Decaying Inherent Nature\"**

- **Traditional Error:** Translating *avyayātmā* as \"imperishable soul\" treats it as a static, dogmatic label rather than describing its immunity to physical decay of the body.

- **Etymological Root:** Privative prefix *a-* (\"non-\" / \"not\") + *vyaya* (from *vi-i*, \"to pass away,\" \"to decay,\" \"to spend,\" \"to undergo reduction or loss\") + *ātmā* (*Ātman*, \"self,\" \"essential nature,\" \"underlying structure\").

- **Literal Sense:** *A-vyaya-ātmā* means \"having a nature (*ātmā*) that is free from depletion, loss, or physically decay (*avyaya*).\"

- **The Reality:** Every structure in the physical world is subject to entropy (*vyaya*)---matter degrades, physical bodies age, wear out, and lose energy. *Avyayātmā* indicates that the non-physical core entity *ātmā* operates under non-physical laws: it does not suffer structural attrition, energy loss, or qualitative degeneration through the wear and tear of time. Even while operating through a physical body that is steadily decaying, the underlying *ātmā* retains its essential integrity unchanged.

**The Defilement and Pollution of the Ātmā**

The Ātmā carries the accumulated impressions (*saṅkhāra* and *vāsanā*) of countless existences within saṃsāra. Because it is non-physical, its vulnerability lies entirely in the realm of spiritual phenomena.

- **Tainted by Unwholesomeness (*Saṃkiliṭṭha*):** The Ātmā becomes bound and heavy through the accumulation of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. These three roots of defilement act as subtle, non-physical contaminants that pollute the Ātmā.

- **The Mechanism of Bondage:** Volitional actions (*kamma*) driven by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha* stain the entity\'s subtle structure. These defilements (*kilesas*) generate the spiritual weight and craving (*taṇhā*) that lock the Ātmā into involuntary reincarnation (*bhava*).

- **The Misconception of Absolute Purity:** Traditional views that claim the Ātmā is inherently untainted and eternally liberated overlook its operational state within saṃsāra. If the Ātmā were already pure and unconditioned, the need for spiritual discipline, moral restraint, and the eradication of defilements would be meaningless. It is precisely because it is defiled that it remains entangled in the physical and subtle worlds.

**The Purification and Liberation from Worldly Bondage**

Just as the Ātmā can be polluted through unwholesome engagement with the physical world, it can be thoroughly purified through the intentional practice of the ancient path.

- **Eradication of the Roots:** Purification is not a physical process, but the systematic removal of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*. By severing craving and clinging, the subtle impurities that bind the entity to the laws of cause and effect in the world (*loka*) are dissolved.

- **Cultivation through Sīla, Samādhi, and Paññā:**

  **Sīla/ Brahmacariya (Virtuous Conduct):** Prevents the generation of new coarse defilements and stabilizes the moral foundation.

  **Samādhi (Concentration):** activate the spiritual primal force of *tapas* to burn out existing defilements.

  **Paññā (Wisdom):** Penetrates the true nature of worldly phenomena, disenchant the soul-spirit from worldly entanglement (*nibbida*), fading of worldly passion and attachment (*viraga*), this leads to liberation of the soul-spirit from the world (*vimutti*).

**The Trap of Involuntary Reincarnation**

Because the *ātmā* is physically non-perishing (*anāśin*), non-generated in the physical plane (*ajaḥ*), and physically non-decaying (*avyayātmā*), physical destruction cannot terminate its existence. It simply survives the collapse of the physical vehicle and remains bound to the universal laws of decay and dissolution within *saṃsāra*.

These attributes confirm dimensional immunity to physical forces---they do not imply that the entity (*ātmā*) itself has gained absolute freedom or has already attained liberation from worldly trappings. On the contrary, because it is defiled and trapped, it will continue to experience forced, involuntary reincarnation (*bhava*). It must be purified by eradicating the root causes of worldly bondage---*rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*---and then liberated from the world as an Arahant; this is exactly the liberation path of renunciation practice taught by Gautama, the Supreme Arahant.

Traditional dogma mistakenly believes this *ātmā* has already gained its absoluteness and ultimate freedom. Thus, their path to freedom becomes a mere awakening (*buddhi*) to its pre-existing state, turning Gautama the Supreme Arahant's liberation path of renunciation into a procedural pursuit toward Buddhahood without requiring true renunciation. When becoming an Arahant degenerates into becoming a Buddha, Gautama's true *dhamma* and his lineage of *bhikkhus* and *bhikkhunīs* lose their true meaning. This was even predicted by Gautama himself in the Pali Vinaya---that his true *dhamma* would only last for five hundred years.

**[32](javascript:void(0))   yathā sarva-gataṁ saukṣmyād, ākāśaṁ nopalipyate;**

**       sarvatrāvasthito dehe, tathātmā nopalipyate.**

Just as space (ākāśa), though all-pervading (sarva-gatam), is not stained or untainted (na upalipyate) due to its subtlety (saukṣmyāt); so the entity (*ātmā*), though stationed everywhere throughout the physical body (sarvatra avasthito dehe), is not stained (na upalipyate).

**[33](javascript:void(0))   yathā prakāśayaty ekaḥ, kṛtsnaṁ lokam imaṁ raviḥ;**

**       kṣetraṁ kṣetrī tathā kṛtsnaṁ, prakāśayati bhārata.**

Just as the single sun (*ekaḥ raviḥ*) illuminates (*prakāśayati*) this entire world (*kṛtsnaṁ lokam imaṁ*); so, O Bharata, the owner of the field (*kṣetrī*, the core entity *ātmā*) illuminates the entire field (*kṣetraṁ kṛtsnaṁ*, the physical/mental complex).

**[34](javascript:void(0))   kṣetra-kṣetrajñayor evam, antaraṁ jñāna-cakṣuṣā;**

**       bhūta-prakṛti-mokṣaṁ ca, ye vidur yānti te param.**

Those who thus see with the eye of knowledge (*jñāna-cakṣuṣā*) the distinction (*antaram*) between the field (*kṣetra*) and the knower of the field (*kṣetrajña*), and who understand the liberation (*mokṣam*) of beings from *prakṛti* (*bhūta-prakṛti*), they attain the supreme (*yānti te param*).

### Chapter 14 Guṇatraya Vibhāga Yoga: The Yoga of Distinction of the Three Guṇas

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   paraṁ bhūyaḥ pravakṣyāmi, jñānānāṁ jñānam uttamam;**

**     yaj jñātvā munayaḥ sarve, parāṁ siddhim ito gatāḥ.**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: I shall again (bhūyaḥ) declare (pravakṣyāmi) the supreme (param) \[knowledge\], the unsurpassed (uttamam) of all knowledges (jñānānām); having known (jñātvā) which (yat), all (sarve) *munis* have attained (gatāḥ) the supreme (parām) perfection and liberation (siddhim) from this world (itaḥ).

**[2](javascript:void(0))   idaṁ jñānam upāśritya, mama sādharmyam āgatāḥ;**

**     sarge 'pi nopajāyante, pralaye na vyathanti ca.**

Having relied upon (upāśritya) this (idam) knowledge (jñānam), they have entered into (āgatāḥ) My (mama) true Dharma / transcendent reality (sādharmyam); even (api) at the time of cosmic manifestation (sarge), they do not undergo rebirth (na upajāyante), nor (ca na) do they suffer, tremble, or experience distress (vyathanti) at the time of cosmic dissolution (pralaye).

**[3](javascript:void(0)) mama yonir mahad brahma, tasmin garbhaṁ dadhāmy aham; sambhavaḥ sarva-bhūtānāṁ, tato bhavati bhārata.**

My (mama) womb / primordial source (yoniḥ) is great Brahma (mahat brahma), into which (tasmin) I (aham) place (dadhāmi) the seed / embryo (garbham); from that (tataḥ), O descendant of Bharata (bhārata), arises (bhavati) the full manifestation (sambhavaḥ) of all living beings (sarva-bhūtānām).

**[4](javascript:void(0))   sarva-yoniṣu kaunteya, mūrtayaḥ sambhavanti yāḥ;**

**     tāsāṁ brahma mahad yonir, ahaṁ bīja-pradaḥ pitā.**

In all (sarva) wombs (yoniṣu), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), whatever (yāḥ) embodied forms (mūrtayaḥ) are born into existence (sambhavanti); of those forms (tāsām), the great Brahma (mahat brahma) is the womb / source (yoniḥ), and I (aham) am the seed-giving (bīja-pradaḥ) father (pitā).

**[5](javascript:void(0))   sattvaṁ rajas tama iti, guṇāḥ prakṛti-sambhavāḥ;**

**     nibadhnanti mahā-bāho, dehe dehinam avyayam.**

Sattva (Luminosity/ purity), raja (passion/ agitation), and tama (heaviness/darkness)---thus (iti) named---are the primary guṇas / qualities or attributes originated from (sambhavāḥ) prakṛti; they bind (nibadhnanti), O mighty-armed one (mahā-bāho), the imperishable embodied entity (dehinam avyayam) within the body (dehe).

**[6](javascript:void(0))   tatra sattvaṁ nirmalatvāt, prakāśakam anāmayam;**

**     sukha-saṇgena badhnāti, jñāna-saṇgena cānagha.**

There (tatra), *sattva*, due to freedom from defilement. (nirmalatvāt), is illuminating (prakāśakam) and free from disease or affliction (anāmayam); it binds (badhnāti) through attachment to pleasure and happiness (sukha-saṅgena) and attachment to knowledge (jñāna-saṅgena), O sinless one (anagha).

**[7](javascript:void(0))   rajo rāgātmakaṁ viddhi, tṛṣṇā-saṇga-samudbhavam;**

**     tan nibadhnāti kaunteya, karma-saṇgena dehinam.**

Know (viddhi) *rajas* to be the passion and desire of ātmā (rāgātmakam), arising from craving and attachment (tṛṣṇā-saṅga-samudbhavam); it firmly binds (tan nibadhnāti), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), the embodied entity (dehinam) through attachment to worldly action (karma-saṅgena).

**  **

**\**

**[8](javascript:void(0))   tamas tv ajñāna-jaṁ viddhi, mohanaṁ sarva-dehinām;**

**     pramādālasya-nidrābhis, tan nibadhnāti bhārata.**

*Tama*, however (tu), know (viddhi) to be born of ignorance (ajñāna-jam), delusional (mohanam) in all embodied beings (sarva-dehinām); it firmly binds (tan nibadhnāti), O descendant of Bharata (bhārata), through negligence and zeallessness (pramāda), laziness (ālasya), and sleep or torpor (nidrābhiḥ).

**[9](javascript:void(0))   sattvaṁ sukhe sañjayati, rajaḥ karmaṇi bhārata;**

**     jñānam āvṛtya tu tamaḥ, pramāde sañjayaty uta.**

*Sattva* binds the mind to pleasure and bliss (sattvaṁ sukhe sañjayati), O descendant of Bharata (bhārata), while *rajas* bind it to action (rajaḥ karmaṇi); but *tamas* (tu tamaḥ), having veiled and obscured knowledge (jñānam āvṛtya), binds the mind directly to negligence and heedlessness (pramāde sañjayaty uta).

**[10](javascript:void(0))   rajas tamaś cābhibhūya, sattvaṁ bhavati bhārata;**

**       rajaḥ sattvaṁ tamaś caiva, tamaḥ sattvaṁ rajas tathā.**

Having overcome (abhibhūya) *rajas* and *tama*, *sattva* arises (bhavati), O descendant of Bharata (bhārata); *raja* arises by prevailing over *sattva* and *tama*, and *tama* arises by prevailing over *sattva* and *raja* in like manner (tathā).

**[11](javascript:void(0))   sarva-dvāreṣu dehe 'smin, prakāśa upajāyate;**

**       jñānaṁ yadā tadā vidyād, vivṛddhaṁ sattvam ity uta.**

Through all the gates of this body \[sense-doors: eyes, ears, nose, tongue, skin, mind/*mana*\] (sarva-dvāreṣu dehe 'smin), the illumination of knowledge shines forth (prakāśa upajāyate jñānaṁ), at that time (yadā tadā) one should know (vidyāt) that *sattva* has grown predominant (vivṛddhaṁ sattvam iti uta).

**[12](javascript:void(0))   lobhaḥ pravṛttir ārambhaḥ, karmaṇām aśamaḥ spṛhā;**

**       rajasy etāni jāyante, vivṛddhe bharatarṣabha.**

Greed (lobhaḥ), continuous involvement in worldly affairs (pravṛttiḥ), the launching of undertaking after undertaking (ārambhaḥ karmaṇām), unceasing agitation and restlessness (aśamaḥ), and longing desire for external stimulants (spṛhā)---these arise (etāni jāyante), O bull among the Bharatas (bharatarṣabha), when *rajas* has grown predominant (vivṛddhe rajasi).

**[13](javascript:void(0))   aprakāśo 'pravṛttiś ca, pramādo moha eva ca;**

**       tamasy etāni jāyante, vivṛddhe kuru-nandana.**

Lack of discrimination or awareness (aprakāśaḥ), inertia and inactivity (apravṛttiḥ), negligence and heedlessness (pramādaḥ), and delusion or ignorance (mohaḥ)---these arise (etāni jāyante), O joy of the Kurus (kuru-nandana), when *tamas* has grown predominant (vivṛddhe tamasi).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   yadā sattve pravṛddhe tu, pralayaṁ yāti deha-bhṛt;**

**       tadottama-vidāṁ lokān, amalān pratipadyate.**

When, however (tu), an embodied soul (deha-bhṛt) meets death / passing away of the physical form (pralayaṁ yāti) while *sattva* is fully predominant (yadā sattve pravṛddhe), at that time (tadā) it reaches (pratipadyate) the stainless, pure realms (amalān lokān) of those who know the highest. (uttama-vidām).

**[15](javascript:void(0))   rajasi pralayaṁ gatvā, karma-saṇgiṣu jāyate;**

**       tathā pralīnas tamasi, mūḍha-yoniṣu jāyate.**

Meeting death / passing away of the physical form, while *rajas* is predominant (rajasi pralayaṁ gatvā), one is born among those attached to action / labor (karma-saṅgiṣu jāyate); likewise, dying and dissolving while *tamas* is predominant (tathā pralīnas tamasi), one is born in the wombs of the deluded and foolish (mūḍha-yoniṣu jāyate).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   karmaṇaḥ sukṛtasyāhuḥ, sāttvikaṁ nirmalaṁ phalam;**

**       rajasas tu phalaṁ duḥkham, ajñānaṁ tamasaḥ phalam.**

They say the fruit of good and wholesome action (karmaṇaḥ sukṛtasya āhuḥ) is pure and stainless, born of *sattva* (sāttvikaṁ nirmalaṁ phalam); but the fruit of *rajas* is dukkha (rajasaḥ tu phalaṁ duḥkham), and the fruit of *tamas* is ignorance and delusion (ajñānaṁ tamasaḥ phalam).

**[17](javascript:void(0))   sattvāt sañjāyate jñānaṁ, rajaso lobha eva ca;**

**       pramāda-mohau tamaso, bhavato 'jñānam eva ca.**

From *sattva* arises knowledge (sattvāt sañjāyate jñānaṁ); from *rajas* arises greed and craving (rajaso lobha eva ca); while from *tamas* arise negligence, confusion, (pramāda-mohau tamaso), and ignorance indeed (bhavato 'jñānam eva ca).

**[18](javascript:void(0))   ūrdhvaṁ gacchanti sattva-sthā, madhye tiṣṭhanti rājasāḥ;**

**       jaghanya-guṇa-vṛtta-sthā, adho gacchanti tāmasāḥ.**

Those who abide in *sattva* go upward to higher realms (ūrdhvaṁ gacchanti sattva-sthā); those dominated by *rajas* remain in the middle human realm (madhye tiṣṭhanti rājasāḥ); while those bound to the lowest function of the guṇas, submerged in *tamas*, sink downward to lower states (jaghanya-guṇa-vṛtta-sthā adho gacchanti tāmasāḥ).

**[19](javascript:void(0))   nānyaṁ guṇebhyaḥ kartāraṁ, yadā draṣṭānupaśyati;**

**       guṇebhyaś ca paraṁ vetti, mad-bhāvaṁ so 'dhigacchati.**

When (yadā) the seer / observer (draṣṭā) high-sees (anupaśyati) no (na) other (anyam) agent / doer (kartāram) than the *guṇas* (guṇebhyaḥ), and (ca) knows (vetti) that it is superior to and transcendent beyond (param) the *guṇas* (guṇebhyaḥ), he (saḥ) high-reaches (adhigacchati) my state of being/ manifest (mad-bhāvam).

**[20](javascript:void(0))   guṇān etān atītya trīn, dehī deha-samudbhavān;**

**       janma-mṛtyu-jarā-duḥkhair, vimukto 'mṛtam aśnute.**

Having gone beyond (atītya) these (etān) three (trīn) *guṇas* (guṇān), which give rise to the physical body (deha-samudbhavān), the embodied \[soul / spirit\] (dehī), liberated (vimuktaḥ) from birth (janma), death (mṛtyu), old age / decay (jarā), and *dukkha* (duḥkhaiḥ), experiences (aśnute) deathlessness / immortality (*amṛtam*).

**Commentary: Self Vs. Soul-Spirit**

I choose not to translate ātmā as self but as soul-spirit because the translation to self is shaped/influenced by modern psychology, it may refer to some idea and concept as "self", not necessary something real existence. In Sanskrit, actually there is a word "sva/ svá/ स्व" shall be translated as self. Definitely not ātmā shall be translated as "self". soul-spirit on the contrary is something concrete, of real existence. And there is slight difference between soul and spirit. Soul is the spirit went through the four layers of "elements" from Akasha to Wind element (energy wave), to Fire element (from spirit to soul), to Water element (life span), to Earth element (matter manifest) which gives rise to matter form.

***Arjuna uvāca***

**[21](javascript:void(0))   kair liṇgais trīn guṇān etān, atīto bhavati prabho;**

**       kim ācāraḥ kathaṁ caitāṁs, trīn guṇān ativartate.**

Arjuna said: By what (kaiḥ) marks / characteristics (liṅgaiḥ) is one who has gone beyond (atītaḥ) these (etān) three (trīn) *guṇas* (guṇān), O Prabho (One who Manifest Forth)? What (kim) is his conduct / behavior (ācāraḥ), and (ca) how (katham) does he overcome (ativartate) these (etān) three (trīn) *guṇas* (guṇān)?

***\***

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[22](javascript:void(0))   prakāśaṁ ca pravṛttiṁ ca, moham eva ca pāṇḍava;**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: Illumination / clarity shine forth / appear (prakāśam), activity / movement (pravṛttim), and (ca) delusion (moham) as well (eva ca), O Pāṇḍava (son of Pāṇḍu/(pale, white))---he does not (na) hate / feel aversion toward (dveṣṭi) \[them\] when they arise / operate (sampravṛttāni), nor (na) crave / long for (kāṅkṣati) \[them\] when they cease / withdraw (nivṛttāni).

Seated (āsīnaḥ) like one who is sitting above \[the world\] (udāsīna-vat), who (yaḥ) is not (na) disturbed (vicālyate) by the *guṇas* (guṇaiḥ); who (yaḥ) remains firmly established (avatiṣṭhati) thinking merely \"the *guṇas* (guṇāḥ) operate / act (vartante)\" (iti eva), and does not (na) waver / stir (iṅgate).

**[24](javascript:void(0))   sama-duḥkha-sukhaḥ sva-sthaḥ, sama-loṣṭāśma-kañcanaḥ;**

Sameness in *dukkha*/suffering and *sukha*/pleasure (sama-duḥkha-sukhaḥ), abiding in one's self (sva-sthaḥ), sameness for a lump of earth, a stone, and gold (sama-loṣṭāśma-kāñcanaḥ), the wise (dhīraḥ) to whom the pleasant/ agreeable and unpleasant/ disagreeable are equal (tulya-priyāpriyaḥ), and to whom blame and praise the ātma are equal (tulya-nindātma-saṁstutiḥ).

**[25](javascript:void(0))   mānāvamānayos tulyas, tulyo mitrāri-pakṣayoḥ;**

Equal / same in honor/pride and dishonor/disrespect (mānāvamānayoḥ), equal / same toward friend/ ally and enemy/ adversary (mitrāri-pakṣayoḥ), completely abandons all personal / egoistic initiatives (sarvārambha-parityāgī)---he (saḥ) is called (ucyate) one who has gone beyond the *guṇas* (guṇātītaḥ).

**[26](javascript:void(0))   māṁ ca yo 'vyabhicāreṇa, bhakti-yogena sevate;**

**       sa guṇān samatītyaitān, brahma-bhūyāya kalpate.**

And (ca) he who (yaḥ) serves (sevate) Me (mām) with unwavering/ constant (avyabhicāreṇa) yoga/union of devotion (bhakti-yogena)---he (saḥ), thoroughly going beyond (samatītya) these (etān) *guṇas* (guṇān), is qualified (kalpate) for becoming of Brahma (brahma-bhūyāya).

**[27](javascript:void(0))   brahmaṇo hi pratiṣṭhāham, amṛtasyāvyayasya ca;**

**       śāśvatasya ca dharmasya, sukhasyaikāntikasya ca.**

For (hi) I (aham) am the foundation (pratiṣṭhā) of Brahma (brahmaṇaḥ), and (ca) of the imperishable (avyayasya) deathlessness / immortality (amṛtasya), and (ca) of the eternal (śāśvatasya) *dharma* (dharmasya), and (ca) of the ultimate (aikāntikasya) bliss (sukhasya).

### Chapter 15 Puruṣa-Uttama Yoga: The Yoga of the Highest Puruṣa

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   ūrdhva-mūlam adhaḥ-śākham, aśvatthaṁ prāhur avyayam;**

**     chandāṁsi yasya parṇāni, yas taṁ veda sa veda-vit.**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: They call (prāhuḥ) the not-passing-away (avyayam) sacred fig tree / bodhi tree (aśvattham) having roots above (ūrdhva-mūlam) and branches below (adhaḥ-śākham); whose (yasya) leaves (parṇāni) are the Vedic hymns (chandāṁsi)---he who (yaḥ) knows (veda) that \[tree\] (tam), he (saḥ) is a knower of the Veda (veda-vit).

**Commentary**: **puruṣottama** (**puruṣa** + **uttama**)- The Supreme Person / Ultimate Being

- **puruṣa**: Person, spirit, conscious entity, primeval man. Etymologically derived from *puri* (in the city/dwelling of the body) + root *sad* / *śī* (dwelling/abiding within), or from root *pṝ* (to fill, pervade).

- **uttama**: Highest, supreme, paramount, unsurpassed. Superlative form derived from prefix *ud-* (up, out) + superlative suffix *-tama* -\> \"that which is highest above all.\"

**[2](javascript:void(0))   adhaś cordhvaṁ prasṛtās tasya śākhā, guṇa-pravṛddhā viṣaya-pravālāḥ; adhaś ca mūlāny anusantatāni, karmānubandhīni manuṣya-loke.**

Its (tasya) branches (śākhāḥ) are spread out (prasṛtāḥ) below (adhaḥ) and (ca) above (ūrdhvam), nourished by the *guṇas* (guṇa-pravṛddhāḥ), with sense-objects as tender sprouts (viṣaya-pravālāḥ); and (ca) secondary roots (mūlāni) are stretched out (anusantatāni) downward (adhaḥ), bound to consequence of action (karmānubandhīni) in the world of human beings (manuṣya-loke).

**[3](javascript:void(0))   na rūpam asyeha tathopalabhyate, nānto na cādir na ca sampratiṣṭhā; aśvattham enaṁ su-virūḍha-mūlam, asaṇga-śastreṇa dṛḍhena chittvā.**

Its (asya) form (rūpam) is not (na) perceived / comprehended (upalabhyate) here (iha) as such (tathā); nor (na) its end (antaḥ), nor (na ca) its origin (ādiḥ), nor (na ca) its firm foundation / support (sampratiṣṭhā); having cut down (chittvā) this (enam) firmly rooted (suvirūḍha-mūlam) *aśvattha* tree / sacred fig tree / bodhi tree \[world tree\] (aśvattham) with the strong (dṛḍhena) weapon of detachment / non-clinging (asaṅga-śastreṇa).

**  [4](javascript:void(0))   tataḥ padaṁ tat parimārgitavyaṁ, yasmin gatā na nivartanti bhūyaḥ; tam eva cādyaṁ puruṣaṁ prapadye, yataḥ pravṛttiḥ prasṛtā purāṇī.**

Then (tataḥ) that (tat) root / foundation (padam) is to be thoroughly searched (parimārgitavyam), those who have entered (gatāḥ) into which (yasmin) \[they\] do not (na) return \[rebirth\] (nivartanti) again (bhūyaḥ); \[thinking:\] \"I surrender unto (prapadye) that (tam) primal / original (ādyam) ***puruṣa*** indeed (eva), from whom (yataḥ) the ancient (purāṇī) lineage of manifestation / human lineage (pravṛttiḥ) originated (prasṛtā).

**  [5](javascript:void(0))   nirmāna-mohā jita-saṇga-doṣā, adhyātma-nityā vinivṛtta-kāmāḥ; dvandvair vimuktāḥ sukha-duḥkha-saṁjñair, gacchanty amūḍhāḥ padam avyayaṁ tat.**

Free from pride and delusion (nirmāna-mohāḥ), having overcome the attachment to fear and anger (jita-saṅga-doṣāḥ), constantly with the high ātma (adhyātma-nityāḥ), with sexual/ sensual desires completely turned away / ceased (vinivṛtta-kāmāḥ), liberated (vimuktāḥ) from the dualities (dvandvaiḥ) perceived as sukha / pleasure and duḥkha / suffering (sukha-duḥkha-saṁjñaiḥ), the undeluded ones (amūḍhāḥ) go to (gacchanti) that (tat) imperishable / not-passing-away foundation (padam avyayam).

**Commentary**: About **Wrong** **Liberation**

This verse presents the popular wrong interpretation of liberation consists merely of \"transcending dualities conceptually\" through a subtle shift in perspective, intellectual re-framing, or mere cognitive awakening, which is an incomplete and fundamentally flawed or distorted notion of true liberation. True liberation is not a psychological trick that redefines worldly suffering as neutral; it is the literal escape from this world (*loka*)---which is inherently *dukkha*---and the eventual transcendence to the *Nibbāna-Dhātu*, which is a realm of supreme, unconditioned *sukha*.

**1). The Fallacy of \"Conceptual Transcendence\"**

To treat *sukha* and *duḥkha* as mere mental designations (*saṁjñā*) to be overcome by \"changing one\'s mindset\" reduces liberation to a mere philosophical trick. Real liberation is not achieved by merely holding a realization that \"pleasure and pain are two sides of the same coin.\" Such conceptual shifts leave the individual still bound to the laws of birth, decay, and death. Liberation is not an awakening to simply a change in thought; it requires the actual detachment of the *citta* from all worldly phenomena, and its purification from *raga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear, anger, aversion and hatred) and *moha* (ignorance and delusion).

**2). The Separation of Body and Mind (*Citta*)**

For one who understands the authentic path of liberation, the experience of physical pleasure and pain is recognized precisely for what it is: a phenomenon occurring strictly at the physical body level.

- **Physical Sensations Remain at the Perimeter:** When an external stimulant produces sensory pleasure or bodily pain, the purified *citta* observes it without ownership. They belong to the world, belong to the body which will destined to decay and disolve---they are not our self, our true self is with our soul-spirit, *ātmā*, which must be protected from external worldly defilements through karma.

- **Guarding the Gate of the *Citta*:** The yogi practitioner does not allow externally driven sensations to seep from the physical body into the mind. External pleasure is refused entry so that it cannot ignite craving (*taṇhā*) or attachment; external pain is kept at the boundary so that it cannot generate aversion (*dosa*). The *citta* remains unpolluted, standing distinct from the physical frame.

**3). Externally Generated vs. Internally Cultivated Pleasure**

A critical distinction must be made regarding the nature and source of pleasure:

**External Worldly Stimuli**

- **Mechanism & Nature:** Generated through contact (*phassa*) of physical sense bases with external objects; belongs to *loka*.

- **Impact on the Citta & Worldly Bondage:** Generates craving, clinging, and worldly bondage *karma*. Must be restricted from seeping into the *citta*.

**Internal Samādhi (Concentration)**

- **Mechanism & Nature:** Generated directly within through *Samādhi*, entirely independent of external objects.

- **Impact on the Citta & Worldly Bondage:** Unconditioned by external sense-fields; serves as a wholesome instrument to transcend the world without generating karma of worldly entanglement.

**4). True Liberation vs. Worldly Entanglement**

The internal, wholesome pleasure of deep concentration (*samādhi*) is wholesome and independent of external worldly objects. It does not produce the corrupting *karma* of worldly attachment.

Ultimately, the individual who comprehends true liberation does not attempt to merge pain and pleasure into a conceptual unity or transcend through intellectual trick of so-called awakening. Instead, through the complete separation of body and mind, cessation of external worldly craving and clinging, and the total eradication of already existing *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, they make the *citta* to become completely disenchanted with all bodily sensations and worldly formations. By severing ties with these transient worldly phenomena, the *citta* leaves them behind, transcends the cycle of involuntary worldly reincarnation, and realizes the ultimate threshold---entering the *Nibbāna-Dhātu*, the deathless realm of unshakeable peace and absolute *sukha*.

**[6](javascript:void(0))   na tad bhāsayate sūryo, na śaśāṇko na pāvakaḥ;**

**     yad gatvā na nivartante, tad dhāma paramaṁ mama.**

The sun (sūryaḥ) does not (na) shine upon (bhāsayate) that (tat), nor (na) the moon (śaśāṅkaḥ), nor (na) fire (pāvakaḥ); having gone (gatvā) to which (yad) \[they\] do not (na) return (rebirth) (nivartante)---that (tat) is My (mama) supreme (paramam) *dhāma*.

**[7](javascript:void(0))   mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke, jīva-bhūtaḥ sanātanaḥ;**

**     manaḥ-ṣaṣṭhānīndriyāṇi, prakṛti-sthāni karṣati.**

An eternal (sanātanaḥ) portion (aṁśaḥ) belonging to Me indeed (mama eva), having become the soul (jīva-bhūtaḥ) in the realm of living beings with souls (jīva-loke), pulls along/ attracts (karṣati) the senses (indriyāṇi) with the mind-will as the sixth (manaḥ-ṣaṣṭhāni), which abide in (sthāni) ***prakṛti***.

**[8](javascript:void(0))   śarīraṁ yad avāpnoti, yac cāpy utkrāmatīśvaraḥ;**

**     gṛhītvaitāni saṁyāti, vāyur gandhān ivāśayāt.**

When (yat) the lord \[of the body\] (īśvaraḥ) acquires (avāpnoti) a physical body (śarīram), and also (ca api) when (yat) it departs / transmigrates (utkrāmati), taking (gṛhītvā) these \[senses\] (etāni), it moves onward / goes forth (saṁyāti), like (iva) the wind (vāyuḥ) \[carrying\] scents (gandhān) from their source (āśayāt).

**[9](javascript:void(0))   śrotraṁ cakṣuḥ sparśanaṁ ca, rasanaṁ ghrāṇameva ca;**

**     adhiṣṭhāya manaś cāyaṁ, viṣayān upasevate.**

Grounding itself in (adhiṣṭhāya) the ear (śrotram), the eye (cakṣuḥ), the sense of touch (sparśanam) and (ca), the tongue / sense of taste (rasanam), and (ca) the nose / sense of smell (ghrāṇam eva), as well as (ca) the mind-will (manaḥ), this \[embodied soul-spirit\] (ayam) experiences / consumes (upasevate) sense-objects (viṣayān).

**[10](javascript:void(0))   utkrāmantaṁ sthitaṁ vāpi, bhuñjānaṁ vā guṇānvitam;**

**       vimūḍhā nānupaśyanti, paśyanti jñāna-cakṣuṣaḥ.**

**[13](javascript:void(0))   gām āviśya ca bhūtāni, dhārayāmy aham ojasā;**

**       puṣṇāmi cauṣadhīḥ sarvāḥ, somo bhūtvā rasātmakaḥ.**

And (ca) entering (āviśya) the earth (gām), I (aham) sustain / support (dhārayāmi) all living beings (bhūtāni) by vital energy / potency (ojasā); and (ca) having become (bhūtvā) the cooling nourishment (*rasa*) originating from Soul-Spirit ātma (rasātmakaḥ), the soma, I nourish (puṣṇāmi) all (sarvāḥ) plants / vegetation (auṣadhīḥ).

**Commentary: Soma the Origin**

To trace **Soma** directly in the earliest layers of the *Rigveda*---stripped of both late ritualistic botanical abstractions and medieval lunar associations---we must look at how the veda verses themselves describe its nature, origin, and cosmic function.

In the *Rigveda*, particularly in **Mandala 9** (*Soma Pavamāna*), Soma is not a plant pressed into a bowl; it is the **primordial, cosmic life-fluid and divine vitality** that flows through the universe, directly connected to the divine principle.

**1). Soma as the Universal Fluid of Life and Divine Order (*Rasa* / *Ṛta*)**

In the oldest Rigvedic hymns, Soma is explicitly identified as the supreme **rasa**---the essential fluid or juice that sustains heaven, earth, and all physical forms.

**Rigveda 9.85.6:** *tiṣṭho varuṇasyoktheṣu, somo virājann arhati \| raso devānām uta martyānām\...*

\"Soma, shining in glory\... is the **rasa** (essence-fluid) of both the devas and mortals.\"

Here, Soma is the essence (*rasa*) shared by both devas and humans that permeates both the subtle and physical dimensions. It is the vital fluid without which physical forms wither and lose their vitality/lifespan.

**2). Soma Originates from High / Spirit (*Div-*) and Enters Matter**

The *Rigveda* repeatedly states that Soma originates in the highest realm (*Div*, the spiritual height) and descends into the earth, plants, and waters to give them life. It is not generated by the earth; it enters and animates the earth.

**Rigveda 9.86.25:** *divaḥ pīyūṣam prathamaṃ vyoṣaḥ, somam pavitram\...*

\"Soma, the pure, primordial nectar (*pīyūṣa*) of Heaven (*Div*)\...\"

**Rigveda 10.85.2:** *somena ādityā balinaḥ, somena pṛthivī mahī \| atho nakṣatrāṇām eṣām upasthe soma āhitaḥ \|\|*

\"By Soma the suns are made mighty; by Soma the great earth is held and supported; and Soma is placed in the lap of all these manifestations.\"

Soma is the higher vital force descending from the divine origin Soul-Spirit / *ātma* into physical manifestation to support and nourish physical earth (*gām āviśya*) and vegetation (*auṣadhīḥ*).

**3). The Distinction Between the Outer Plant and the True Soma**

The *Rigveda* itself explicitly warns against confusing physical plants or drinks with the actual, unmanifest Soma. The poets knew that what people pressed on earth was only an outer symbol or physical medium, whereas the real Soma is known only to the spiritual consciousness.

**Rigveda 10.85.3:** *somam manyate papivān, yat saṃpiṣanti oṣadhim \| somam yam brahmāṇo vidur, na tasyāśnāti kaścana \|\|*

**\"One thinks he has drunk Soma when they crush a plant; but of the true Soma which the *brahmāṇa* know, no one consumes by mouth.\"**

This single verse from the 10th Mandala confirms: **Soma is not a botanical drink:** The crushed plant (*oṣadhi*) is merely physical matter. **It is an essential life-fluid (*rasa*):** The true Soma is an unmanifest, divine essence received only by direct spiritual union.

Thus, when Bhagavad Gītā 15.13 states *somo bhūtvā rasātmakaḥ*, it is drawing directly on this original Rigvedic truth: the Divine manifests as **Soma**---the cooling, life-sustaining *rasa* originating from Soul-Spirit (*ātma*)---which flows into the earth to nourish and maintain all plant life.

The reason **Soma** targets **plants (*auṣadhīḥ*)** in verse 13---and **not living beings (*bhūtāni*)** directly---is because of the structural sequence of life, energy, and consumption laid out across **verses 13 and 14**:

I. Verse 13a: Divine Energy (Ojas) -\> Sustains All Living Beings (Bhūtāni)

II\. Verse 13b: Soma (Rasātmaka) -\> Nourishes Plants (Auṣadhīḥ)

III\. Verse 14: Digestive Fire (Vaiśvānara) -\> Digests Plant inside Living Body

**(1). Living Beings (*Bhūtāni*) and Plants (*Auṣadhīḥ*) Require Different Divine Mechanisms**

Living beings and plants operate on fundamentally different physiological principles:

**Living Beings (*Bhūtāni*) receive *Ojas* directly:** The first half of verse 13 says: *gām āviśya ca bhūtāni, dhārayāmy aham ojasā*. Humans and animals require direct energy and vital force (*ojas*) so they do not collapse or disintegrate on earth.

**Plants (*Auṣadhīḥ*) receive *Soma* directly:** Vegetation cannot digest solid food or hold active motor *ojas*. Plants grow by absorbing **fluid, sap, and cooling moisture (*rasa*)** from the earth and sky. *Soma* is that exact essential life-fluid infused with Soul-Spirit (*rasātmakaḥ*) that makes plants sap-rich, nutritious, and full of vitality.

**(2). Plants are the Transmitters of *Soma* to Humans**

Humans **do** receive *Soma*, but they receive it **indirectly through the plants they eat**. In Vedic physiology, humans cannot absorb raw *Soma* directly out of the soil or atmosphere. Plants act as the essential bridge:

1.  The Divine Source becomes *Soma* (the essence fluid) and enters the plant kingdom (*auṣadhīḥ*).

2.  The plants absorb this *Soma*, converting it into leaves, grains, fruits, and herbs filled with taste and nourishment (*rasa*).

3.  Humans consume these plants.

4.  As stated in the very next verse (**15.14**), the Divine Source then becomes the internal fire (*vaiśvānara*) in the human stomach to digest that plant food (*annam*): **ahaṁ vaiśvānaro bhūtvā prāṇināṁ deham āśritaḥ\...** *\"Becoming the internal fire residing in the bodies of living beings, I digest the four kinds of food\...\"*

If the Divine Source gave *Soma* directly to humans without going through plants, the physical chain of nutrition would break. **To living beings directly:** Cosmic order gives **Ojas** (vital grounding force) \[verse 13a\]. **To plants directly:** Cosmic order becomes **Soma** (essence fluid / sap) to grow food \[verse 13b\]. **Inside humans:** Cosmic order becomes **Vaiśvānara** (digestive fire) to break down that plant food into life energy \[verse 14\].

**[14](javascript:void(0))   ahaṁ vaiśvānaro bhūtvā, prāṇināṁ deham āśritaḥ;**

**       prāṇāpāna-samāyuktaḥ, pacāmy annaṁ catur-vidham.**

I (aham), having become (bhūtvā) nutrition common to all human beings (vaiśvānaraḥ), dwelling within (āśritaḥ) the physical body (deham) of living beings with vital breath *prāṇa* (prāṇinām), fully yoked / joined with (samāyuktaḥ) the in-breath and out-breath (prāṇāpāna), I digest (pacāmi) the types of (catur-vidham) food (annam).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   dvāv imau puruṣau loke, kṣaraś cākṣara eva ca;**

There are these two (dvau imau) *puruṣas* in the world (loke): the perishable (kṣaraḥ) and (ca) the imperishable (akṣaraḥ) indeed (eva ca); all (sarvāṇi) living beings / manifestations (bhūtāni) are the perishable (kṣaraḥ), while the underlying core / non-decaying principle (kūṭa-sthaḥ) is called (ucyate) the imperishable (akṣaraḥ).

**Commentary: kūṭa-stha The Immovable Foundation**

**The Metaphor of Kūṭa-stha: 1) Anvil (*Kūṭa*):** An anvil remains completely stationary and intact no matter how many heavy hammers strike upon it. Similarly, the *Akṣara* principle remains unaffected, motionless, and unchanged despite the constant flux of the material manifestations happening around or upon it.

**2) Summit / Peak:** *Kūṭa* also refers to a high mountain peak that stands far above the turbulent weather below.

*Akṣara* is the underlying, non-decaying principle that sustains the field of change without itself entering into flux. It represents the immutable core that stands unshakeable amid worldly conditions.

**[20](javascript:void(0))   āsurīṁ yonim āpannā, mūḍhā janmani janmani;**

**       mām aprāpyaiva kaunteya, tato yānty adhamāṁ gatim.**

Having entered into (āpannāḥ) ***āsurī*** womb (yonim), delusion-bound and utterly foolish (mūḍhāḥ) birth after birth (janmani janmani), failing to reach (aprāpya) Me (mām) indeed (eva), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), thereafter (tataḥ) they descend (yānti) to the lowest and vilest (adhamām) destination (gatim).

**[21](javascript:void(0))   tri-vidhaṁ narakasyedaṁ, dvāraṁ nāśanam ātmanaḥ;**

**       kāmaḥ krodhas tathā lobhas, tasmād etat trayaṁ tyajet.**

Three kinds (tri-vidham) is this (idam) gateway (dvāram) to the realm of torment / hell (narakasya), leading to the ruin and destruction (nāśanam) of ***ātma***: sex and worldly craving (kāmaḥ), anger and hostility (krodhaḥ), as well as (tathā) greed and covetousness (lobhaḥ); therefore (tasmāt), one should relinquish (tyajet) this (etat) triad (trayam).

**[22](javascript:void(0))   etair vimuktaḥ kaunteya, tamo-dvārais tribhir naraḥ;**

**       ācaraty ātmanaḥ śreyas, tato yāti parāṁ gatim.**

Liberated (vimuktaḥ) from these (etaiḥ) three (tribhiḥ) doors of darkness (tamo-dvāraiḥ), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), a human being (naraḥ) practices (ācarati) wholesomeness (śreyaḥ) of *ātma*; thereafter (tataḥ), one attains (yāti) the supreme (parām) destination (gatim).

**[23](javascript:void(0))   yaḥ śāstra-vidhim utsṛjya, vartate kāma-kārataḥ;**

**       na sa siddhim avāpnoti, na sukhaṁ na parāṁ gatim.**

He who (yaḥ), having abandoned (utsṛjya) the teaching and instruction of sacred text (śāstra-vidhim), acts (vartate) under sexual drive (kāma-kārataḥ), that person (saḥ) does not (na) reach (avāpnoti) perfection (siddhim), nor (na) bliss (sukham), nor (na) the supreme (parām) destination (gatim).

**[24](javascript:void(0))   tasmāc chāstraṁ pramāṇaṁ te, kāryākārya-vyavasthitau;**

**       jñātvā śāstra-vidhānoktaṁ, karma kartum ihārhasi.**

Therefore (tasmāt), the sacred teaching (śāstram) is your (te) standard of truth (pramāṇam) in determining what ought to be done and what ought not to be done (kāryākārya-vyavasthitau); having understood (jñātvā) what is declared by the sacred text (śāstra-vidhānoktam), you are fit (arhasi) to perform (kartum) action (karma) here in this world (iha).

**Commentary: The Gods Above, Humanity Below**

*Divine Conflict, Heavenly Politics, and the Human Being as a Pawn in Ancient Cosmology*

**Part I --- The Heavenly Order**

**What Is a "God" in Ancient Cosmology?**

Before asking why the gods fight, why they intervene in human affairs, or whether humanity becomes a pawn in their conflicts, we must first ask a more fundamental question: **what did ancient people mean by a "god"?**

The modern English word *god* can make ancient traditions appear much more uniform than they actually were. When we say "god," we may imagine an omnipotent creator, a supreme moral being, or simply a supernatural person. Ancient cosmologies often had no such single category. They could contain supreme gods and subordinate gods, gods associated with particular places or functions, ancestral divine beings, heavenly rulers, spirits, divine messengers, cosmic powers, and beings who stood somewhere between the divine and human worlds.

More importantly, **a god in ancient cosmology was often not merely an object of worship. A god belonged to a world.**

That world had structure. There could be generations of gods, divine families, councils, offices, rival factions, territories, servants, messengers, armies, and systems of authority. Some gods ruled heaven; others governed particular natural forces, cities, nations, or regions of the cosmos. Some could determine human destiny. Some could grant kingship. Some could punish humanity. Some could protect particular individuals or communities. And sometimes the gods themselves appeared to be subject to a greater order---fate, cosmic law, or an established divine decree.

The result is one of the most important features of ancient cosmology: **The divine realm was frequently imagined as a society above the human realm.**

Human beings did not simply live *in a universe containing gods*. They lived **under a universe governed by gods**.

And once this distinction is recognized, a new question emerges. If the gods constitute a society, then they can have politics. If they have politics, they can have disagreements. If they disagree, their conflicts can descend into the human world. This is where our investigation of **The Gods Above, Humanity Below** begins.

**The God as a Being of a Higher Order**

One of the simplest ways ancient cultures distinguished gods from humans was through **status and power**.

The Indo-Iranian vocabulary provides an illuminating example. The Vedic word *deva* and the Avestan *daēva* derive from an Indo-European root associated with brightness or shining. In the Vedic tradition, *deva* could refer broadly to divine beings, while *asura* could also designate powerful divine beings. In early Vedic material, *asura* was not originally equivalent to the later Indian concept of a demonic enemy of the gods. Encyclopaedia Iranica notes that early Vedic *asura* referred to "older gods," including Varuṇa and Mitra, and that the Ṛgveda preserves distinctions between *asuras who became devas* and *asuras who remained non-devas*.

This is already enough to complicate the familiar picture. There was not necessarily one simple division: gods on one side, demons on the other. Instead, the early material preserves **different classes and generations of divine beings**. That observation is especially important for our research because it means that the categories by which one divine group is distinguished from another may themselves have a history.

A being can be called *asura* in one period and later become associated with a very different category. A *deva* can be understood as a legitimate god in one tradition and condemned as a false divine being in another. Thus, when we ask, **"What is a god?"**, we must also ask: **Who has the authority to decide which beings are gods?**

**The God as Ruler**

The Mesopotamian evidence gives us an extraordinarily concrete answer. The ancient Mesopotamian world was populated by a large divine hierarchy. At its highest levels stood powerful gods such as Anu, Enlil, and Enki/Ea. The god Anu, for example, was understood as one of the oldest and highest-ranking gods. ORACC describes him as an authority figure, decision-maker, and progenitor who could allot functions to other gods and confer kingship upon earthly rulers. His decisions were regarded as unalterable.

This tells us something fundamental. A god was not simply a powerful invisible being. A god could possess **jurisdiction**. Anu could assign functions to other gods. He could confer kingship upon humans. He belonged to a hierarchy. And therefore the Mesopotamian divine world begins to resemble a kingdom. There is a ruler. There are subordinate powers. There are jurisdictions. There are decisions. And below the divine world stands the human political order.

This vertical relationship between heaven and earth is especially clear in the ancient conception of kingship. The human king does not necessarily possess authority merely because he has seized political power. His authority can be represented as something **given from heaven**.

The consequence is profound: **Earthly government becomes an extension of heavenly government.** The political structure of humanity reflects the political structure of the gods.

**Enlil and the Power to Determine Fate**

The Mesopotamian god Enlil makes this even clearer. ORACC describes Enlil as one of the supreme deities of the Mesopotamian pantheon. He is associated with decreeing fates, granting kingship, and exercising an authority whose commands could not ordinarily be altered. In the *Anzû* myth, he possesses the Tablet of Destinies, and possession of the tablet represents command over the world.

This is an important conception of divinity. The god is not merely someone who **knows** what will happen. The god can be someone who **determines** what happens. That distinction will become central later in this commentary. There is a vast difference between: **knowing destiny** and **decreeing destiny.** If a god merely knows the future, human agency might remain intact. But if a god determines the fate of kingdoms, kings, cities, and individuals, then humanity occupies a very different position. The human being becomes subject to a decision made at a higher level. This is one of the earliest places where the image of **humanity below** becomes more than metaphor.

**The Gods as a Divine Family**

Yet ancient cosmology does not always imagine heaven as an impersonal bureaucracy. The gods may also constitute a **family**. Anu, for example, is repeatedly described in Mesopotamian sources as the father of gods. Numerous deities are presented as his children in different traditions. He therefore occupies simultaneously the position of divine ancestor and supreme authority. This is significant because the divine realm begins to resemble an extended royal household. The gods can be: father and mother, sons and daughters, brothers and sisters, husbands and wives. And from these relationships emerge alliances and rivalries.

The same pattern becomes particularly vivid in Greek mythology, where the Olympian gods form a divine family centered on Zeus. The gods have relationships with one another, argue with one another, favor particular mortals, and intervene in human affairs.

Heaven, therefore, is not necessarily imagined as a realm of perfect agreement. It can look surprisingly familiar. There is family. There is authority. There is jealousy. There are loyalties. There are competing interests. There is political power. The difference is that the inhabitants of this political society possess powers vastly exceeding those of ordinary human beings.

**Olympus: A Court Above the Human World**

The *Iliad* gives us one of the clearest literary pictures of this divine society. At the beginning of Book 20, Zeus summons the gods to assembly. The scene is not presented as a collection of unrelated supernatural beings. They are summoned to **the house of Zeus**, where they take their seats in the divine dwelling. Poseidon asks Zeus why he has called them together and whether the subject concerns the Trojans and Achaeans, whose armies are preparing for battle. This small scene is extraordinarily important for our research. The gods have a **meeting**. They have a **place of assembly**. They have a **presiding authority**. They discuss **human events**.

And the subject of their deliberation is a war in which thousands of human beings are about to kill and be killed. The divine world is therefore not disconnected from human history. It stands above it and watches it. More importantly, the gods can intervene.

Zeus has called the gods together because the coming battle requires the divine order to determine how much intervention will occur. The gods are permitted to assist the side they favor, and the human battlefield becomes the arena in which the competing divine loyalties are expressed. This is one of the clearest ancient examples of what this commentary means by **heavenly politics**. There is a political event on earth. But above it is another political event.

The Trojan War is simultaneously: **a human war** and **a divine conflict.** The human participants fight on the plain beneath Troy. The gods deliberate above them.

**The Gods See More Than Humans See**

This vertical arrangement produces another important characteristic of ancient divinity: **superior knowledge.** The gods can see events from a perspective unavailable to ordinary humans.

In the *Iliad*, the gods can observe the human battlefield from Olympus, and the divine perspective allows the epic to move between what mortals experience and what the gods know or intend. Modern scholarship has specifically examined this relationship between divine viewing, divine knowledge, and divine control in Homeric epic.

The significance of this should not be underestimated. Imagine two people watching a battle. One is a soldier standing inside the battlefield. The other watches from a mountain. The soldier sees the enemy immediately before him. The observer sees the movement of the entire army. Now imagine that the observer also has the power to change what happens on the battlefield. The difference is no longer merely one of **perspective**. It becomes a difference of **power**.

This is precisely the kind of relationship ancient cosmology often establishes between gods and humans. The gods see from above. Humans live from below. The gods possess knowledge unavailable to humans. And sometimes the gods possess the power to alter the events that humans experience as their own lives.

**The God as One Who Can Enter Human History**
