**[19](javascript:void(0))   mūḍhā-grāheṇātmano yat, pīḍayā kriyate tapaḥ;**

**       parasyotsādanārthaṁ vā, tat tāmasam udāhṛtam.**

That primordial force (tapaḥ yat) which is undertaken (kriyate) based on a foolish / deluded notion (mūḍha-grāheṇa), through torture the *ātma* (pīḍayā), or for the purpose of harming another (parasya utsādanārthaṁ vā)---that (tat) is declared to be *tāmasa* (udāhṛtam).

**[20](javascript:void(0))   dātavyam iti yad dānaṁ, dīyate 'nupakārine;**

**       deśe kāle ca pātre ca, tad dānaṁ sāttvikaṁ smṛtam.**

That giving/ charity act (yat dānam) which is commited (dīyate) with such: \"it ought to be given / a duty to give\" (dātavyam iti), to one who can offer no return or benefit (anupakāriṇe), at the proper place (deśe), at the proper time (kāle), and to a worthy recipient (pātre ca)---that giving/ charity (tat dānam) is declared to be *sāttvika* (smṛtam).

**[21](javascript:void(0))   yat tu pratyupakārārthaṁ, phalam uddiśya vā punaḥ;**

**       dīyate ca parikliṣṭaṁ, tad dānaṁ rājasaṁ smṛtam.**

But that (yat tu) giving / offering (dānam) which is commited (dīyate) for the purpose of a return favor or reciprocal service (pratyupakārārthaṁ), or again (vā punaḥ) expecting a reward or fruit (phalam uddiśya), and (ca) is given reluctantly or with distress (parikliṣṭaṁ)---that giving / offering (tat dānam) is declared to be *rājasa* (smṛtam).

**[22](javascript:void(0))   adeśa-kāle yad dānam, apātrebhyaś ca dīyate;**

**       asat-kṛtam avajñātaṁ, tat tāmasam udāhṛtam.**

That giving / charity (yat dānam) which is commited (dīyate) at an improper place and time (adeśa-kāle), to unworthy or unfit recipients (apātrebhyaḥ ca), without respect (asat-kṛtam), and with contempt or disdain (avajñātaṁ)---that giving / charity (tat dānam) is declared to be *tāmasa* (udāhṛtam).

**[23](javascript:void(0))   oṁ tat sad iti nirdeśo, brahmaṇas tri-vidhaḥ smṛtaḥ;**

**       brāhmaṇās tena vedāś ca, yajñāś ca vihitāh purā.**

\"Oṁ Tat Sat \[Oṁ that Truth\]\" (oṁ tat sad iti)---thus (iti) is remembered as (smṛtaḥ) the threefold (tri-vidhaḥ) designation (nirdeśaḥ) of *brahmaṇa*. By this (tena), the *brāhmaṇā*, and the vedas, and the sacrifice / offerings (yajñāḥ ca) were established (vihitāḥ) in ancient times (purā).

**[24](javascript:void(0))   tasmād om ity udāhṛtya, yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-kriyāḥ;**

**       pravartante vidhānoktāḥ, satataṁ brahma-vādinām.**

Therefore (tasmāt), chanting \"Oṁ\" (om iti udāhṛtya), the acts of sacrifice, charity, and primordial force / *tapas* (yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-kriyāḥ) as prescribed by scripture (vidāna-uktāḥ) are always initiated (satataṁ pravartante) by the expounders/teachers of Brahma (brahma-vādinām).

**[25](javascript:void(0))   tad ity anabhisandhāya, phalaṁ yajña-tapaḥ-kriyāḥ;**

**       dāna-kriyāś ca vividhāḥ, kriyante mokṣa-kāṇkṣibhiḥ.**

\"Tat\" (tat iti), without aiming at or desiring (anabhisandhāya) reward or fruit (phalam), various acts of sacrifice and primordial force / *tapas* (yajña-tapaḥ-kriyāḥ), as well as (ca) diverse acts of charity (dāna-kriyāś ca vividhāḥ), are performed (kriyante) by those who long for (kāṅkṣibhiḥ) liberation / mokṣa.

**[26](javascript:void(0))   sad-bhāve sādhu-bhāve ca, sad ity etat prayujyate;**

**       praśaste karmaṇi tathā, sac-chabdaḥ pārtha yujyate.**

In the state (bhāve) of "Sat", and in the state (bhāve ca) of sādhu (goodness / virtue), this word \"Sat\" (sad iti etat) is employed (prayujyate); likewise (tathā), O son of Pṛthā (pārtha), the word \"Sat\" (sat-śabdaḥ) is applied (yujyate) to an auspicious / praiseworthy action (praśaste karmaṇi).

**[27](javascript:void(0))   yajñe tapasi dāne ca, sthitiḥ sad iti cocyate;**

**       karma caiva tad-arthīyaṁ, sad ity evābhidhīyate.**

Abiding (sthitiḥ) in sacrifice, primordial force / tapas, and charity (yajñe tapasi dāne ca) is also called \"Sat\" (sat iti ca ucyate); and indeed (ca eva) action (karma) performed for the sake of That (tad-arthīyam) is likewise designated as \"Sat\" (sat iti eva abhidhīyate).

**[28](javascript:void(0))   aśraddhayā hutaṁ dattaṁ, tapas taptaṁ kṛtaṁ ca yat;**

**       asad ity ucyate pārtha, na ca tat pretya no iha.**

Whatever sacrifice is offered (hutaṁ), whatever gift/charity is given (dattaṁ), whatever primordial force / tapas is practiced (tapas taptaṁ), and whatever action is performed (kṛtaṁ ca yat) without faith (aśraddhayā)---that is declared as \"Asat\" (asad iti ucyate), O son of Pṛthā (pārtha); it is of no value hereafter / in the next world (na ca tat pretya), nor here in this life (no iha).

### Chapter 18 Mokṣa Sannyāsa Yoga: The Yoga of Liberation Through Renunciation

***Arjuna uvāca***

**[1](javascript:void(0))   saṁnyāsasya mahā-bāho, tattvam icchāmi veditum;**

**     tyāgasya ca hṛṣīkeśa, pṛthak keśi-niṣūdana.**

Arjuna said: O mighty-armed one (mahā-bāho), I wish (icchāmi) to know (veditum) the *tattva* of renunciation (saṁnyāsasya), and (ca) \[of\] relinquishment (tyāgasya), distinctly (pṛthak), O lord of the senses (hṛṣīkeśa), O slayer of the Keśin demon (keśi-niṣūdana).

***Śrī-Bhagavān uvāca***

**[2](javascript:void(0))   kāmyānāṁ karmaṇāṁ nyāsaṁ, saṁnyāsaṁ kavayo viduḥ;**

**     sarva-karma-phala-tyāgaṁ, prāhus tyāgaṁ vicakṣaṇāḥ.**

Bhagava Kṛṣṇa said: The seers (kavayo) know (viduḥ) the renouncing / letting go (nyāsaṁ) of actions driven by desire (kāmyānāṁ karmaṇāṁ) as true renunciation (saṁnyāsaṁ); the clear-sighted (vicakṣaṇāḥ) speak of (prāhuḥ) the relinquishment (tyāgaṁ) of the fruits of all actions (sarva-karma-phala-tyāgaṁ) as true relinquishment (tyāgaṁ).

**[3](javascript:void(0))   tyājyaṁ doṣa-vad ity eke, karma prāhur manīṣiṇaḥ;**

**     yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-karma, na tyājyam iti cāpare.**

Some (eke) thinkers (manīṣiṇaḥ) speak (prāhuḥ) that (iti) action (karma) is to be relinquished (tyājyaṁ) as leading to doṣa \[from worldly craving and attachment\] (doṣa-vat); and (ca) others (apare) \[declare\] that (iti) action of sacrifice, giving/charity, and primordial force/ tapas (yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-karma) is not to be relinquished (na tyājyam).

**[4](javascript:void(0))   niścayaṁ śṛṇu me tatra, tyāge bharata-sattama;**

**     tyāgo hi puruṣa-vyāghra, tri-vidhaḥ samprakīrtitaḥ.**

Hear (śṛṇu) from me (me) the verdict (niścayaṁ) regarding that (tatra) relinquishment (tyāge), O best of the Bharatas (bharata-sattama); for (hi) relinquishment (tyāgo), O tiger among men (puruṣa-vyāghra), is clearly declared (samprakīrtitaḥ) as threefold (tri-vidhaḥ).

**[5](javascript:void(0))   yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-karma, na tyājyaṁ kāryam eva tat;**

**     yajño dānaṁ tapaś caiva, pāvanāni manīṣiṇām.**

Action of sacrifice, giving/charity, and primordial force / tapas (yajña-dāna-tapaḥ-karma) is not to be abandoned (na tyājyaṁ); indeed (eva), that (tat) must be performed (kāryam); for sacrifice (yajño), giving / charity(dānaṁ), and (ca) primordial force/ tapas (tapaḥ) indeed (eva) are purifiers (pāvanāni) of the thinkers (manīṣiṇām).

**[6](javascript:void(0))   etāny api tu karmāṇi, saṇgaṁ tyaktvā phalāni ca;**

**     kartavyānīti me pārtha, niścitaṁ matam uttamam.**

However (tu), even (api) these (etāni) actions (karmāṇi) must be performed (kartavyāni) relinquishing (tyaktvā) attachment (saṅgaṁ) and (ca) fruits (phalāni); this (iti) is my (me) definitive (niścitaṁ) and supreme (uttamam) judgment (matam), O son of Pṛthā (pārtha).

**[7](javascript:void(0))   niyatasya tu saṁnyāsaḥ, karmaṇo nopapadyate;**

**     mohāt tasya parityāgas, tāmasaḥ parikīrtitaḥ.**

However (tu), the renunciation (saṁnyāsaḥ) of obligatory (niyatasya) action (karmaṇaḥ) is not appropriate (na upapadyate); thetotal relinquishment (parityāgaḥ) of that \[obligatory action\] (tasya) out of ignorance (mohāt) is declared (parikīrtitaḥ) as *tāmasa*.

**[8](javascript:void(0))   duḥkham ity eva yat karma, kāya-kleśa-bhayāt tyajet;**

**     sa kṛtvā rājasaṁ tyāgaṁ, naiva tyāga-phalaṁ labhet.**

Whatever (yat) action (karma) one gives up (tyajet) out of bodily discomfort (kāya-kleśa-bhayāt), thinking (iti) indeed (eva) \"it is painful\" (duḥkham); having performed (sa kṛtvā) that relinquishment (tyāgaṁ) of *rājasa*, he certainly does not (na eva) attain (labhet) the fruit of relinquishment (tyāga-phalaṁ).

**[9](javascript:void(0))   kāryam ity eva yat karma, niyataṁ kriyate 'rjuna;**

**     saṇgaṁ tyaktvā phalaṁ caiva, sa tyāgaḥ sāttviko mataḥ.**

Whatever (yat) obligatory (niyataṁ) action (karma) is performed (kriyate) thinking (iti) indeed (eva) \"it must be performed\" (kāryam), O Arjuna ('rjuna); relinquishing (tyaktvā) attachment (saṅgaṁ) and (ca) fruit (phalaṁ) indeed (eva); that (sa) relinquishment (tyāgaḥ) is held to be (mataḥ) *sāttvika*.

**[10](javascript:void(0))   na dveṣṭy akuśalaṁ karma, kuśale nānuṣajjate;**

**       tyāgī sattva-samāviṣṭo, medhāvī chinna-saṁśayaḥ.**

He does not hate or feel aversion toward (na dveṣṭi) unwholesome / harmful (akuśalaṁ) action (karma), nor (na) does he cling or become attached to (anuṣajjate) wholesome / good (kuśale) \[action\]; he is a true relinquisher (tyāgī), completely entered (samāviṣṭaḥ) sattva, wise (medhāvī), with all doubts cut off (chinna-saṁśayaḥ).

**Commentary**: **Gautama's Four Right Efforts**

In the Pāḷi Canon (e.g., *Samma-padhāna Sutta*, AN 4.13, and *Sammādiṭṭhi Sutta*, MN 9), Gautama Buddha defines *Sammā-vāyāma* (Right Effort) as a deliberate, systematic practice of discrimination and mental cultivation:

1.  **Prevention (*Saṁvara-padhāna*):** The effort to prevent unarisen unwholesome mentalities (*akusala dhamma*) from arising.

2.  **Abandonment (*Pahāna-padhāna*):** The effort to abandon unwholesome mentalities (*akusala dhamma*) that have already arisen.

3.  **Cultivation (*Bhāvanā-padhāna*):** The effort to give rise to unarisen wholesome mentalities (*kusala dhamma*).

4.  **Maintenance (*Anurakkhaṇā-padhāna*):** The effort to maintain, increase, and perfect wholesome mentalities (*kusala dhamma*) that have already arisen.

**Comparative Analysis: BG 18.10 vs. The Four Right Efforts**

**Relation to Akuśala (Unwholesome)**

- **Bhagavad Gītā 18.10 (*Sāttvika Tyāga*):** Equanimity / Non-aversion --- Does not hate or shrink from (*na dveṣṭi*) *akuśala* action.

- **Gautama Buddha\'s Four Right Efforts (*Sammā-Padhāna*):** Active Elimination --- Actively prevents and abandons (*pahāna*) all *akusala* mental states and actions.

**Relation to Kuśala (Wholesome)**

- **Bhagavad Gītā 18.10 (*Sāttvika Tyāga*):** Non-attachment --- Performs it without personal clinging (*nānuṣajjate*) or ownership of fruit.

- **Gautama Buddha\'s Four Right Efforts (*Sammā-Padhāna*):** Active Cultivation --- Actively generates (*bhāvanā*) more and stabilizes (*anurakkhaṇā*) *kusala* mental states and actions.

**Underlying Premise**

- **Bhagavad Gītā 18.10 (*Sāttvika Tyāga*):** Duty (*Svadharma*) & Non-agency --- Action is dictated by one\'s prescribed worldly duty and *guṇas*. The goal is transcending personal agency while completing necessary tasks. **Ultimately:** resting in purity (*sattva*) and abandoning fruit clears doubts (*chinna-saṁśayaḥ*).

- **Gautama Buddha\'s Four Right Efforts (*Sammā-Padhāna*):** Volition (*Cetanā*) & Mind --- Action (*kamma*) is rooted in intention. *Akusala* is driven by *raga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear, anger, aversion, and hate), and *moha* (ignorance and delusion), and at the same to avoid unwholesome karma seeds left imprints in the mind-heart which may eventually creates worldly bondage---it must be completely eradicated, never merely endured. **Ultimately:** Right Effort systematically cleanses the mind (*citta*) of worldly bondage manifest as being called defilements (*kilesas*) of the mind-heart: *raga*, *dosa* and *moha*, and eventually by stopping new defilements to leave karma seeds in the mind-heart (*nibbida*), and by cleansing those defilements already in the mind-heart (*viraga*), the mind-heart becomes fully purified and is able to transcend (liberated from) the world completely (*vimutti*) because all the worldly bondage is gone.

**Key Points of Divergence**

**1). Tolerating vs. Eradicating *Akuśala***

- **In the Gītā:** *Akuśala* here largely denotes external, unpleasant, unwholesome or socially difficult duties (e.g., fighting in a war, killing, handling painful tasks). The Gītā instructs Arjuna not to feel emotional aversion (*dveṣa*) toward such unpleasant tasks, as long as they align with *svadharma*.

- **In the Suttas:** *Akusala* refers strictly to actions born of worldly craving and attachment (*raga*), fear, anger, ill-will and hate (*dosa*), and ignorance & delusion (*moha*). The Buddha explicitly states that an unwholesome act, such as the fighting and killing that Arjuna is persuaded to commit, or mental state must **never** be tolerated, adopted, or passed off with passive neutrality. It must be recognized, restrained, and abandoned. This is non-negotiable.

**2). The Functional Role of *Kuśala***

- **In the Gītā:** Clinging to good deeds (*kuśale\... anuṣajjate*) creates karmic bondage (*puṇya* binding the soul to *saṁsāra* just as *pāpa* does). Thus, the *tyāgī* lets go of attachment to the "wholesome" and \"good\" actions or reward of the actions.

- **In the Suttas:** *Kusala* states and actions (such as mindfulness, Samadhi, wisdom, and the Seven Factors of Awakening) are the essential merits used to cross the river of *saṁsāra*. Concentration/ Samadhi including *jhana* and attainments are among the best merits that align humans with cosmic Brahma values, and Nibbana basically is Brahma-Nirvana that use brahma heavenly realms as step stones (pure abode brahma heavens). All these good merits eventually lead to Arahantship. That is why *kusala* is actively cultivated and guarded right up to the final exit from this world to enter the deathless realm of Nibbana. There is no such thing as "clinging" to *jhana* or its attainments is a bad thing. This is misinformation from those forces anti-Gautama's liberation path. Every yogi practitioner walking on Gautama's authentic path of liberation need to use discernment to distinguish because such misinformation is everywhere.

**3). Equanimity vs. Indiscriminate Passivity**

In the early Pāḷi Canon, *upekkha* (transcendental release, wrongly translated as equanimity, which is *sama*) is never defined as indifference toward whether an action is wholesome or unwholesome. True *upekkha* arises because *akusala* has been abandoned and *kusala* has been perfectly established---not by looking at *akusala* and *kusala* with equal indifference during the path of practice. This is another misinformation drama in this world.

Some people always talk about attachment (*rāga*/*chanda*) as a bad thing. They don't understand that in Gautama's true teaching, it is worldly craving and attachment that is a bad thing---not \"attaching\" to wholesomeness, which is always a good thing and always needs to be upheld, even as an *arahant*.

It is clear that Gautama Buddha\'s Four Right Efforts demand an uncompromising, active discrimination: unwholesome mental states and actions (*akusala*) are to be relentlessly abandoned, and wholesome mental states and actions (*kusala*) are to be systematically brought to full development. The definition of wholesome and unwholesome is clear: whatever leads to decreasing the bondage with the world is wholesome; otherwise, it is unwholesome. This is subjective based on this liberation from the world ideology. This bondage is clearly detailed as:

- ***Rāga*:** Worldly craving and attachment.

- ***Dosa*:** Fear and anger, aversion and hate. We have to understand that fear and anger are very necessary to survive in this world. When humans meet a powerful adversary, fear arises---they run and they survive. Anger is the same; anger empowers the person. It draws energy for the body and mind, not just from this physical dimension, but from higher dimensions as well, to conquer other competitors or associates in a group. So the teaching of reducing *dosa* is to make the person less able to survive in this world. The same applies to the entire *Vinaya/ Sīla* system: the progression from 5 precepts, to 8 precepts, to 10 precepts, to the full precepts of a *bhikkhu* and *bhikkhunī* is clearly a pulling away of the mind from the world, to make the renunciant less able to live and even survive in this world. That is the point. To liberate from the world is the only practice passed down from our Gautama Buddha.

- ***Moha*:** The ignorance and delusion of not knowing this renouncing of the world as the solution to the problem of aging and death in this world.

**[11](javascript:void(0))   na hi deha-bhṛtā śakyaṁ, tyaktuṁ karmāṇy aśeṣataḥ;**

**       yas tu karma-phala-tyāgī, sa tyāgīty abhidhīyate.**

For (**hi**) it is not (**na**) possible (**śakyaṁ**) for an embodied being \[a bearer/holder of a body\] (**deha-bhṛtā**) to completely (**aśeṣataḥ**) abandon (**tyaktuṁ**) actions (**karmāṇi**); but (**tu**) he who (**yaḥ**) is a renouncer of the fruits of action (**karma-phala-tyāgī**), he (**saḥ**) is called (**abhidhīyate**) a true renouncer (**tyāgī**).

**Commentary**: **The Misunderstanding of \"Abandoning All Action\"**

A common error in worldly thinking---and presented here in BG 18.11---is the assumption that total renunciation requires the complete, physical cessation of *all* actions (*karmāṇi aśeṣataḥ*). Because an embodied being (*deha-bhṛt, deha*: the body, *bhṛt:* the bearer, the soul-spirit ***ātmā***) cannot cease physical bodily processes, this view concludes that complete abandonment of action is impossible, and therefore one must simply remain engaged in worldly duties while surrendering the outcome (*karma-phala-tyāgī*).

However, in Gautama the Supreme Arahant\'s ancient path to the deathless realm of *Nibbāna*, true renunciation is not the blind, blanket abandonment of *every* action indiscriminately. Rather, it is the deliberate, uncompromising abandonment of **worldly actions**---those actions, intentions, and mental states that lead to or increase bondage with the world (*loka*).

**Worldly Actions vs. Path Actions**

Action (*kamma*) is rooted in volition (*cetanā*). The crucial distinction lies in whether an action binds the *citta* to the world or pulls it away from the world:

1.  **Worldly Actions (*Akusala* / World-Bound):** Actions driven by *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, or actions aimed at securing a place, status, or survival within the world. These reinforce the laws of cause and effect, keep the *citta* entangled in the world, and produce *dukkha*. These are the actions that must be relentlessly abandoned.

2.  **Path Actions (Actions for Transcendence):** There are many essential actions that do **not** lead to worldly bondage. The practice of *Sīla*, the cultivation of Concentration (*Samadhi*), and the practice of *Paññā* are active processes, yet they do not bind the mind to the world.

**The Joy of Concentration (*Samadhi*):** A primary example of non-binding action is the cultivation of Concentration (*Samadhi*): The joy, rapture, and transcendental knowledge generated within *Samadhi* (such as the *jhānas*) are entirely **internal**. They are not derived from the external world, nor are they dependent on worldly sensory objects. Because this internal cultivation does not feed upon the external world, it does not create worldly bondage. Instead, it systematically starves the underlying tendencies of *rāga*, *dosa*, and *moha*, pulling the *citta* further and further away from worldly dependencies.

**[12](javascript:void(0))   amiṣṭam iṣṭaṁ miśraṁ ca, tri-vidhaṁ karmaṇaḥ phalam;**

**       bhavaty atyāgināṁ pretya, na tu saṁnyāsināṁ kvacit.**

Unpleasant (**aniṣṭam**), pleasant (**iṣṭam**), and (**ca**) mixed (**miśraṁ**) is the threefold (**tri-vidhaṁ**) fruit (**phalam**) of action (**karmaṇaḥ**); it accrues (**bhavati**) after death (**pretya**) for non-renouncers (**atyāginām**), but (**tu**) not (**na**) for true renouncers (**saṁnyāsinām**) at any time (**kvacit**).

**Commentary: The Path of True Purification**

**The Theoretical Ideal vs. Practical Reality**

In verse 18.12 of the Bhagavad Gītā, a profound psychological formula is presented: if one simply disconnects the physical execution of an action (**karma**) from its emotional and karmic outcomes (**karma-phala**), no karmic residue (**phalam**) can attach to the doer. On a purely theoretical level, the equation is seamless. However, when applied to real-world conditions, this standard demands a level of psychological detachment that is nearly impossible for an unpurified mind to sustain.

Consider Arjuna on the battlefield of Kurukṣetra. He is instructed to engage in direct, lethal combat---to slay his kinsmen, teachers, and elders under the rubric of his *ksatriya-dharma*. Krishna taught him that as long as he remains internally untainted, separating the act of killing from any personal preference, malice, or desire for the pleasant (**iṣṭam**) or unpleasant (**aniṣṭam**) results of killing, he remains stainless. Yet, in the crucible of real action, the human mind cannot easily perform severe, destructive acts while maintaining absolute purity of intention. Deep within the subtle mental continuum, latent tendencies, subtle aversion (**dosa**), and delusion (**moha**) inevitably contaminate the act. For an ordinary person, trying to remain unaffected while performing inherently violent actions such as killings is not a realistic path to liberation; it risks becoming a rationalization for unwholesome deeds.

**The Practical Methodology of Mind Purification**

When severe unwholesome actions have been committed, true liberation cannot be achieved through a mere cognitive shift in attitude during the act. It requires a systematic, thorough methodology for purifying the mind\'s latent karmic seeds (**saṁskāra**) from such action/ karma.

This contrast is vividly illustrated in the Pali Canon by the account of Aṅgulimāla. Having committed atrocious acts of violence, killing scores of people, he did not simply \"detach\" from his ongoing actions. Upon encountering Gautama the supreme Arahant, his entire trajectory shifted through genuine repentance and complete cessation from worldly bondage:

- **Sīla (Bhikkhu Discipline):** He entered the Order as a **bhikkhu**, adopting the **Bhikkhu** **Vinaya** and strict disenchantment (*nibbida*) and dispassion (*viraga*) conducts to immediately halt the generation of new unwholesome actions and start to cleanse the latent karma seeds of past unwholesome actions.

- **Samādhi (Concentration):** Through intense mental discipline and one-pointedness concentration, he effectively induced the primordial spiritual force *tapas* to cleanse the latent karma seeds from past unwholesome actions. **Tapas** is the intense, burning spiritual force generated through ascetic austere meditative yogi practice---to burn through and purify the heavy latent karmic seeds of his past killings.

- **Paññā (Liberation Wisdom):** Guided by direct insight into the nature of worldly existence, he completely eradicated all remaining worldly bondage of **rāga** (attachment), **dosa** (aversion), and **moha** (delusion), and progressively into worldly disenchantment (*nibbida*), fading of worldly passion (*viraga*), and liberated from the world (*vimutti*).

Through this rigorous training system of **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, and **Paññā**, Aṅgulimāla completely severed his ties with the world (**loka**). Having destroyed all worldly bondage, he achieved the state of an Arahant---fully purified and ready to transcend this world and universe.

Separating an action from its fruit in everyday life sounds elegant in theory, but in practice, it is exceptionally difficult and fraught with spiritual risk. For anyone bound by the mechanics of cause and effect, true freedom from karmic consequence demands a thorough, systematic purification of the mind. Liberation is not earned by redefining one\'s relationship to unwholesome action while performing it, but by cultivating radical **Sīla**, **Samādhi**, and **Paññā** to completely transcend the world and enter into the real home for mankind, the deathless realm of nibbana.

**[13](javascript:void(0))   pañcaitāni mahā-bāho, kāraṇāni nibodha me;**

**       sāṇkhye kṛtānte proktāni, siddhaye sarva-karmaṇām.**

O mighty-armed one (**mahā-bāho**), learn (**nibodha**) from me (**me**) these (**etāni**) five (**pañca**) causes (**kāraṇāni**), declared (**proktāni**) in the conclusive system (**kṛtānte**) of Sāṅkhya (**sāṅkhye**), for the perfection (**siddhaye**) of all actions (**sarva-karmaṇām**).

**[14](javascript:void(0))   adhiṣṭhānaṁ tathā kartā, karaṇaṁ ca pṛthag-vidham;**

**       vividhāś ca pṛthak ceṣṭā, daivaṁ caivātra pañcamam.**

The (1) physical body (**adhiṣṭhānaṁ**), as well as (**tathā**) the (2) agent/ doer (**kartā**), and (**ca**) the various kinds (**pṛthag-vidham**) of (3) doers \[organs of perception and action (senses, mind-will, hands, etc.)\] (**karaṇaṁ**); and (**ca**) the various (**vividhāḥ**) different (**pṛthak**) (4) efforts and functions *prāṇa* (**ceṣṭāḥ**), and (**ca**) (5) **daiva** as well (**eva**) here (**atra**) is the fifth (**pañcamam**).

**Commentary: Analysis of the 5 Causes of Action (*Pañca Kāraṇāni*)**

**The 5 Causes of Action (*Pañca Kāraṇāni*) Integrated Model**

**Unconditioned Realm (Beyond / Non-Doer)**

- **True Self / Soul-Spirit (*Ātman* / Silent Witness):** The pure observer standing outside and beyond the mechanical apparatus of action.

**Worldly Realm (*Loka*) / Conditioned Existence**

**(1). Adhiṣṭhāna:** Physical body.

**(2). Kartā:** Empirical ego, \"I-Maker\" (*ahaṅkāra*), and worldly identity.

**(3). Karaṇa:** Subconscious mind, body memory, and sensory faculties.

**(4). Ceṣṭā:** Vital energies (*prāṇa*) and physical exertions.

**(5). Daiva:** Celestial Devas, subtle divine influence, and direct possession.

**Breakdown of the 5 Factors:**

**1). Adhiṣṭhāna --- The Physical Seat & Biological Substrate**

- **Classical Definition:** The physical body, the base or platform where all material actions reside.

- **Modern Integration:** The hardware---the anatomical structure, skeletal frame, neuromuscular system, and physical environment that provides the physical locus for action to take place.

**2). Kartā --- The Empirical Ego / Constructed Worldly Identity / False Self**

- **Classical Definition:** The *Ahaṅkāra* (\"I-maker\") that is the true owner of action (*\"I am doing this\"*).

- **Modern psychology:** The psychological **Ego**---a superficial narrative construct synthesized from sensory experience and social conditioning. It is not the true self (*Ātman*), but an illusory worldly identity that retroactively takes credit for all actions include ones executed by subconscious and divine mechanisms.

**3). Karaṇa --- The Subconscious Engine, Body Consciousness & Sensory Organs**

- **Classical Definition:** The instruments---traditionally classified as the internal and external sensory, mental, and physical faculties.

- **Modern psychology:** **Subconscious Processing & Body Consciousness.**

  This encompasses muscle memory, procedural memory, implicit reflexes, and automatic motor programs stored in the somatic network and subconscious mind (individual and collective). Just as a high-diver or driver relies on automatic subconscious pathways because conscious reflection is too slow, *Karaṇa* acts as the rapid, automated instrument through which deep conditioning translates instantly into complex physical execution.

**4). Ceṣṭā --- Vital Life Force, Higher Energy Channels**

- **Classical & Canonical Definition:** **The Vital Life Force (*Prāṇa* / *Viriya*):** Beyond basic physiological drives, *Ceṣṭāḥ* encompasses the cultivation and deployment of vital life force. In canonical texts, *viriya* (energy/vigor) is one of the **5 Powers (*Five Bala*)** and a fundamental **Basis for Spiritual/Psychic Power (*Iddhipāda*)**. **Multi-Dimensional Vehicle:** When developed through concentration (*samādhi*) and mental discipline, this life force acts as a refined, higher-dimensional energy stream capable of transcending ordinary physical limitations and executing extraordinary acts of mind and body.

- **Modern Integration & Multi-Dimensional Scope:** **The Multi-Dimensional Energy Spectrum:** Rather than being restricted to dense bio-chemical processes (ATP consumption or muscular contraction), *Ceṣṭāḥ* functions as an integrated energy continuum. It ranges from higher-dimensional energetic impulses down into the autonomic physical system. **The Physical Substrate:** At its lowest, dense level of expression in the worldly realm (*loka*), it manifests as breath (*prāṇa*), cardiovascular movement, neurological impulses, and kinetic mechanical effort required to propel the biological body.

**5). Daiva --- Unseen Deva Agency & Celestial Influence**

- **Classical Definition:** That which originates from the **Devas** (celestial, higher dimensional entities).

- **Modern Integration:** Unseen supernatural agency and divine forces operating beyond human control.

  Rather than abstract, impersonal \"luck\" or \"karma,\" *Daiva* denotes direct or subtle celestial influence---ranging from divine inspiration and subtle mental drives nudging the collective or individual subconscious, to direct possession or overshadowing (*āveśa* / *avāsa*) impelling the human apparatus toward a specific outcome.

**The Dynamic Architecture: Unmanifested vs. Manifested**

**The Unmanifested (*Avyakta* / Pure Witness):** The unmanifested part of the Ātman is completely non-active (*akartā*), luminous, and unconditioned. It does not act, desire, or suffer. It simply is.

**The Manifested Sign (*Lakkhaṇa* / *Lakṣaṇa*):** The manifested self-identities---composed of the five factors of action (*pañca kāraṇāni*)---act as the **marks, or signs (*lakkhaṇa*)** of that underlying unmanifested reality projected into the field of world (*prakṛti* / *loka*).

**Why is the Soul-Spirit \"Trapped\" and \"Defiled\"?**

The entrapment is not because the unmanifested Ātman itself physically deteriorates, but because the **manifested projection is never completely severed from the unmanifested source**.

1.  **The Karmic Bridge / Reflection (*Cicchāyā*):** Just as moonlight is a reflection of the sun on moving water, the empirical ego (*kartā* / *ahaṅkāra*) is an animated reflection of the unmanifested light onto the five mechanical factors (*pañca kāraṇāni*).

2.  **The Illusion of Agency (*Kartātva*):** Because the unmanifested spirit continuously illuminates the empirical ego (*kartā*) and the internal instruments (*karaṇa*), the ego falsely assumes: *\"I am the doer; this consciousness is mine.\"*

3.  **The Transmission of Defilement (*Kilesa* / *Kleśa*):** When the empirical ego (*kartā*) reacts with *rāga* (worldly craving and attachment), *dosa* (fear, anger, aversion and hate), or *moha* (ignorance and delusion of not knowing the path to be liberated from the world) during an action: The physical body (*adhiṣṭhāna*) and vital forces (*ceṣṭā*) execute the act. The impression (*saṁskāra*) lodges into the subconscious mind and body memory (*karaṇa*). Because the karmic link between the manifested sign (*lakkhaṇa*) and the unmanifested source remains open, these defilements feed back along the link, obscuring the unmanifested light and keeping the continuum tethered to *saṃsāra*.

**Integrating the 5 Causes of Action (*Pañca Kāraṇāni*)**

In Bhagavad Gītā 18.14, five components assemble to execute every worldly act:

  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **Cause of Action**                     **Metaphysical Locus**   **Role in Entrapment**
  --------------------------------------- ------------------------ -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  **1. Adhiṣṭhāna** (Physical Body)       Outer *Loka*             The dense physical container where consequences manifest (*karmakṣetra*).

  **2. Kartā** (Empirical Ego)            Interface (*Ahaṅkāra*)   The psychological node that falsely claims *\"I am doing this,\"* creating fresh *karma*.

  **3. Karaṇa** (Mind & Senses)           Subconscious / Memory    The sensory apparatus and stored memory banks that filter and warp perception.

  **4. Ceṣṭā** (Vital Energies)           Subtle Body (*Prāṇa*)    The dynamic breath and physical exertion that drive bodily movement.

  **5. Daiva** (Unseen / Subtle Forces)   Cosmic Order             The external, subtle, or divine determinants that govern fruit and circumstance.
  ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

When action occurs through these five factors, the pure Ātman is merely the illuminator---yet the empirical ego (*kartā*) appropriates the whole process as *\"myself.\"*

**Mechanics of Liberation: Turning Away from the Manifested Sign**

Because entrapment relies on the active link between the unmanifested spirit and the manifested *lakkhaṇa*, liberation requires a total reversal of attention (**turning the main consciousness inward**): **Disenchantment from the world (*Nibbida*); Severing the Karmic Bridge:** By relinquishing the sense of agency (*kartātva*), the empirical ego (*kartā*) collapses. The subconscious mind (*karaṇa*) stops producing new *karmic* seeds; **Retraction into the Unmanifest (*Nivṛtti*):** As the main consciousness turns away from the outer signs (*lakkhaṇa*), it recedes along the projection path back into its unmanifested, unconditioned origin; **Liberation from the World:** With the link to the manifested *loka* fully dissolved, the unmanifested part is no longer drawn back into involuntary reincarnation. It stands entirely liberated, autonomous, and is ready to transcendent beyond the worldly jurisdiction to exit this world and universe to enter the real deathless realm of Nibbana.

**[15](javascript:void(0))   śarīra-vāṇ-manobhir yat, karma prārabhate naraḥ;**

**       nyāyyaṁ vā viparītaṁ vā, pañcaite tasya hetavaḥ.**

Whatever (**yat**) action (**karma**) a human being (**naraḥ**) undertakes (**prārabhate**) through body, speech, or mind (**śarīra-vāṅ-manobhiḥ**), whether righteous/just (**nyāyyaṁ vā**) or unrighteous/unjust (**viparītaṁ vā**), these (**ete**) five (**pañca**) are its (**tasya**) causes (**hetavaḥ**).

**[16](javascript:void(0))   tatraivaṁ sati kartāram, ātmānaṁ kevalaṁ tu yaḥ;**

**       paśyaty akṛta-buddhitvān, na sa paśyati durmatiḥ.**

Such being the case (**tatra evaṁ sati**), he who (**yaḥ tu**) views (**paśyati**) the pure, unattached **ātmā** (**kevalaṁ ātmānam**) as the sole doer (**kartāram**) owing to an uncultivated, untrained awakening/**buddhi** state (**akṛta-buddhitvāt**), that foolish person (**saḥ durmatiḥ**) does not see/ perceives correctly (**na paśyati**).

**[17](javascript:void(0))   yasya nāhaṇkṛto bhāvo, buddhir yasya na lipyate;**

**       hatvāpi sa imāṇl lokān, na hanti na nibadhyate.**

He whose (**yasya**) existence/ manifest (**bhāvaḥ**) is free from egoism/I-making (**na ahaṅkṛtaḥ**), whose (**yasya**) awakening intellect (**buddhiḥ**) is not (**na**) stained/entangled (**lipyate**); even (**api**) having slain/ killed / destroyed (**hatvā**) these (**imān**) living beings in the world (**lokān**), he (**saḥ**) does not (**na**) kills/ destroys (**hanti**), nor (**na**) is he bound / entangled by karma (**nibadhyate**).

**[18](javascript:void(0))   jñānaṁ jñeyaṁ parijñātā, tri-vidhā karma-codanā;**

**       karaṇaṁ karma karteti, tri-vidhaḥ karma-saṇgrahaḥ.**

Knowledge (*jñānam*), the object of knowledge (*jñeyam*), and the knower (*parijñātā*) constitute the threefold impulse to action (*tri-vidhā karma-codanā*); the instrument (*karaṇam*), the action (*karma*), and the agent (*kartā*)---thus (*iti*) is the threefold constituents of action (*tri-vidhaḥ karma-saṁgrahaḥ*).

**[19](javascript:void(0))   jñānaṁ karma ca kartā ca, tridhaiva guṇa-bhedataḥ;**

**       procyate guṇa-saṇkhyāne, yathāvac chṛṇu tāny api.**

Knowledge (*jñānam*), action (*karma*), and the agent (*kartā*) are spoken of (*procyate*) as three types (*tridhā eva*) according to the distinctions of the primordial *guṇa* (*guṇa-bhedataḥ*) in the analysis of the *guṇa* (*guṇa-saṅkhyāne*); listen (*śṛṇu*) to these (*tāni*) also (*api*) as they truly are (*yathāvat*).

**[20](javascript:void(0))   sarva-bhūteṣu yenaikaṁ, bhāvam avyayam īkṣate;**

**       avibhaktaṁ vibhakteṣu, taj jñānaṁ viddhi sāttvikam.**

By which (*yena*) one sees (*īkṣate*) the one (*ekam*) physically imperishable (*avyayam*) existence (*bhāvam*) in all existing beings (*sarva-bhūteṣu*), undivided (*avibhaktam*) among the divided (*vibhakteṣu*)---know (*viddhi*) that (*tat*) knowledge (*jñānam*) to be *sāttvika* (*sāttvikam*).

**[21](javascript:void(0))   pṛthaktvena tu taj jñānaṁ, nānā-bhāvān pṛthag-vidhān;**

**       vetti sarveṣu bhūteṣu, taj jñānaṁ viddhi rājasam.**

But (*tu*) know (*viddhi*) that (*tat*) knowledge (*jñānam*) to be *rājasa* (*rājasam*) by which (*yena*, implied) one perceives (*vetti*) in all beings (*sarveṣu bhūteṣu*) various distinct existences (*nānā-bhāvān pṛthag-vidhān*) as fundamentally separate (*pṛthaktvena*).

**[22](javascript:void(0))   yat tu kṛtsna-vad ekasmin, kārye saktam ahaitukam;**

**       atattvārtha-vad alpaṁ ca, tat tāmasam udāhṛtam.**

But (*tu*) that (*tat*) knowledge which (*yat*) is attached (*saktam*) to a single body (*ekasmin kārye*) as if it were the whole (*kṛtsna-vat*), lacking rational cause (*ahaitukam*), devoid of real meaning (*atattva-artha-vat*), and trivial (*alpam ca*)---that (*tat*) is declared (*udāhṛtam*) to be *tāmasa* (*tāmasam*).

**[23](javascript:void(0))   niyataṁ saṇga-rahitam, arāga-dveṣataḥ kṛtam;**

**       aphala-prepsunā karma, yat tat sāttvikam ucyate.**

Action (*karma*) which (*yat*) is obligatory (*niyatam*), free from attachment (*saṅga-rahitam*), performed without craving or aversion (*arāga-dveṣataḥ kṛtam*), and executed by one who does not desire the fruits (*aphala-prepsunā*)---that (*tat*) is called *sāttvika* (*sāttvikam ucyate*).

**[24](javascript:void(0))   yat tu kāmepsunā karma, sāhaṇkāreṇa vā punaḥ;**

**       kriyate bahulāyāsaṁ, tad rājasam udāhṛtam.**

But (*tu*) that action (*karma*) which (*yat*) is performed (*kriyate*) by one seeking rewards (*kāmepsunā*), or again (*vā punaḥ*) with egoism / self-conceit (*sāhaṅkāreṇa*), involving much strenuous effort (*bahula-āyāsam*)---that (*tat*) is declared (*udāhṛtam*) to be *rājasa* (*rājasam*).

**[25](javascript:void(0))   anubandhaṁ kṣayaṁ hiṁsām, anapekṣya ca pauruṣam;**

**       mohād ārabhyate karma, yat tat tāmasam ucyate.**

Action (*karma*) which (*yat*) is undertaken (*ārabhyate*) out of delusion (*mohāt*), without regarding (*anapekṣya*) the consequences (*anubandham*), destruction (*kṣayam*), injury to others (*hiṁsām*), and one\'s own capability (*pauruṣam ca*)---that (*tat*) is called *tāmasa* (*tāmasam ucyate*).

**[26](javascript:void(0))   mukta-saṇgo 'nahaṁ-vādī, dhṛty-utsāha-samanvitaḥ;**

**       siddhy-asiddhyor nirvikāraḥ, kartā sāttvika ucyate.**

The actor, doer, or agent (*kartā*) who is free from attachment (*mukta-saṅgaḥ*), devoid of egoistic talk or self-assertive chat (*anaham-vādī*), accompanied by steadfast fortitude and dynamic enthusiasm (*dhṛti-utsāha-samanvitaḥ*), unperturbed (*nirvikāraḥ*) in both success and failure (*siddhi-asiddhyoḥ*), is declared (*ucyate*) *sāttvika*.

**[27](javascript:void(0))   rāgī karma-phala-prepsur, lubdho hiṁsātmako 'śuciḥ;**

**       harṣa-śokānvitaḥ kartā, rājasaḥ parikīrtitaḥ.**

The actor, doer, or agent (*kartā*) who is full of passion and craving (*rāgī*), desirous of obtaining (*prepsuḥ*) the fruit of action (*karma-phala*), greedy (*lubdhaḥ*), of harmful nature (*hiṃsātmakaḥ*), impure (*aśuciḥ*), and moved by and subject to joy and grief (*harṣa-śokānvitaḥ*), is declared (*parikīrtitaḥ*) *rājasa*.

**[28](javascript:void(0))   ayuktaḥ prākṛtaḥ stabdhaḥ, śaṭho naikṛtiko 'lasaḥ;**

**       viṣādī dīrgha-sūtrī ca, kartā tāmasa ucyate.**

The actor, doer, or agent (*kartā*) who is undisciplined (*ayuktaḥ*), vulgar, and gross (*prākṛtaḥ*), stubborn (*stabdhaḥ*), deceitful and dishonest (*śaṭhaḥ*), malicious and spiteful (*naikṛtikaḥ*), indolent and lazy (*alasaḥ*), despondent and chronically sorrowful (*viṣādī*), and procrastinating (*dīrgha-sūtrī*) is declared (*ucyate*) *tāmasa*.

**[29](javascript:void(0))   buddher bhedaṁ dhṛteś caiva, guṇatas tri-vidhaṁ śṛṇu;**

**       procyamānam aśeṣeṇa, pṛthaktvena dhanañjaya.**

Hear (*śṛṇu*), O conqueror of wealth (*dhanañjaya*), the threefold division (*tri-vidhaṃ bhedam*) of the awakening *buddhi* as well as (*ca eva*) of sustained determination (*dhṛteḥ*), according to the *guṇa*, as it is being fully declared (*procyamānam*) completely (*aśeṣeṇa*), and in detail (*pṛthaktvena*).

**[30](javascript:void(0))   pravṛttiṁ ca nivṛttiṁ ca, kāryākārye bhayābhaye;**

**       bandhaṁ mokṣaṁ ca yā vetti, buddhiḥ sā pārtha sāttvikī.**

That awakening *buddhi* which knows (*yā vetti*) the path of outward activity (*pravṛttim ca*) and the path of disengagement from action (*nivṛttim ca*), what ought to be done and what ought not to be done (*kāryākārye*), fear and fearlessness (*bhayābhaye*), and bondage (*bandham*) and liberation (*mokṣam ca*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *sāttvika*.

**[31](javascript:void(0))   yayā dharmam adharmaṁ ca, kāryaṁ cākāryam eva ca;**

**       ayathāvat prajānāti, buddhiḥ sā pārtha rājasī.**

That awakening *buddhi* by which (*yayā*) one incorrectly understands (*ayathāvat prajānāti*) the *dharma*, and *non*-*dharmaṃ*, as well as (*ca eva ca*) what ought to be done (*kāryam*) and what ought not to be done (*akāryam*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *rājasa*.

**[32](javascript:void(0))   adharmaṁ dharmam iti yā, manyate tamasāvṛtā;**

**       sarvārthān viparītāṁś ca, buddhiḥ sā pārtha tāmasī.**

That awakening *buddhi* which, shrouded in delusion (*tamasā āvṛtā*), considers (*manyate*) *non-dharmam*) as (*iti*) *dharma*, and perverts all matters, truths, and values (*sarvān arthān*) into their complete opposites (*viparītān ca*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *tāmasa*.

**[33](javascript:void(0))   dhṛtyā yayā dhārayate, manaḥ-prāṇendriya-kriyāḥ;**

**       yogenāvyabhicāriṇyā, dhṛtiḥ sā pārtha sāttvikī.**

That sustained fortitude (*dhṛtiḥ sā*) by which (*yayā*) one sustains (*dhārayate*) the activities (*kriyāḥ*) of the mind (*manas*), life-force energy (*prāṇa*), and sensory organs (*indriya*), through unwavering discipline (*yomena avyabhicāriṇyā*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *sāttvika*.

**[34](javascript:void(0))   yayā tu dharma-kāmārthān, dhṛtyā dhārayate 'rjuna;**

**       prasaṇgena phalākāṇkṣī, dhṛtiḥ sā pārtha rājasī.**

But (*tu*), O Arjuna (*arjuna*), that fortitude (*dhṛtiḥ sā*) by which (*yayā dhṛtyā*) a person maintains (*dhārayate*) *dharma*, sexual/ sensory pleasure (*kāma*), and material wealth (*artha*), while desiring the fruits of actions (*phalākāṅkṣī*) through intense attachment (*prasaṅgena*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *rājasa*.

**[35](javascript:void(0))   yayā svapnaṁ bhayaṁ śokaṁ, viṣādaṁ madam eva ca;**

**       na vimuñcati durmedhā, dhṛtiḥ sā pārtha tāmasī.**

That fortitude (*dhṛtiḥ sā*) by which (*yayā*) an foolish person (*durmedhā*) does not relinquish (*na vimuñcati*) sleep and lethargy (*svapnam*), fear (*bhayam*), grief (*śokam*), depression (*viṣādam*), and arrogance or intoxication (*madam eva ca*), O son of Pṛthā (*pārtha*), belongs to *tāmasa*.

**[36](javascript:void(0))   sukhaṁ tv idānīṁ tri-vidhaṁ, śṛṇu me bharatarṣabha;**

**       abhyāsād ramate yatra, duḥkhāntaṁ ca nigacchati.**

Now (*tu idānīm*), O bull among the Bharatas (*bharatarṣabha*), hear from me (*śṛṇu me*) about the three kinds (*tri-vidham*) of happiness (*sukham*): that in which (*yatra*) one finds delight (*ramate*) through sustained cultivation (*abhyāsāt*), and in which one attains (*ca nigacchati*) the end of dukkha (*duḥkhāntam*).

**[37](javascript:void(0))   yat tad agre viṣam iva, pariṇāme 'mṛtopamam;**

**       tat sukhaṁ sāttvikaṁ proktam, ātma-buddhi-prasāda-jam.**

That happiness (*tat sukham*) which (*yat*) feels like poison at first (*agre viṣam iva*), but in its ultimate transformation (*pariṇāme*) is like immortal nectar (*amṛta-upamam*), is declared to be (*proktam*) *sāttvika*; it is born from (*jam*) the tranquility (*prasāda*), awakening *buddhi*, of *ātma*.

**Commentary**: **Sāttvika Sukha** (Pure And Wholesome Happiness)

**1). The Paradox: \"Poison at First\" (*Agre Viṣam Iva*)**

In the initial stages of spiritual discipline, cultivating pure happiness feels demanding, restrictive, and even painful---resembling \"poison\" (*viṣam iva*). This initial discomfort occurs because the untamed mind-heart (*citta*) and sensory organs (*indriyas*) are accustomed to pursuing immediate worldly gratification. When one applies discipline (*sīla*), concentration (*samādhi*), and restraint over sensory pursuits:

- The habitual cravings (*rāga*) resist being restrained.

- The egoic self feels friction when forced to relinquish sensual attachments and mental inertia (*tamas*).

- The practice of early morning meditation, continuous mindfulness, and unswerving fortitude (*dhṛti*) requires rigorous internal friction against old patterns.

**2). The Outcome: \"Nectar in its Culmination\" (*Pariṇāme \'Mṛtopamam*)**

Though the initial training feels difficult, its ultimate transformation (*pariṇāma*) yields a deep, unshakable peace that resembles *amṛta* (immortal nectar). As the mental pollutants (*rāga*, *dosa*, *moha*) dissolve through disciplined practice:

- The agitation of constantly seeking external objects subsides.

- The practitioner realizes a form of happiness that does not depend on changing external circumstances in the world (*loka*).

- The mind attains a state of enduring freedom, unburdened by temporal anxiety or fear.

**3). The Source: *Ātma-Buddhi-Prasāda-Jam***

Unlike worldly pleasures that rely on sensory contact with external objects, *sāttvika sukha* is generated internally. It arises from *prasāda*---the complete settling, clearing, and tranquility of the awakening intellect *buddhi* directed inward toward the pure soul-spirit *ātma*.

When the intellect is no longer clouded by projections or desires, its natural state is light, serene, and clear. This tranquil clarity (*prasāda*) produces an unalloyed joy that is self-sustaining, leading directly toward complete liberation and the total cessation of *dukkha*.

**[38](javascript:void(0))   viṣayendriya-saṁyogād, yat tad agre 'mṛtopamam;**

**       pariṇāme viṣam iva, tat sukhaṁ rājasaṁ smṛtam.**

That happiness (*tat sukham*) which (*yat*) arises from the contact of the sensory organs with external objects (*viṣayendriya-saṁyogāt*), appearing like immortal nectar at first (*agre amṛta-upamam*), but in its ultimate transformation (*pariṇāme*) becomes like poison (*viṣam iva*), is declared to be (*smṛtam*) *rājasa*.

**[39](javascript:void(0))   yad agre cānubandhe ca, sukhaṁ mohanam ātmanaḥ;**

**       nidrālasya-pramādotthaṁ, tat tāmasam udāhṛtam.**

That happiness (*tat sukham*) which (*yat*) both in its initial stage (*agre*) and in its long-term consequence (*anubandhe ca*) is deluding and stupefying to the (*mohanam*) *ātma*, arising from sleep and torpor (*nidrā*), laziness and inertia (*ālasya*), and heedlessness or negligence (*pramāda-uttham*), is declared to be (*udāhṛtam*) *tāmasa*.

**[40](javascript:void(0))  na tad asti pṛthivyāṁ vā, divi deveṣu vā punaḥ;**

**      sattvaṁ prakṛti-jair muktaṁ, yad ebhiḥ syāt tribhir guṇaiḥ.**

Not (na) that (tat) is/exists (asti) on earth / terrestrial existence (pṛthivyām) or (vā) in heaven / celestial existence (divi) among the *devas* or again (punaḥ), could (syāt) *sattva* born of (jaiḥ) making-forth / *prakṛti* be liberated (muktam) from these (ebhiḥ) three (tribhiḥ) *guṇas*.

**Commentary**: **Universal Scope of the Material Sphere (*Samsāric* Inclusivity)**

This verse marks the definitive conclusion of the three modes of nature, or *guṇas,* detailed throughout Chapter 18 (covering knowledge, action, agent, awakening intellect / *buddhi*, resolve, and happiness).

By specifically identifying both terrestrial existence---on earth (pṛthivyām)---and celestial existence---in heaven (divi) among the gods (deveṣu)---the verse establishes that all manifest planes within cosmic existence are entirely conditioned by primordial material nature / manifestation (*prakṛti*). Even *devas* in heavenly realms are fundamentally composed of and bound by varying configurations of the three *guṇas*: **Sattva** (purity, light, harmony), **Rajas** (passion, activity, motion) and **Tamas** (inertia, darkness, ignorance)

**The *Guṇa* Framework and Liberation**

The statement *sattvaṁ prakṛti-jair muktaṁ yad ebhiḥ syāt tribhir guṇaiḥ* asserts an absolute rule of cosmology and metaphysics: no *sattva* within the cosmic order is inherently free from these three strands.

**The Higher Realms:** Heavenly rebirths among the *devas* are predominantly governed by a high concentration of *sattva-guṇa*, but because *sattva* remains one of the three *prakṛti-jaiḥ* (born of *prakṛti*), it still constitutes a subtle form of bondage.

**Transcendence:** Absolute freedom or liberation (*mukti*) cannot be attained merely by ascending to higher planes of existence within the universe, as those realms still operate under the universal laws of decay and dissolution and the perpetual cycling of the modes. Transcendence requires rising entirely beyond all three modes (*triguṇātīta*), leaving behind all worldly phenomena governed by *prakṛti*.

**[41](javascript:void(0))   brāhmaṇa-kṣtratriya-viśāṁ, śūdrāṇāṁ ca parantapa;**

**       karmāṇi pravibhaktāni, svabhāva-prabhavair guṇaiḥ.**

O scorcher of enemies (parantapa), the duties / actions (karmāṇi) of the *brāhmaṇa*, the warriors (kṣatriya), and the merchants/agriculturalists (viśām), as well as (ca) of the servants/labourers (śūdrāṇām), are differentiated (pravibhaktāni) according to the *guṇas* arising from inherent nature / self existence (svabhāva-prabhavaiḥ).

**[42](javascript:void(0))   śamo damas tapaḥ śaucaṁ, kṣāntir ārjavam eva ca;**

**       jñānaṁ vijñānam āstikyaṁ, brahma-karma svabhāva-jam.**

Mastery/calming of the mind (śamaḥ), sensory restraint / self-restraint (damaḥ), primordial spiritual force / *tapas*, purity / cleanliness (śaucam), forbearance / patience (kṣāntiḥ), uprightness (ārjavam), as well as (eva ca) knowledge (jñānam), experiential discernment (vijñānam), and faith/affirmation in liberation for the soul-spirit from the world entrapment (āstikyam)---these are (iti) the duties (karma) for Brahma \[source of cosmic mankind\], born of inherent nature (svabhāva-jam).

**[43](javascript:void(0))   śauryaṁ tejo dhṛtir dākṣyaṁ, yuddhe cāpy apalāyanam;**

**       dānam īśvara-bhāvaś ca, kṣātra-karma svabhāva-jam.**

Bravery/ valor (śauryam), radiance/ vigor (tejaḥ), resolve / fortitude (dhṛtiḥ), efficiency / resourcefulness (dākṣyam), as well as (ca api) not running away (apalāyanam) in battle (yuddhe), generosity / charity (dānam), and (ca) lord becoming (īśvara-bhāvaḥ)---these are (iti) the duties of a warrior (kṣātra-karma), born of inherent nature (svabhāva-jam).

**[44](javascript:void(0))   kṛṣi-go-rakṣya-vāṇijyaṁ, vaiśya-karma svabhāva-jam;**

**       paricaryātmakaṁ karma, śūdrasyāpi svabhāva-jam.**

Agriculture / farming (kṛṣi), cattle protection / animal husbandry (go-rakṣyam), and trade / commerce (vāṇijyam) are the duties of a merchant or producer (vaiśya-karma), born of inherent nature (svabhāva-jam); action (karma) consisting of service / supportive work (paricaryātmakam) is also (api) the duty of a servant or laborer (śūdrasya), born of inherent nature (svabhāva-jam).

**[45](javascript:void(0))   sve sve karmaṇy abhirataḥ, saṁsiddhiṁ labhate ṇaraḥ;**

**       sva-karma-nirataḥ siddhiṁ, yathā vindati tac chṛṇu.**

Taking delight in (abhirataḥ) his own self (sve sve) action / duty (karmaṇi), a human being (naraḥ) attains (labhate) full perfection (saṁsiddhim); hear (śṛṇu) that (tat) how (yathā) one fully engaged in one\'s own duty (sva-karma-nirataḥ) attains (vindati) perfection (siddhim).

**[46](javascript:void(0))   yataḥ pravṛttir bhūtānāṁ, yena sarvam idaṁ tatam;**

**       sva-karmaṇā tam abhyarcya, siddhiṁ vindati mānavaḥ.**

From whom (yataḥ) manifestation (pravṛttiḥ) of all beings (bhūtānām), by whom (yena) all (sarvam) this \[universe\] (idam) is pervaded (tatam)---by intense worshipping / honoring (abhyarcya) Him (tam) through one\'s own duty (sva-karmaṇā), a human being (mānavaḥ) attains (vindati) perfection (siddhim).

**[47](javascript:void(0))   śreyān sva-dharmo viguṇaḥ, para-dharmāt sv-anuṣṭhitāt;**

**       svabhāva-niyataṁ karma, kurvan nāpnoti kilbiṣam.**

Better (śreyān) is one\'s own duty (sva-dharmaḥ), even though imperfectly performed (viguṇaḥ), than the duty of another (para-dharmāt) well performed (sv-anuṣṭhitāt); performing (kurvan) action (karma) bound to one\'s own becoming/ inherent nature (svabhāva-niyatam), one does not (na) incur (āpnoti) fault (kilbiṣam).

**[48](javascript:void(0))   saha-jaṁ karma kaunteya, sa-doṣam api na tyajet;**

**       sarvārambhā hi doṣeṇa, dhūmenāgnir ivāvṛtāḥ.**

O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), one should not (na) relinquish (tyajet) the action / duty (karma) one is born with (saha-jam), even if (api) accompanied by *dosa* / fear, anger, aversion, hatred (sa-doṣam); for (hi) all undertakings / endeavors (sarvārambhāḥ) are covered (āvṛtāḥ) by *dosa* / fear, anger, aversion, hatred (doṣeṇa), just as (iva) fire (agniḥ) \[is covered\] by smoke (dhūmena).

**[49](javascript:void(0))   asakta-buddhiḥ sarvatra, jitātmā vigata-spṛhaḥ;**

**       naiṣkarmya-siddhiṁ paramāṁ, saṁnyāsenādhigacchati.**

One whose awakening intellect / *buddhi* is unattached (asakta) everywhere (sarvatra), whose *ātmā* is mastered (jitā), and from whom craving (spṛhaḥ) has departed (vigata)---such a one attains (adhigacchati) through renunciation (saṁnyāsena) the supreme (paramām) perfection of freedom from the binding reaction of action (naiṣkarmya-siddhim).

**[50](javascript:void(0))   siddhiṁ prāpto yathā brahma, tathāpnoti nibodha me;**

**       samāsenaiva kaunteya, niṣṭhā jñānasya yā parā.**

Having attained (prāptaḥ) perfection (siddhim) as (yathā) Brahma, one also (tathā) attains (āpnoti) fully awakening (nibodha) of Me (me), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya)---that which (yā) is the supreme (parā) firm standing (niṣṭhā) of knowledge (jñānasya), in summary indeed (samāsena eva).

**[51](javascript:void(0))   buddhyā viśuddhayā yukto, dhṛtyātmānaṁ niyamya ca;**

Yoked (yuktaḥ) with a purified (viśuddhayā) awakening /*buddhi*, restraining (niyamya) the *ātmā* with firm determination/ willpower (dhṛtyā), relinquishing (tyaktvā) sense objects (viṣayān) such as sound and others (śabdādīn), and completely casting aside (vyudasya ca) attachment / craving (rāga) and aversion / hatred (dveṣau)---

**[52](javascript:void(0))   vivikta-sevī laghv-āśī, yata-vāk-kāya-mānasaḥ;**

**       dhyāna-yoga-paro nityaṁ, vairāgyaṁ samupāśritaḥ.**

Resorting to / dwelling in (sevī) secluded places (vivikta), eating lightly / consuming moderate food (laghu-āśī), with speech, body, and mind (vāk-kāya-mānasaḥ) controlled / subdued (yata), constantly (nityam) in the supreme (paraḥ) yoga/union with dhyāna / Jhāna meditation (dhyāna-yoga), and fully high-grounded upon (samupāśritaḥ) disenchantment / dispassion / detachment (vairāgyam)---

**[53](javascript:void(0))   ahaṇkāraṁ balaṁ darpaṁ, kāmaṁ krodhaṁ parigraham;**

**       vimucya nirmamaḥ śānto, brahma-bhūyāya kalpate.**

Having completely released (vimucya) egoism / false self-conception (ahaṅkāram), power (balam), arrogance / pride (darpam), sexual and sensual desire / lust (kāmam), anger / wrath (krodham), and possessiveness / accumulation of external property (parigraham)---free from sense of ownership (nirmamaḥ) and peaceful / pacified (śāntaḥ)---one becomes qualified (kalpate) for becoming (bhūyāya) Brahma.

**[54](javascript:void(0))   brahma-bhūtaḥ prasannātmā, na śocati na kāṇkṣati;**

**       samaḥ sarveṣu bhūteṣu, mad-bhaktiṁ labhate parām.**

Having become Brahma (brahma-bhūtaḥ), fully settle down in (prasann) *ātmā*, one neither grieves / laments (na śocati) nor desires / longs (na kāṅkṣati); being equanimity / impartial (samaḥ) toward all beings (sarveṣu bhūteṣu), one obtains (labhate) supreme (parām) devotion / surrender to Me (mat-bhaktim).

**[55](javascript:void(0))   bhaktyā mām abhijānāti, yāvān yaś cāsmi tattvataḥ;**

**       tato māṁ tattvato jñātvā, viśate tad-anantaram.**

Through devotion (bhaktyā), one fully know (abhijānāti) Me (mām)---how great (yāvān) and who (yaḥ ca) I am (asmi) in thatness/ nature (tattvataḥ); then (tataḥ), having known (jñātvā) Me (mām) in thatness/ nature (tattvataḥ), one enters into (viśate) Me immediately thereafter (tat-anantaram).

**[56](javascript:void(0))   sarva-karmāṇy api sadā, kurvāṇo mad-vyapāśrayaḥ;**

**       mat-prasādād avāpnoti, śāśvataṁ padam avyayam.**

Performing (kurvāṇaḥ) even (api) all (sarva) actions (karmāṇi) continuously and always (sadā), leaning entirely upon Me (mad-vyapāśrayaḥ), by My (mat) grace and divine favor (prasādāt) \[he\] attains (avāpnoti) the eternal (śāśvatam), physically imperishable (avyayam) abode (padam).

**[57](javascript:void(0))   cetasā sarva-karmāṇi, mayi saṁnyasya mat-paraḥ;**

**       buddhi-yogam upāśritya, mac-cittaḥ satataṁ bhava.**

Mentally (cetasā) having completely renounced (saṁnyasya) all (sarva) actions (karmāṇi) unto Me (mayi), holding Me as the supreme goal (mat-paraḥ), leaning upon (upāśritya) the yoga of awakening intellect / buddhi (buddhi-yogam), become (bhava) perpetually (satatam) one whose mindheart *citta* is fixed on Me (mac-cittaḥ).

**[58](javascript:void(0))   mac-cittaḥ sarva-durgāṇi, mat-prasādāt tariṣyasi;**

Having your mind-heart *citta* fixed on Me (mac-cittaḥ), by My (mat) grace and divine favor (prasādāt) you shall cross over (tariṣyasi) all (sarva) obstacles and difficulties (durgāṇi); but (atha) if (cet) you (tvam) out of egoism (ahaṅkārāt) do not (na) listen or pay heed (śroṣyasi), you will perish (vinaṅkṣasi).

**[59](javascript:void(0))   yad ahaṇkāram āśritya, na yotsya iti manyase;**

**       mithyaiṣa vyavasāyas te, prakṛtis tvāṁ niyokṣyati.**

If (yat), leaning upon (āśritya) egoism (ahaṅkāram), you think (manyase) \"I will not (na) fight (yotsye)\"; false (mithyā) is this (eṣaḥ) resolve (vyavasāyaḥ) of yours (te), \[for your\] making forth /prakṛti will compel (niyokṣyati) you (tvām).

**[60](javascript:void(0))   svabhāva-jena kaunteya, nibaddhaḥ svena karmaṇā;**

**       kartuṁ necchasi yan mohāt, kariṣyasy avaśo 'pi tat.**

Bound (nibaddhaḥ) by your own (svena) born inherent nature/ own existence (svabhāva-jena) action (karmaṇā), O son of Kuntī (kaunteya), that which (yat) out of delusion (mohāt) you do not (na) wish (icchasi) to do (kartum), even (api) against your will (avaśaḥ) you will do (kariṣyasi) that very thing (tat).

**[61](javascript:void(0))   īśvaraḥ sarva-bhūtānāṁ, hṛd-deśe 'rjuna tiṣṭhati;**

The Lord (īśvaraḥ) resides (tiṣṭhati) in the heart-region (hṛd-deśe) of all (sarva) living beings (bhūtānām), O Arjuna (arjuna); causing all (sarva) beings (bhūtāni) to wander through existence (bhrāmayan), mounted as if on a machine (yantrārūḍhāni), through illusion /māya.

**[62](javascript:void(0))   tam eva śaraṇaṁ gaccha, sarva-bhāvena bhārata;**

Go (gaccha) unto Him (tam) alone (eva) for refuge (śaraṇam) with your entire being (sarva-bhāvena), O descendant of Bharata (bhārata); by His (tat) grace and divine favor (prasādāt) you shall attain (prāpsyasi) supreme (parām) peace (śāntim) and the eternal (śāśvatam) abode (sthānam).

Thus (iti), to you (te) the knowledge (jñānam) more secret (guhyataram) than any secret (guhyāt) has been revealed (ākhyātam) by Me (mayi); reflecting (vimṛṣya) this (etat) completely and without omission (aśeṣeṇa), as (yathā) you wish (icchasi), so (tathā) act (kuru).

Listen (śṛṇu) again (bhūyaḥ) to My (me) supreme (paramam) teaching (vacaḥ), the most secret of all secrets (sarva-guhyatamam); because you are (asi) dearly (dṛḍham) beloved (iṣṭaḥ) by Me (me), therefore (tataḥ) I will speak (vakṣyāmi) for your (te) welfare (hitam).

Fix your mind on Me (man-manāḥ bhava), be devoted to Me (mad-bhaktaḥ), offer worship and sacrifice to Me (mad-yājī), bow down in reverence to Me (mām namaskuru); unto Me (mām) alone (eva) you shall come (eṣyasi); truly (satyam) I promise (pratijāne) you (te), \[for\] you are (asi) dear (priyaḥ) to Me (me).

Completely renouncing (parityajya) all (sarva) *dharma* (concepts and ideas on duties / religious paths / rites), take (vraja) refuge (śaraṇam) in Me (mām) alone (ekam); I (aham) shall release (mokṣayiṣyāmi) you (tvā) from all (sarva) evils and bondage (pāpebhyaḥ); do not (mā) grieve (śucaḥ).

This (idam) should never at any time (na kadācana) be spoken (vācyam) by you (te) to anyone who is without devotion to the primordial force/ *tapas* (na atapaskāya), nor to anyone who is non-devoted (na abhaktāya), nor to anyone who has no desire to listen or serve (na ca aśuśrūṣave), nor to anyone who (yaḥ) is envious of (abhyasūyati) Me (mām).

He who (yaḥ) shall impart (abhidhāsyati) this (idam) supreme (paramam) secret (guhyam) to My devotees (mat-bhakteṣu), having performed (kṛtvā) supreme (parām) devotion (bhaktim) unto Me (mayi), shall come (eṣyati) to Me (mām) alone (eva)---there is no doubt (asaṁśayaḥ).

And (ca) he who (yaḥ) shall study (adhyeṣyate) this (imam) *dharma* of our (āvayoḥ) dialogue (saṁvādam), by him (tena) I (aham) shall be worshipped (iṣṭaḥ syām) through the sacrifice of knowledge (jñāna-yajñena)---such is (iti) My (me) conviction (matiḥ).

And (ca) even that (yo api) human being (naraḥ) who is faithful (śraddhāvān) and free from cynicism (anasūyaḥ), may hear (śṛṇuyāt), even he (saḥ api), being liberated (muktaḥ), shall attain (prāpnuyāt) the radiant realms (śubhān lokān) of those with wholesome (puṇya) *karma*.

O son of Pṛthā (pārtha)! Has this (etat) been heard (śrutam) by you (tvayā) with focused mind (ekāgreṇa cetasā)? O conqueror of wealth (dhanañjaya)! Has your (te) delusion born of ignorance (ajñāna-sammohaḥ) been completely destroyed (praṇaṣṭaḥ)?

***Arjuna uvāca***

**[73](javascript:void(0))   naṣṭo mohaḥ smṛtir labdhā, tvat-prasādān mayācyuta;**

Arjuna said: My delusion (mohaḥ) is destroyed (naṣṭaḥ), and awareness (smṛtiḥ) have been obtained (labdhā) through Your grace (tvat-prasādāt), O Infallible One (acyuta)! I stand firm (sthitaḥ asmi), free from all doubt (gata-sandehaḥ); I shall carry out (kariṣye) Your teaching (vacanam tava).

Sañjaya said: Thus (iti) I (aham) have heard (aśrauṣam) this (imam) wondrous (adbhutam) and hair-standing (roma-harṣaṇam) dialogue (saṁvādam) of Vāsudeva (vāsudevasya) and the great ātma of (mahātmanaḥ) son of Pṛthā, Arjuna (pārthasya ca).

O King (rājan)! Bringing vividly to mind again and again (saṁsmṛtya saṁsmṛtya) this (imam) wondrous (adbhutam) and wholesome (puṇyam) dialogue (saṁvādam) of Keśava and Arjuna (keśavārjunayoḥ), I rejoice with delight (hṛṣyāmi) at every single moment (ca muhur muhuḥ).

**[77](javascript:void(0))   tac ca saṁsmṛtya saṁsmṛtya, rūpam aty-adbhutaṁ hareḥ;**

And (ca) remembering repeatedly (saṁsmṛtya saṁsmṛtya) that (tat) exceedingly wondrous form (rūpam aty-adbhutam) of Hari / Kṛṣṇa (hareḥ), my (me) awe is immense (vismayaḥ mahān), O King (rājan), and I thrill with joy (hṛṣyāmi) again and again (punaḥ punaḥ).

**[78](javascript:void(0))   yatra yogeśvaraḥ kṛṣṇo, yatra pārtho dhanur-dharaḥ;**

Wherever (yatra) there is Kṛṣṇa (kṛṣṇaḥ), the Supreme Lord of Yoga (yoga-īśvaraḥ), and wherever (yatra) there is Arjuna, the wielder of the bow (pārthaḥ dhanur-dharaḥ), right there (tatra) will inevitably be auspicious splendor (śrīḥ), victory (vijayaḥ), thriving power (bhūtiḥ), and enduring moral order (dhruvā nītiḥ)---such is (iti) my (mama) conviction (matiḥ).

### About the Author

Bhante Mudita Bhikkhu Thera is a Theravāda monk, meditation teacher, and founder of Arahanism---a structured training system dedicated to liberation (Vimutti). He serves as Academic Director of the Buddhist College of Canada, Lead Instructor of the Gautama's Liberation Path program, and Head of Theravāda Buddhism for the Buddhist Association of Canada.

Ordained in 2014 at the Pa Auk Forest Monastery in Myanmar, Bhante Mudita underwent intensive training in samatha--vipassanā meditation, grounded in the Pāli Canon and strict monastic discipline. He later spent several years in the Thai forest tradition, deepening his practice in solitude, simplicity, and direct experiential insight. This dual formation---textual rigor and meditative depth---shapes his approach to teaching as both systematic and experiential.

Bhante Mudita presents the path as a precise method of training. His teaching emphasizes ethical withdrawal from the world (Sīla), stabilization and lifting of the mind into higher dimensions (Samādhi), and penetrating insight (Paññā) that uproots rāga, dosa, and moha, leading to dispassion (virāga) and liberation from the world.

Through his work, he aims to restore clarity to the original liberation path of Gautama the Supreme Arahant---guiding practitioners beyond conceptual understanding toward direct realization of the Deathless realm (Nibbāna-dhātu). Residing in a forested hermitage in Ontario, Canada, he continues to teach a disciplined, verifiable path oriented toward awakening and arahantship.
